Azumaya loci of skein algebras
Hiroaki Karuo (1), Julien Korinman (2)

Abstract
. We compute the Azumaya loci of Kauffman-bracket skein algebras of closed surfaces at odd roots of unity and provide partial results for open surfaces as well. As applications, we give an alternative definition of the projective representations of the Torelli groups derived from non-semisimple TQFTs and we strengthen a result by Frohman-Kania Bartoszynska-Lê about the dimensions of some quotients of the skein modules of closed 3-manifolds.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
57
R
56
,
57
M
25
.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background on skein algebras and their representations

Let
\Sigma_{g}
be an oriented connected closed genus
g
surface,
N\geq 3
an odd integer,
A\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
a root of unity of order either
N
or
2N
and
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
the associated Kauffman-bracket skein algebra. A representation
r:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
is a
weight representation
if
V
is semisimple as a module over the center of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
. The purpose of this paper is to make progresses towards the

Problem 1.1
.

Classify all finite dimensional weight representations of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
.

Our study is based on several recent results that we now summarize briefly and review in details in Section
2
.

Chebyshev-Frobenius morphisms:

Write
\varepsilon=A^{N}
, so
\varepsilon=+1
if
A
has order
N
and
\varepsilon=-1
if
A
has order
2N
.
Bonahon and Wong defined in
[
BW16a
]
an injective morphism of algebras

Ch_{A}:\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma_{g})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\right)

named
Chebyshev-Frobenius morphism
, from the (commutative) skein algebra at
A=\varepsilon
into the center of the skein algebra at root of unity of odd order. It is proved in
[
FKL19
]
that
Ch_{A}
is onto.
A classical result of Bullock
[
Bul97
,
PS00
,
CM09
]
combined with the result in
[
Bar99
]
shows the existence of a (non-canonical when
\varepsilon=+1
) isomorphism

\Psi:\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma_{g})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})]

between the skein algebra at
A=\varepsilon
and the ring of regular functions of the character variety defined by the GIT quotient

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g}):=\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g}),\operatorname{SL}_{2})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\operatorname{SL}_{2}.

Every indecomposable weight representation of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
induces a character over the center of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
, thus a closed point in
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
named its
classical shadow
.

Azumaya locus:
Let
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
be the class of a representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
seen as a closed point in
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
and denote by
\chi_{[\rho]}
the corresponding induced character over the center of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
. Write
\mathcal{I}_{[\rho]}\subset\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
the ideal generated by elements
\chi_{[\rho]}(z)-Ch_{A}(z)
for all
z\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and consider the finite dimensional algebra

\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\right)_{[\rho]}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{I}_{[\rho]}$}\right.}.

The
Azumaya locus
of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
is defined as the subset

\mathcal{AL}:=\left\{[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})\mbox{ such that }\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\right)_{[\rho]}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{N^{3g-3}}(\mathbb{C})\right\}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g}).

Therefore, for every
[\rho]\in\mathcal{AL}
, there exists a unique indecomposable weight representation
r_{[\rho]}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(V_{[\rho]})
with classical shadow
[\rho]
: this representation corresponds to the unique irreducible representation of
\operatorname{Mat}_{N^{3g-3}}(\mathbb{C})
, so
r_{[\rho]}
is irreducible and has dimension
N^{3g-3}
. Therefore, the representation theory of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
over its Azumaya locus is trivial.
A classical result of De Concini-Kac
[
DCK89
]
, further extended in
[
BG02
,
BY17
,
FKL19
]
, asserts that if a complex algebra is
(i)
affine,
(ii)
prime and
(iii)
of finite rank over its center, then its Azumaya locus is a Zariski open dense set. This result was applied to
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
in
[
FKL19
]
to prove that
\mathcal{AL}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
is open dense.

Poisson order:

The character variety
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
has a Poisson structure defined by Goldman in
[
Gol86
]
. When
g\geq 2
, it decomposes as

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})=\mathcal{X}^{(0)}\sqcup\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\sqcup\mathcal{X}^{(2)}

where:

•

\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
is the smooth locus which corresponds to the classes of irreducible representations
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
,

•

\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
is the smooth locus of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})\setminus\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
and corresponds to the classes of representations which are diagonal but not central,

•

\mathcal{X}^{(2)}\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
is the finite locus of central representations.

Both
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
are smooth symplectic varieties and the symplectic leaves of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
are the sets
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
and the singletons
\{[\rho]\}
for
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
.
Ganev-Jordan-Safronov noticed in
[
GJS24
]
that skein algebras admit structures of Poisson orders over
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
. The theory of Poisson orders was initiated by De Concini-Kac in
[
DCK89
]
and fully developed in
[
BG03
]
. The main result of the theory implies that whenever
[\rho_{1}]
and
[\rho_{2}]
belong to the same symplectic leaf, then

\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\right)_{[\rho_{1}]}\cong\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\right)_{[\rho_{2}]}.

In particular, if a leaf intersects non-trivially the Azumaya locus, then it is included in it. Since both the Azumaya locus and
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
are dense, they intersect non-trivially, therefore one has (this is part of
[
GJS24
, Theorem
1.1
]
):

(1)

\mathcal{X}^{(0)}\subset\mathcal{AL}.

An alternative proof of (a weaker version of) this result can also be found in
[
DS22
]
.

WRT representations:
When
[\rho_{1}],[\rho_{2}]\in\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
, we easily see that the two algebras
\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\right)_{[\rho_{1}]}
and
\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\right)_{[\rho_{2}]}
are isomorphic so either all the points of
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
belong to the Azumaya locus or none of them does.
The Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFTs in
[
Wit89
,
RT91
]
induce representations
r^{WRT}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(V(\Sigma_{g}))
which are known to be irreducible
[
GU15
]
and to have classical shadows in
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}

[
BW16b
]
. Moreover, their dimensions are given by the so-called Verlinde formula which satisfies
1<\dim(V(\Sigma_{g}))<N^{3g-3}
, so these representations are not induced by representations of
\operatorname{Mat}_{N^{3g-3}}(\mathbb{C})
. Therefore the existence of the WRT representations proves that

(2)

\mathcal{AL}\cap\mathcal{X}^{(2)}=\emptyset.

1.2.
Main results

By Equations (
1
) and (
2
), the only remaining open question regarding the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
is how it intersects the leaf
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
. The goal of the present paper it to answer this question.
By the theory of Poisson orders, we are left with the alternative:

\mbox{either one has }\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\subset\mathcal{AL}\mbox{, or }\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{AL}=\emptyset.

Theorem 1.2
.

One has
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\subset\mathcal{AL}
, therefore the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
is the locus of non-central representations.

This theorem disproves a conjecture of Lê and Yu in
[
LYb
]
who conjectured that the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
was the smooth locus of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
.

The strategy to prove Theorem
1.2
goes as follows. By a theorem of Posner, (see
[
BG02
, Theorem III.I.6]
) if
[\rho]
does not belong to the Azumaya locus, then every irreducible representations of
\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\right)_{[\rho]}
have dimension strictly smaller than
N^{3g-3}
, so we get the following alternative characterization of the Azumaya locus:

\mathcal{AL}=\{[\rho]\mbox{ s.t. }[\rho]\mbox{ is the classical shadow of an irreducible representation of (maximal) dimension }N^{3g-3}\}.

In particular if
r:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
is a central representation of dimension
N^{3g-3}
whose classical shadow is
[\rho]
then
[\rho]
belongs to the Azumaya locus if and only if
r
is irreducible.

For every
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
, there exist two central representations of dimension
N^{3g-3}
whose classical shadow is
[\rho]
:

(1)

the representations defined by Bonahon and Wong in
[
BW19
]
using quantum Teichmüller theory and

(2)

the representations defined by Blanchet-Costantino-Geer-Patureau Mirand in
[
BCGP16
]
derived from non-semisimple TQFTs.

The skein representations defined in
[
BCGP16
]
are part of a larger algebraic structure named (non-semisimple)
extended topological quantum field theory
well studied in
[
BCGP16
,
DR22
]
. This additional structure permits one to define interesting bases and non-degenerate pairings which permit one to perform explicit computations.
Theorem
1.2
will be derived from the following:

Theorem 1.3
.

The skein representations derived from non-semisimple TQFTs with classical shadows in
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
are irreducible.

Note that, by the theory of Poisson orders, either every such representation is irreducible or none of them is. We will prove that the latter alternative leads to a contradiction with the non-vanishing of certain
6j
-symbols.

Corollary 1.4
.

Any two skein representations from
[
BW19
]
and
[
BCGP16
]
respectively having the same classical shadow in
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
are isomorphic.

Therefore, the only remaining question to complete Problem
1.1
for closed surfaces is to classify all indecomposable modules of the finite dimensional algebras
\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\right)_{[\rho]}
for
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
(these algebras are pairwise isomorphic).

A first consequence of Theorem
1.2
is that it permits one to give an alternative very simple definition of the projective representations of the Torelli groups defined in
[
BCGP16
]
(see Section
6.1
). A second consequence will be stated in Corollary
1.6
below.

Let
\Sigma_{g,n}
be a genus
g
surface with
n\geq 1
boundary components. If
Z
denotes the center of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
, then
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}(\Sigma_{g,n}):=\operatorname{MaxSpec}(Z)
is a finite branched covering of the character variety
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
. Its closed points are
n+1
-tuples
([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
with
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
and
z_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
such that if
\gamma_{i}
is the peripheral curve encircling the
i
-th boundary component once, then
T_{N}(z_{i})=-\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))
(here
T_{N}
is the Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind).

The Azumaya locus
\mathcal{AL}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{X}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
is still a dense open subset and we call
fully Azumaya locus
the subset
\mathcal{FAL}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
of classes
[\rho]
such that
([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathcal{AL}
for every
z_{i}
(satisfying
T_{N}(z_{i})=-\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))
). Using Poisson orders, we see that the fully Azumaya locus is a union of symplectic leaves. Let
\nu:\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
\nu([\rho]):=(\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{1})),\ldots,\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{n})))
. The pull-backs
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathbf{z}):=\nu^{-1}(\mathbf{z})
are called
relative
or
sliced character varieties
and the smooth loci of these sliced character varieties are symplectic leaves of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
. In particular, unlike the closed case, no symplectic leaf is open dense. However, by analyzing the quotient algebras
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\gamma_{i}-z_{i})$}\right.}
, Frohman-Kania Bartoszynska-Lê proved in
[
FKL23
]
that the smooth loci of sliced character varieties are included in the fully Azumaya loci. The computation of these smooth loci is not known in general though many partial results are presented in
[
FKL23
]
.

For
\Sigma_{1,1}
and
\Sigma_{0,4}
, Takenov exhibited in
[
Tak
]
some open dense subsets which are included in the Azumaya loci of their skein algebras.
Large families of irreducible representations of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})
were defined by Havlíček-Pošta
in
[
HP01
]
(after remarking that
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})\cong U^{\prime}_{q}\mathfrak{so}_{3}
), and Takenov’s result for
\Sigma_{1,1}
is derived from this work. A classical result of Fricke asserts that
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1})\cong\mathbb{C}^{3}
through the isomorphism sending
[\rho]
to
\{\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\alpha_{i})\}_{i=1,2,3}
where
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
are described in Figure
4
.
The
quaternionic representation
is the point
[\rho_{0}]
sent to
(0,0,0)
by this isomorphism.

Fix
A^{1/2}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and let
q
denote
A^{2}
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Suppose that
\chi(\Sigma_{g,n})<0
. Let
x=([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
and write
c_{i}:=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))
.

(1)

If
(g,n)\neq(1,1)
and there exists
i
such that
z_{i}=-q-q^{-1}
then
x\notin\mathcal{AL}
. So if there exists
i
such that
c_{i}=2
, then
[\rho]\notin\mathcal{FAL}
.

(2)

Both
\mathcal{AL}
and
\mathcal{FAL}
are invariant under the natural
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
action. So if
n\geq 2
and
c_{i}=\pm 2
for some
i
, then
[\rho]\notin\mathcal{FAL}
.

(3)

If
[\rho]
is central and
z_{i}=-q^{n_{i}}-q^{-n_{i}}
with
n_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
, then
x\notin\mathcal{AL}
. So no central representation is fully Azumaya.

(4)

If
g\geq 1
and
[\rho]
is diagonal non-central and for all
1\leq i\leq n
either
c_{i}\neq\pm 2
or
z_{i}=\pm 2
, then
([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathcal{AL}
. In particular, if
[\rho]
is diagonal non-central and
c_{i}\neq\pm 2
for all
i
, then
[\rho]\in\mathcal{FAL}
.

(5)

Let
x=([\rho],z)\in\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1})
.

•

If
[\rho]
is central and
z\neq-2
,
x
does not belong to the Azumaya locus.

•

If
[\rho]=[\rho_{0}]
is the quaternionic representation, then
x
belongs to the Azumaya locus if and only if
z=-2
.

•

If
[\rho]
is neither central nor quaternionic, then
x
belongs to the Azumaya locus.

The first two points are immediate. The third follows from the existence of the Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev representations whereas the fourth follows from our study of the representations in
[
BCGP16
]
. The last point follows by combining the works in
[
FKL23
]
and
[
HP01
]
. In this case, we could not decide whether the four points
([\rho],-2)
for
[\rho]
central belong to the Azumaya locus or not.
Soon after the prepublication of the present paper, Yu proved in
[
Yu23
]
that these four points belong to the Azumaya locus and compute the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{0,4})
as well.

For
M
a
3
-manifold, the Chebyshev-Frobenius morphism
Ch_{A}:\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M)\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)
turns
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)
into a
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M)
-module; equivalently it defines a coherent sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{M}\to\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
. For
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
corresponding to a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{[\rho]}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M)
, the
reduced skein module
is the quotient:

\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$Ch_{A}(\mathfrak{m}_{[\rho]})\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)$}\right.}.

Equivalently, it is the space of sections of the fiber
\mathchoice{{\mathscr{L}^{M}\,\smash{\vrule height=7.12982pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,[\rho]}}{{\mathscr{L}^{M}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.72983pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,[\rho]}}{{\mathscr{L}^{M}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.1476pt,depth=2.20056pt}}_{\,[\rho]}}{{\mathscr{L}^{M}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.8889pt,depth=2.78612pt}}_{\,[\rho]}}
of
\mathscr{L}^{M}
over
[\rho]
.
In
[
FKL23
]
, the authors proved that for
M
a closed
3
-manifold and
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
the class of an irreducible representation then
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}

is one-dimensional as a consequence of the fact that irreducible characters are in the Azumaya locus of closed surfaces
\Sigma_{g}
and of punctured disks
\Sigma_{0,g+1}
.
As explained in Section
6.2
, their argument extends word-by-word to deduce from Theorem
1.2
and
1.5
, the following stronger statement.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
M
be a closed oriented
3
manifold and let
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
be the class of a non-central representation. Then
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}

is one-dimensional.

So
\mathscr{L}^{M}
is a line bundle over the locus of non-central representations. For applications of Theorem
1.6
, see
[
DKS23
,
Det23
]
.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)
be the skein module over the ring
\mathbb{Q}[v^{\pm 1}]
, so
v
is generic formal parameter.
We say that the skein module
\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)
is
A
-
tame
if is a direct sum of a cyclic
\mathbb{Q}[v^{\pm 1}]
-modules which does not contain any summand of the form
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{Q}[v^{\pm 1}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\phi_{N}(v))$}\right.}
if
\varepsilon=+1
or of the form
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{Q}[v^{\pm 1}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\phi_{2N}(v))$}\right.}
if
\varepsilon=-1
, where
\phi_{N}(X)
is the cyclotomic polynomial; see
[
DKS23
]
for a slightly different definition of
A
-tame.
Using the results of
[
DKS23
]
, we easily deduce the following:

Theorem 1.7
.

Suppose that
M
is a closed
3
-manifold such that
(1)

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
is finite and reduced and
(2)
the skein module
\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)
is
A
-tame. Then for any central representation
[\rho]
,
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}
is one-dimensional. So
\mathscr{L}^{M}\to\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
is a line bundle.

It is very hard to decide when
M
satisfies hypotheses
(1)
and
(2)
; see however
[
DKS23
]
for large classes of examples.
Two years after the prepublication of the present paper, the second author proved in
[
Kor
]
that

\mathscr{L}^{M}\to\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
is a line bundle for any closed
3
-manifold
M
without assumptions
(1)
or
(2)
needed.

1.3.
Plan of the paper

In Section
2
, we review the results stated in Subsection
1.1
, mainly the Chebyshev-Frobenius morphism, the Azumaya locus, the theory of Poisson orders and the WRT representations.
Sections
3
and
4
are devoted to the study of the skein representations derived from non-semisimple TQFTs. In the original construction in
[
BCGP16
]
, the authors considered
A
to be a root of unity of even order, whereas our paper is concerned about odd roots of unity, so we will need to adapt the construction in
[
BCGP16
]
to our setting. In Section
3
we describe the category underlying the construction of these non-semisimple TQFTs and in Section
4
we construct the skein representations derived from these TQFTs and prove Theorem
1.3
.
In Section
5
we extend the previous results to open surfaces and prove Theorem
1.5
.
In Section
6.1
we give a simple alternative construction of the representations of the Torelli groups arising in non-semisimple TQFTs.
In Section
6.2
we prove Theorems
1.6
and
1.7
.
In the appendices, we prove some technical results concerning the multiplicity modules and
6j
-symbols in the category of representations of the unrolled quantum group.

2.
Preliminary results on Kauffman-bracket skein algebras

2.1.
Kauffman-bracket skein algebras

Definition 2.1
.

Let
M
be a compact oriented
3
-manifold,
k
be a (unital, associative) commutative ring and
A\in k^{\times}
an invertible element. The
Kauffman-bracket skein module

\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)
is the quotient of the
k
-module freely generated by embedded framed links
L\subset M
by the ideal generated by elements
L-L^{\prime}
, for
L,L^{\prime}
two isotopic links, and by the following skein relations:

\hbox to17.47pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.7359pt\lower-8.7359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

When
M=\Sigma\times[0,1]
is a thickened surface, then
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma):=\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma\times[0,1])
has a structure of associative unital algebra where
the product of two classes links
[L_{1}]
and
[L_{2}]
is defined by isotoping
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
in
\Sigma\times(1/2,1)
and
\Sigma\times(0,1/2)
respectively and then setting
[L_{1}]\cdot[L_{2}]:=[L_{1}\cup L_{2}]
.

A
multi-curve

\gamma\subset\Sigma
is a one-dimensional compact submanifold without contractible component. It defines a framed link through the inclusion
\gamma\subset\Sigma\times\{1/2\}\subset\Sigma\times(0,1)
with blackboard framing. A
curve
is a connected multi-curve.

Theorem 2.2
.

(1)

(Przytycki
[
Prz99
]
) The set

\mathcal{B}:=\{[\gamma]\mid\gamma\mbox{ is a multi-curve in }\Sigma\}

is a basis of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
.

(2)

(Bullock
[
Bul99
]
, see also
[
AF17
,
FK18
,
San18
]
) There exists a finite set of curves whose images generate
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
as an algebra.

(3)

(Przytycki-Sikora
[
PS19
]
for closed surfaces, Bonahon-Wong
[
BW11
]
for open surfaces)
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
is a domain.

A=\pm 1
, the skein algebra
\mathcal{S}_{\pm 1}(\Sigma)

The
n
-th Chebyshev polynomial of first kind is the polynomial
T_{n}(X)\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
defined by the recursive formulas
T_{0}(X)=2
,
T_{1}(X)=X
and
T_{n+2}(X)=XT_{n+1}(X)-T_{n}(X)
for
n\geq 0
. It is characterized by the formula

T_{n}(\operatorname{tr}(M))=\operatorname{tr}(M^{n})\mbox{, for all }M\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
N\geq 3
an odd integer.
Suppose that
A\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
is a root of unity of order
N
or
2N
. Write
\varepsilon=+1
if the order of
A
is
N
and
\varepsilon=-1
if the order of
A
is
2N
.

(1)

(Bonahon-Wong
[
BW16a
]
) There exists an embedding

Ch_{A}:\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)\right)

into the center of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
characterized by the formula
Ch_{A}([\gamma]):=T_{N}([\gamma])
for
\gamma\subset\Sigma
a curve.

(2)

(Frohman-Kania Bartoszynska-Lê
[
FKL19
]
) For
\Sigma
a closed surface
Ch_{A}
is onto, so provides an isomorphism between the skein algebra at
A=\varepsilon
and the center
Z_{\Sigma}
of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
is finitely generated over
Z_{\Sigma}
.

Proposition 2.4
.

(Barrett
[
Bar99
]
) Let
S
be a spin structure on
\Sigma
with Johnson quadratic form
w_{S}:\mathrm{H}_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. There is an algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{-A}(\Sigma)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
sending
[\gamma]
to
(-1)^{w_{S}([\gamma])}[\gamma]
.

2.2.
Azumaya locus

Definition 2.5
.

A
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is
almost Azumaya
if:

(i)

\mathcal{A}
is affine , i.e.
A
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{C}
-algebra;

(ii)

\mathcal{A}
is prime, i.e. any
a,b\in A
satisfy that if
arb=0
for all
r\in A
then
a=0
or
b=0
;

(iii)

\mathcal{A}
has finite rank over its center
Z
.

Let
S:=Z\setminus\{0\}
so that
K:=ZS^{-1}
is a field. A theorem of Posner-Formanek (see
[
BG02
, Theorem
I.13.3
]
) asserts that the localization
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{Z}K
is a central simple algebra over
K
, therefore there exists a finite extension
\overline{K}
of
K
such that
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{Z}\overline{K}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(\overline{K})
is a matrix algebra. The integer
D
is called the
PI-degree
of
\mathcal{A}
and is characterized by the formula:

\dim_{\overline{K}}\mathcal{A}\otimes_{Z}\overline{K}=D^{2}.

By Theorem
2.3
,
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
is almost Azumaya.

Theorem 2.6
.

(Frohman-Kania Bartoszynska-Lê
[
FKL21
]
)
The algebra
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
has PI-degree
N^{3g-3}
if
g\geq 2
and
N
if
g=1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an almost Azumaya algebra with center
Z
,
\mathcal{X}:=\operatorname{MaxSpec}(Z)
and for each maximal ideal
x\in\mathcal{X}
, consider the finite dimensional algebra

\mathcal{A}_{x}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{A}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$x\mathcal{A}$}\right.}.

Note that when
\mathcal{A}
is a domain, by the Artin-Tate lemma,
Z
is finitely generated so
\mathcal{X}
is an irreducible affine variety.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\rho:\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
be a representation.

(1)

\rho
is a
central representation
if
it sends central elements to scalar operators. In this case, it induces a character over the center
Z
, so a point in
\mathcal{X}
named its
classical shadow
.

(2)

\rho
is a
weight representation
if
V
is semisimple as a
Z
-module, i.e. if
V
is a direct sum of central representations.

Indecomposable weight representations are clearly central. A central representation
\rho:\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
with classical shadow
x
induces by quotient a representation
\rho:\mathcal{A}_{x}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
.

Definition 2.8
.

The
Azumaya locus
of
\mathcal{A}
is the subset

\mathcal{AL}:=\{x\in\mathcal{X},\mbox{ such that }\mathcal{A}_{x}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(\mathbb{C})\}.

Note that
\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(\mathbb{C})
is simple with only simple module the standard module
\mathbb{C}^{D}
.
So if
x\in\mathcal{AL}
, there exists a unique indecomposable representation of
\mathcal{A}
with classical shadow
x
(up to isomorphism); this representation is irreducible and has dimension
D
.
The following theorem is essentially due to De Concini-Kac in their original work on quantum enveloping algebras
[
DCK89
]
and was further generalized by several authors including
[
DCL94
,
BY17
,
BG02
,
FKL19
]
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an almost Azumaya algebra with PI-degree
D
.

(1)

(
[
BG02
, Theorem III.I.7]
) The Azumaya locus is a Zariski open dense subset.

(2)

(
[
BG02
, Theorem III.I.6]
) If
\rho:\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
is an irreducible representation whose classical shadow is not in the Azumaya locus, then
\dim(V)<D
. So the Azumaya locus admits the following alternative description:

\mathcal{AL}=\{x\in\mathcal{X}|x\mbox{ is induced by an irreducible representation of maximal dimension }D\}.

Therefore, if
\rho:\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
is a
D
-dimensional central representation inducing
x\in\mathcal{X}
, then
\rho
is irreducible if and only if
x\in\mathcal{AL}
.

Corollary 2.10
.

(Frohman-Kania Bartoszynska-Lê
[
FKL19
]
)
The Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
is a Zariski open dense subset.

Corollary 2.11
.

If
\rho:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
is a central representation with classical shadow
x
such that
\dim(V)=N^{3g-3}
when
g\geq 2
and
\dim(V)=N
when
g=1
, then

\rho\mbox{ is irreducible if and only if }x\in\mathcal{AL}.

Note that these results were extended to stated skein algebras and their reduced versions in
[
Kor21c
]
.

2.3.
Character varieties and deformation quantization

The commutative algebra
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma)
has a natural Poisson structure defined as follows. Let
\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\Sigma)
be the skein algebra associated to the ring
k=\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
of formal power series in
\hbar
with parameter
A_{\hbar}:=\varepsilon\exp(\hbar/2)
. Since the set
\mathcal{B}
of classes of multicurves is a basis for both the algebras
\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma)
, we can define a
\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
-linear isomorphism
\Psi:\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\Sigma)
characterized by the fact that it sends a basis elements
[\gamma]\in\mathcal{B}
to itself. Let
\star
be the pull-back in
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
of the product in
\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\Sigma)
.

Definition 2.12
.

The Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma)
is defined by the formula

x\star y-y\star x=\hbar\{x,y\}\pmod{\hbar^{2}}.

In particular, the associativity of
\star
implies the Jacobi identity for
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
. The Poisson algebra
(\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma),\{\cdot,\cdot\})
admits the following geometric interpretation.

Definition 2.13
.

Suppose that
\Sigma
is connected and fix a base point
v\in\Sigma
.

(1)

The
representation variety

\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
is the space of representations
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
equipped with its natural structure of affine variety. The group
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
acts algebraically on
\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
by conjugacy. The
character variety
is the algebraic (GIT) quotient

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma):=\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\operatorname{SL}_{2}.

Note that
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
does not depend on the base point
v
up to canonical isomorphism.

(2)

For
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)
, the
trace function
is the regular map
\tau_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)]
defined by

\tau_{\gamma}([\rho]):=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma))\mbox{, for all }\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v),\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}.

(3)

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{C}^{*}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}
be the diagonal embedding sending
z
to
\begin{pmatrix}z&0\\
0&z^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
.

A representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is said

•

irreducible
if
\rho
is irreducible;

•

central
if it factorizes as
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)\to\{-1,+1\}\xrightarrow{\subset}\mathbb{C}^{*}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
;

•

diagonal
if it is not central and it is conjugate to a representation which factorizes as
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)\to\mathbb{C}^{*}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
.

For
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g}
, we denote by
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
,
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
the subvarieties of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
of classes of representations which are irreducible, diagonal and central respectively, so we get the decomposition

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{X}^{(0)}\bigsqcup\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\bigsqcup\mathcal{X}^{(2)}&\mbox{when }g\geq 2;\\
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\bigsqcup\mathcal{X}^{(2)}&\mbox{when }g=1.\end{array}\right.

Note that
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
is finite and that, when
g\geq 2
, the locus of diagonal representations is defined by the following algebraic equations which say that the trace of the commutator of
\rho(\alpha)
and
\rho(\beta)
is
+2
(see e.g.
[
Mar16
]
for details):

\tau_{\alpha}^{2}+\tau_{\beta}^{2}+\tau_{\alpha\beta}^{2}-\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}\tau_{\alpha\beta}-4=0\mbox{, for }\alpha,\beta\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v);

so
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
is indeed open in the Zariski topology.
For
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
, the stabilizer for the
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
action by conjugacy is equal to
\{\pm\mathds{1}_{2}\}
,
\varphi(\mathbb{C}^{*})
and
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
when
\rho
is irreducible, diagonal and central respectively. It follows that, when
g\geq 2
,
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
is the smooth locus of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
and that
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
is the smooth locus of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})\setminus\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
. In the genus one case,
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
is the smooth locus of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1})
.

The character variety has a Poisson structure defined by Goldman in
[
Gol86
]
and described as follows. By a theorem of Procesi (
[
Pro87
]
), the algebra of regular functions of the character variety is generated by curve functions, so we need to define the Poisson bracket of two such functions
\tau_{\alpha}
and
\tau_{\beta}
. Clearly, a trace function
\tau_{\alpha}
only depends on the isotopy class of
\alpha
seen as an immersed curve in
\Sigma
.

Suppose that
\alpha
and
\beta
are two embedded curves which intersect transversally with at most double intersection points. For
v\in\alpha\cap\beta
, let
\alpha_{v},\beta_{v}\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)
be representatives of
\alpha
and
\beta
and
\rho_{v}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
a representative of
[\rho]
.
Let
\epsilon(v)\in\{-1,+1\}
be equal to
+1
if the set
(T_{v}\alpha,T_{v}\beta)
of tangent vectors of
\alpha
and
\beta
at
v
forms an oriented basis of
T_{v}\Sigma
and set
\epsilon(v)=-1
else.
Let
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right):\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\otimes\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
be the non-degenerate Ad-invariant symmetric form normalized so that
(A,B)=\operatorname{tr}(AB)
for
A,B\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
(i.e.
\frac{1}{4}
times the Killing form). For
f\in\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
a conjugacy invariant function (for instance
f=\tau
the trace function), let

X_{f,\alpha}(v)\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
be the unique vector satisfying

(X_{f,\alpha},Y)=\mathchoice{{\frac{d}{dt}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=1.27501pt}}_{\,t=0}}{{\frac{d}{dt}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.88889pt,depth=1.27501pt}}_{\,t=0}}{{\frac{d}{dt}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=0.85pt}}_{\,t=0}}{{\frac{d}{dt}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=1.275pt}}_{\,t=0}}f(\rho_{v}(\alpha_{v})e^{tY}),\mbox{ for all }Y\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}.

Extend
(\cdot,\cdot)
to a bilinear form on
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{\otimes 2}
by
(x\otimes y,z\otimes t):=(x,z)(y,t)
.

Definition 2.14
.

(Goldman
[
Gol86
]
) The Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on
\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})]
is defined by the following formula

\displaystyle\{\tau_{\alpha},\tau_{\beta}\}([\rho])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{v\in c_{1}\cap c_{2}}\epsilon(v)\left(X_{\tau,\alpha}(v),X_{\tau,\beta}(v)\right).

Goldman gave in
[
Gol86
]
an explicit formula which shows that
\{\tau_{\alpha},\tau_{\beta}\}
is a linear combination of trace functions, so the Poisson bracket of two regular functions is a regular function. Goldman original work concerns closed surfaces, though it was extended to open surfaces as well in
[
Law09
,
Kor19b
]
.
When
\Sigma
is closed, it is proved in
[
Gol84
,
Gol86
]
that the smooth locus of the character variety is isomorphic to the smooth part of the moduli space of flat
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
structures on
\Sigma
equipped with its Atiyah-Bott symplectic structure defined in
[
AB83
]
.
In particular, one has:

Theorem 2.15
.

(Goldman
[
Gol84
,
Gol86
]
) When
\Sigma
is closed, the smooth locus of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
is symplectic.

.

Lemma 2.16
.

When
\Sigma
is closed, the diagonal locus
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
is symplectic.

Proof.

Consider the symplectic torus
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)
defined as follows. As an affine variety

\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma):=\operatorname{MaxSpec}\left(\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})]\right).

Here
\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})]
denotes the group algebra
with basis consisting of
X_{[\gamma]}
for
[\gamma]\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
and relations
X_{[\gamma_{1}]}X_{[\gamma_{2}]}=X_{[\gamma_{1}]+[\gamma_{2}]}
.
The symplectic structure is given by the formula

\{X_{[\alpha]},X_{[\beta]}\}:=([\alpha],[\beta])X_{[\alpha+\beta]},

where
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the intersection form. Since
\Sigma
is closed, the intersection form has trivial kernel so the Poisson structure is indeed symplectic. Note that the set of closed points of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)
is identified with
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{*})
. The diagonal inclusion
\varphi:\mathbb{C}^{*}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}
induces a regular morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
sending
\chi:\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
to
\Phi(\chi):=[\varphi\circ\chi]
. Said differently,
\Phi^{*}:\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)]\to\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})]
sends
\tau_{\gamma}
to
X_{[\gamma]}+X_{-[\gamma]}
. Clearly
\Phi:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
is a double branched covering branched along
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
.

Let us prove that
\Phi
is Poisson. Let
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
, consider two simple closed curves
\alpha,\beta
which intersects transversally as before and let
v\in\alpha\cap\beta
. Let

\rho_{v}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)\xrightarrow{\lambda}\mathbb{C}^{*}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
be a diagonal representation representing the class
[\rho]
. For
Y=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&-a\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, then

\tau(\rho_{v}(\alpha_{v})Y)=\operatorname{Tr}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\lambda(\alpha_{v})&0\\
0&\lambda(\alpha_{v})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&-a\end{pmatrix}\right)=(\lambda(\alpha)-\lambda(\alpha)^{-1})a=(\lambda(\alpha)-\lambda(\alpha)^{-1})(\frac{H}{2},Y).

Therefore
X_{\tau,\alpha}(v)=(\lambda(\alpha)-\lambda(\alpha)^{-1})\frac{H}{2}
so

(X_{\tau,\alpha}(v),X_{\tau,\beta}(v))=(\lambda(\alpha)-\lambda(\alpha)^{-1})(\lambda(\beta)-\lambda(\beta)^{-1})\frac{(H,H)}{4}=\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{[\alpha+\beta]}-\tau_{[\alpha-\beta]})(\rho_{v}).

So Goldman’s formula in Definition
2.14
reads:

\{\tau_{[\alpha]},\tau_{[\beta]}\}([\rho])=\left(\sum_{v\in\alpha\cap\beta}\epsilon(v)\right)(\tau_{[\alpha+\beta]}-\tau_{[\alpha-\beta]})([\rho])=([\alpha],[\beta])(\tau_{[\alpha+\beta]}-\tau_{[\alpha-\beta]})([\rho]).

On the other hand, one has

\{\Phi^{*}(\tau_{\alpha}),\Phi^{*}(\tau_{\beta})\}=\{X_{[\alpha]}+X_{[\alpha]}^{-1},X_{[\beta]}+X_{[\beta]}^{-1}\}=([\alpha],[\beta])(X_{[\alpha+\beta]}+X_{[\alpha+\beta]}^{-1}-X_{[\alpha-\beta]}-X_{[\alpha-\beta]}^{-1})\\
=([\alpha],[\beta])(\Phi^{*}(\tau_{[\alpha+\beta]}+\tau_{[\alpha-\beta]})).

Therefore
\{\Phi^{*}(\tau_{[\alpha]}),\Phi^{*}(\tau_{[\beta]})\}=\Phi^{*}(\{\tau_{[\alpha]},\tau_{[\beta]}\})
so
\Phi^{*}
is Poisson. Since
\Phi:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
is a double branched covering, regular at
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
, the fact that
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)
is symplectic implies that
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
is symplectic as well.
∎

Theorem 2.17
.

(
[
Bul97
,
PS00
,
Bar99
,
Tur91
]
)
Let
S
be a spin structure on
\Sigma
and denote by
w_{S}:\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
its associated Johnson quadratic form. There exists a Poisson isomorphisms

\Psi:(\mathcal{S}_{-1}(\Sigma),\{\cdot,\cdot\})\xrightarrow{\cong}(\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)],\{\cdot,\cdot\})\ \mbox{ and }\ \Psi^{S}:(\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\Sigma),\{\cdot,\cdot\})\xrightarrow{\cong}(\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)],\{\cdot,\cdot\})

characterized by

\Psi([\gamma]):=-\tau_{\gamma},\quad\Psi^{S}([\gamma]):=(-1)^{w_{S}([\gamma])+1}\tau_{\gamma}.

From now on, we fix once and for all a spin structure on
\Sigma
if
\varepsilon=+1
and identify
\operatorname{MaxSpec}(\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma))
with
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
. In particular, we consider classical shadows as closed points in
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
.

2.4.
Poisson orders

The theory of Poisson orders was created by DeConcini-Kac in their original work on quantum algebras
[
DCK89
]
and generalized by Brown-Gordon in
[
BG03
]
.

Definition 2.18
.

A
Poisson order
is a
4
-tuple
(\mathcal{A},{X},\phi,D)
where:

(1)

\mathcal{A}
is an (associative, unital) affine prime
\mathbb{C}
-algebra finitely generated over its center
\mathcal{Z}
;

(2)

{X}
is a Poisson affine
\mathbb{C}
-variety;

(3)

\phi:\mathcal{O}[{X}]\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}
a finite injective morphism of algebras;

(4)

D:\mathcal{O}[{X}]\to Der(\mathcal{A}):z\mapsto D_{z}
a linear map such that for all
f,g\in\mathcal{O}[{X}]
, we have

D_{f}(\phi(g))=\phi(\{f,g\}).

Let
X
be an affine complex Poisson variety.

•

Define a first partition
X=X^{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup X^{n}
where
X^{0}
is the smooth locus of
X
and for
i=0,\ldots,n-1
,
X^{i+1}
is the smooth locus of
X\setminus(X^{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup X^{i})
. Then each
X^{i}
is a smooth complex affine variety that can be seen as an analytic Poisson variety.

•

Define an equivalence relation
\sim
on
X^{i}
by
writing
x\sim y
if there exists a finite sequence
x=p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}=y
and functions
h_{0},\ldots,h_{k-1}\in\mathcal{O}[X^{i}]
such that
p_{i+1}
is obtained from
p_{i}
by deriving along the Hamiltonian flow of
h_{i}
. Write
X^{i}=\bigsqcup_{j}X^{i,j}
the orbits of this relation.

Definition 2.19
.

The elements
X^{i,j}
of the (analytic) partition
X=\bigsqcup_{i,j}X^{i,j}
are called the
symplectic leaves
of
X
.

The symplectic leaves are analytic subsets. One can also define a partition into algebraic subsets as follows. An ideal
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{O}[X]
is a
Poisson ideal
if
\{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{O}[{X}]\}\subset\mathcal{I}
. Since the sum of two Poisson ideals is a Poisson ideal, every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathcal{O}[{X}]
contains a unique maximal Poisson ideal
P(\mathfrak{m})\subset\mathfrak{m}
. Define an equivalence relation
\sim
on
{X}=\mathrm{Specm}(\mathcal{O}[{X}])
by
\mathfrak{m}\sim\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
if
P(\mathfrak{m})=P(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
. The equivalence class of
\mathfrak{m}
is called its
symplectic core
and is denoted by
C(\mathfrak{m})
.

Definition 2.20
.

The partition
{X}=\bigsqcup_{\mathfrak{m}\in{X/\sim}}C(\mathfrak{m})
is called the
symplectic cores partition
of
{X}
.

If
x,y\in{X}
belong to the same smooth strata of
{X}
and are such that
y
can be obtained from
x
by deriving along some Hamiltonian flow, Brown and Gordon proved that
C(x)=C(y)
from which they deduce the

Proposition 2.21
.

(Brown-Gordon
[
BG03
, Proposition
3.6
]
) The symplectic leaves partition is a refinement of the symplectic cores partition.

If
X^{i,j}
is a symplectic leaf contained in a symplectic core
C
, then
C
is the smallest algebraic subset containing
X^{i,j}
.

Theorem 2.22
.

(Brown-Gordon
[
BG03
, Theorem
4.2
]
) Let
(\mathcal{A},{X},\phi,D)
be a Poisson order. If
x,y\in{X}
belong to the same symplectic core, then
\mathcal{A}_{x}\cong\mathcal{A}_{y}
.

Note that Theorems
2.9
and
2.22
imply that if
X
contains a symplectic leaf (or core) which is dense, then it is included into the Azumaya locus.

Here is our main source of examples of Poisson orders; we closely follow
[
BG03
]
to which we refer for further details.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{q}
be a free affine
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
-algebra which is a domain. Let
N\geq 1
, consider the
k_{N}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(q^{N}-1)$}\right.}
algebra
\mathcal{A}_{N}:=\mathcal{A}_{q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]}k_{N}
and denote by
\pi:\mathcal{A}_{q}\to\mathcal{A}_{N}
the quotient map. Fix a basis
\mathcal{B}
of the
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
module
\mathcal{A}_{q}
. Then
\mathcal{A}_{N}
has basis
\{q^{i}\pi(b),b\in\mathcal{B},i=0,\ldots,N-1\}
over
\mathbb{C}
.
Define a
\mathbb{C}
linear embedding
\hat{\cdot}:\mathcal{A}_{N}\to\mathcal{A}_{q}
sending a basis element
x=q^{i}\pi(b)\in\mathcal{A}_{N}
to the element
\hat{x}:=q^{i}b\in\mathcal{A}_{q}
. Note that
\hat{\cdot}
is a left inverse for
\pi
. Suppose that the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{+1}=\mathcal{A}_{q}\otimes_{q=1}\mathbb{C}
is commutative and suppose there exists a central embedding
\phi_{N}:\mathcal{A}_{+1}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{N})
into the center of
\mathcal{A}_{N}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{N}
is finitely generated as an
\mathcal{A}_{+1}
-module. The pair
(\mathcal{A}_{q},\phi_{N})
will be referred to as a
quantum algebra
.
Write
\mathcal{X}:=\mathrm{Specm}(\mathcal{A}_{+1})
and define
D:\mathcal{A}_{+1}\to Der(\mathcal{A}_{N})
by the formula

D_{x}y:=\pi\left(\frac{[\widehat{\phi_{N}(x)},\hat{y}]}{N(q^{N}-1)}\right).

Clearly
D_{x}
is a derivation, is independent on the choice of the basis
\mathcal{B}
and preserves
\phi_{N}(\mathcal{A}_{+1})
, so it defines a Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{N}
on
\mathcal{A}_{+1}
by

(3)

D_{x}\phi_{N}(y)=\phi_{N}(\{x,y\}_{N}).

So
\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Specm}(\mathcal{A}_{+1})
is a Poisson variety. Let
\zeta
be a root of unity of order
N
, write
\mathcal{A}_{\zeta}=\mathcal{A}_{q}\otimes_{q=\zeta}\mathbb{C}
and consider
D:\mathcal{A}_{+1}\to Der(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta})
and
\phi_{\zeta}
obtained by tensoring by
\mathbb{C}
.
Then
(\mathcal{A}_{\zeta},\mathcal{X},\phi_{\zeta},D)
is a Poisson order. Note that
\mathcal{X}
also admits a Poisson structure coming from deformation quantization, namely setting
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}=\mathcal{A}_{q}\otimes_{q=\exp(\hbar)}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
with product
\star_{\hbar}
, we also have a bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
by the formula

a\star b-b\star a\equiv\{a,b\}\hbar\pmod{\hbar^{2}}.

It is not clear that the two brackets
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
and
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{N}
coincide in general. When the two brackets coincide, we say that the quantum algebra
(\mathcal{A}_{q},\phi_{N})
is
regular
.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\Sigma)
be the skein algebra over the ring
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
and
Ch_{N}:\gamma\mapsto T_{N}(\gamma)
.
Clearly
(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\Sigma),Ch_{N})
is a quantum algebra so we have a Poisson order
(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma),\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma),Ch_{A},D)
.

Lemma 2.23
.

(
[
Kor21a
, Lemma
4.6
]
)
(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\Sigma),Ch_{A})
is regular, i.e.
the Poisson brackets
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
and
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{N}
on
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma)
coming respectively from deformation quantization (Definition
2.12
) and from the Poisson order
structure coincide.

Since Lemma
2.23
is published in a non peer-reviewed journal, we add its proof for the reader convenience.

Proof.

We suppose
\varepsilon=+1
, the case
\varepsilon=-1
is similar.
We first prove the analogue of Lemma
2.23
in the easier case of a quantum torus. Let
E
be a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of finite rank (so
E\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
) and
(\cdot,\cdot):E\otimes E\to\mathbb{Z}
a skew-symmetric pairing. The associated
quantum torus

\mathbb{T}_{q}
is the
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
-algebra equal to
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}][Z_{e},e\in E]
as a module with product
Z_{a}Z_{b}=q^{(a,b)}Z_{a+b}
. Clearly
\mathbb{T}_{q}
is free with basis
\{Z_{e},e\in E\}
, is a domain and is finitely generated (by the set
Z_{e}
for
e
in a generating set of
E
).
When
\zeta
is a root of unity of order
N
, the Frobenius morphism
Fr_{N}:\mathbb{T}_{+1}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{T}_{\zeta})
is defined by
Fr_{N}(Z_{e})=(Z_{e})^{N}(=Z_{Ne})
.
By the preceding discussion we obtain a Poisson order
(\mathbb{T}_{\zeta},\mathcal{X},Fr_{N},D)
where
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{MaxSpec}(\mathbb{T}_{+1})\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
is equipped with the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{N}
. On the one hand, the Poisson bracket coming from deformation quantization is computed via:

Z_{a}\star Z_{b}-Z_{b}\star Z_{a}=(q^{(a,b)}-q^{-(a,b)})Z_{a+b}=\hbar 2(a,b)Z_{a+b}\pmod{\hbar^{2}},

giving the Poisson bracket
\{Z_{a},Z_{b}\}=2(a,b)Z_{a+b}
. On the other hand, the Poisson bracket coming from the Poisson order structure is computed via:

\displaystyle\pi\left(\frac{[Fr_{N}(Z_{a}),Fr_{N}(Z_{b})]}{N(q^{N}-1)}\right)=\pi\left(\frac{[Z_{a}^{N},Z_{b}^{N}]}{N(q^{N}-1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi\left(\frac{q^{N^{2}(a,b)}-q^{-N^{2}(a,b)}}{N(q^{N}-1)}\right)Z_{a+b}^{N}

\displaystyle=\pi\left(q^{-N^{2}(a,b)}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=0}^{2N(a,b)-1}q^{Ni}\right)Fr_{N}(Z_{a+b})=Fr_{N}(2(a,b)Z_{a+b}).

So
\{Z_{a},Z_{b}\}_{N}=2(a,b)Z_{a+b}
and the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{N}
coincides with the bracket coming from deformation quantization and
(\mathbb{T}_{q},Fr_{N})
is regular.

A morphism of quantum algebras
f:(\mathcal{A}_{q},\phi_{N})\to(\mathcal{B}_{q},\Psi_{N})
is an algebra morphism
f:\mathcal{A}_{q}\to\mathcal{B}_{q}
such that the induced morphisms
f_{N}:\mathcal{A}_{N}\to\mathcal{B}_{N}
and
f_{+1}:\mathcal{A}_{+1}\to\mathcal{B}_{+1}
satisfy
\Psi_{N}\circ f_{+1}=f_{N}\circ\phi_{N}
. Clearly
f_{+1}
is a Poisson morphism for both the deformation quantization and the Poisson order Poisson brackets. In particular if
f
is injective, then
(\mathcal{B}_{q},\Psi_{N})
is regular implies that
(\mathcal{A}_{q},\phi_{N})
is regular as well. For an open surface
\Sigma_{g,n}
with
n\geq 1
, Bonahon and Wong defined in
[
BW11
]
an injective morphism of quantum algebras
\operatorname{Tr}_{q}^{\Delta}:(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\Sigma_{g,n}),Ch_{N})\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{T}_{q},Fr_{N})
, named the quantum trace, from the skein algebra to a quantum torus named the balanced Chekhov-Fock algebra so
(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\Sigma_{g,n}),Ch_{N})
is regular.

Similarly, if
p:(\mathcal{A}_{q},\phi_{N})\twoheadrightarrow(\mathcal{B}_{q},\Psi_{N})
is a surjective morphism, then
(\mathcal{A}_{q},\phi_{N})
is regular implies that
(\mathcal{B}_{q},\Psi_{N})
is regular as well. The inclusion
\iota:\Sigma_{g,1}\hookrightarrow\Sigma_{g}
induces a surjective morphism
\iota_{*}:(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\Sigma_{g,1}),Ch_{N})\to(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\Sigma_{g}),Ch_{N})
(sending
\gamma
to
\iota(\gamma)
), so
(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\Sigma_{g}),Ch_{N})
is regular.
∎

Remark 2.24
.

The above proof generalizes word-by-word to prove that the stated skein algebras and the reduced stated skein algebras define regular quantum algebras as well. Indeed, stated skein algebras can be embedded into reduced stated skein algebra (see
[
LYa
]
) and Bonahon-Wong quantum trace embeds reduced stated skein algebras into quantum tori as well (see
[
Le18
,
CL22
]
).

By Theorem
2.15
and Lemma
2.16
, both
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
are symplectic leaves of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
. Since the smooth locus is dense, Corollary
2.10
and Theorem
2.22
imply the

Corollary 2.25
.

(Ganev-Jordan-Safronov
[
GJS24
, Theorem
1.1
]
) The smooth locus of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
is included into the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
.

Corollary 2.26
.

For
g\geq 2
, if the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
intersects
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
non-trivially, then it contains
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
.

2.5.
Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev representations

The Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFTs from
[
Wit89
,
RT91
]
provide representations of Kauffman-bracket skein algebras at roots of unity. Let us describe a simple construction following
[
BHMV95
]
.
Let
H_{g}
be a genus
g
handlebody and consider the skein module
\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g})
. By identifying a tubular neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
of
\partial H_{g}
with
\Sigma_{g}\times[0,1]
, we get an oriented embedding
i:\Sigma_{g}\times[0,1]\to H_{g}
. We consider
\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g})
as a
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
-left module by defining for two framed links
L_{1}\subset\Sigma_{g}\times[0,1]
and
L_{2}\subset H_{g}
the module product
[L_{1}]\cdot[L_{2}]:=[i(L_{1})\cup L_{2}]
, where
L_{2}
has been first isotoped outside
\mathcal{N}
.
Fix a genus
g
Heegaard splitting of the sphere, i.e. two oriented homeomorphisms
S:\partial H_{g}\cong\overline{\partial H_{g}}
and

H_{g}\bigcup_{S:\partial H_{g}\rightarrow\overline{\partial H_{g}}}H_{g}\cong S^{3}
. Denote by
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}:H_{g}\hookrightarrow S^{3}
the embeddings into the first and second factors.

For two framed links
L_{1},L_{2}\subset H_{g}
, the above gluing defines a link
\varphi_{1}(L_{1})\bigcup\varphi_{2}(L_{2})\subset S^{3}
. Let
\left<\cdot\right>:\mathcal{S}_{A}(S^{3})\cong\mathbb{C}
be the isomorphism sending the empty link to
1
. The Hopf pairing is the Hermitian form:

\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{A}^{H}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g})\times\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

defined by

\left(L_{1},L_{2}\right)_{A}^{H}:=\left<\varphi_{1}(L_{1})\bigcup\varphi_{2}(L_{2})\right>

and the spaces
V_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
are the quotients:

V_{A}(\Sigma_{g}):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{Rker}\left(\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{A}^{H}\right)$}\right.}

The structure of left
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
-module of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g})
induces, by passing to the quotient, the so-called
Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev representation

r^{WRT}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(V_{A}(\Sigma_{g})).

The following theorem was stated for
A
a root of unity of even order, though as detailed in
[
Kor21b
, Theorem
5.7
]
, the proofs extend straightforwardly to the odd case.

Theorem 2.27
.

(1)

(Reshetikhin-Turaev
[
RT91
,
Tur10
]
, see also
[
BHMV95
]
) The space
V_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
are non-zero and finite dimensional with dimension given by the Verlinde formula. In particular
\dim(V_{A}(\Sigma_{g}))<PI-deg(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g}))
.

(2)

(Gelca-Uribe
[
GU15
, Theorem
6.6
]
, see also
[
BW16b
]
) The representation
r^{WRT}
is irreducible.

(3)

(Bonahon-Wong
[
BW16b
]
) The classical shadow of
r^{WRT}
is a central representation.

”Which central representation is the classical shadow of
r^{WRT}
” depends on the choice of spin structure used in the identification of Theorem
2.17
. This knowledge is irrelevant thanks to the

Lemma 2.28
.

If
[\rho_{1}]
,
[\rho_{2}]
are both in
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
then
\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)\right)_{[\rho_{1}]}\cong\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)\right)_{[\rho_{2}]}
.

Proof.

The group
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
acts by automorphisms on
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
by the formula defined on a multi-curve
\gamma=\gamma^{(1)}\cup\ldots\cup\gamma^{(n)}
with connected components
\gamma^{(i)}
and
\omega\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
by the formula

\omega_{*}[\gamma]:=(-1)^{\sum_{i}\omega([\gamma^{(i)}])}[\gamma].

By Theorem
2.3
, this action restricts to an action on the center of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
which, once identified with
\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)]
, corresponds to the algebraic action of
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
on
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
defined by

(\omega\cdot\rho)(\gamma):=(-1)^{\omega([\gamma])}\rho(\gamma).

Therefore, the automorphism
\omega_{*}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma))
induces an isomorphism
\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)\right)_{[\rho]}\cong\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)\right)_{\omega\cdot[\rho]}
.
We conclude using the fact that the action of
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
on
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
is transitive.
∎

Theorem
2.27
implies that the classical shadow of
r^{WRT}
is not in the Azumaya locus. Since it belongs to
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
, Lemma
2.28
implies the

Corollary 2.29
.

The Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
does not intersect
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
.

Together with Corollary
2.25
, this implies the

Corollary 2.30
.

The Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1})
is the locus of non-central representations.

3.
Categories of representations of the unrolled quantum groups

The non-semi extended TQFTs from
[
BCGP16
]
are defined using some
\mathbb{C}
-modular relative categories as described by De Renzi in
[
DR22
]
which are defined as representation categories of (unrolled) quantum groups at root of unity. In this paper, we are interested in the category of representations of
U^{H}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
when
q:=A^{2}
has odd order
N
. The category of representations of
U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
is studied in great details in
[
CGPM15
]
when
q
has even order whereas the category of representations of
U^{H}_{q}\mathfrak{g}
for any simple
\mathfrak{g}
at odd roots of unity is studied in
[
DRGPM20
]
. Our case of interest (
U^{H}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
at odd roots of unity) is much simpler than these two cases though many computations (such as the
6j
symbols) have never been done in this case.
Since we will need to perform precise computations in this category, we now describe it completely.

3.1.
Unrolled quantum groups and their representations

Throughout all this section,
q:=A^{2}
is a root of unity of odd order
N\geq 3
, so
q=\exp(\frac{2i\pi k}{N})
for some
k\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
prime to
N
.
For
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we write
q^{z}:=\exp(\frac{2i\pi kz}{N})
and
A=q^{1/2}
. Set also
\{z\}:=q^{z}-q^{-z}
and
[z]:=\frac{q^{z}-q^{-z}}{q-q^{-1}}
.

Definition 3.1
.

The
unrolled quantum group

U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
is the algebra generated by
E,F,K^{\pm 1}
and
H
with relations:

\displaystyle KE=q^{2}EK,\quad KF=q^{-2}FK,\quad[E,F]=\frac{K-K^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}

\displaystyle[H,E]=2E,\quad[H,F]=-2F,\quad[H,K]=0.

It has a Hopf algebra structure with coproduct:

\Delta(E)=1\otimes E+E\otimes K,\quad\Delta(F)=K^{-1}\otimes F+F\otimes 1,\quad\Delta(K)=K\otimes K,\quad\Delta(H)=H\otimes 1+1\otimes H,

with counit:

\epsilon(E)=\epsilon(F)=\epsilon(H)=0,\quad\epsilon(K)=1,

and antipode:

S(E)=-EK^{-1},\quad S(F)=-KF,\quad S(K)=K^{-1},\quad S(H)=-H.

We denote by
\mathcal{C}
the monoidal category of finite dimensional representations
\rho:U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
such that:

(1)

\rho(E^{N})=\rho(F^{N})=0
,
\rho(K^{N})
is semisimple and

(2)

if
\rho(H)\cdot v=\lambda v
for
v\in V
, then
\rho(K)\cdot v=q^{\lambda}v
.

So
V
is a direct sum of submodules on which
\rho(K^{N})
is scalar; in such a submodule, the minimal polynomial of
\rho(K)
divides
X^{N}-z
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, so its zeroes have multiplicity one. This implies that

\rho(K)
is diagonalizable, so
V
is spanned by vectors
v\in V
such that
Hv=\lambda v
; we call such
v
a
weight vector of weight

\lambda
. For
\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
, we say that
V

has weight

\overline{\alpha}
if it is spanned by weight vectors with weights in
\overline{\alpha}
.
The category
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathcal{C}=\bigsqcup_{\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
where
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
is the full subcategory of modules with weights
\alpha\in\overline{\alpha}
. Since
H
is primitive, one has
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\beta}}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha+\beta}}
. If
V\in\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
,
W\in\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\beta}}
with
\overline{\alpha}\neq\overline{\beta}
, then clearly
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,W)=0
.

Definition 3.2
.

(1)

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, the module
V_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
has the basis
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{N-1}\}
and module structure

Hv_{n}=(\alpha+N-1-2n)v_{n}\quad,Fv_{n}=v_{n+1},Fv_{N-1}=0\quad,Ev_{n}=[n][\alpha-n]v_{n-1},Ev_{0}=0.

(2)

For
n\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
, the module
S_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\overline{0}}
is the
n+1
dimensional module with the basis
\{e_{0},\ldots,e_{n}\}
and module structure

He_{i}=(n-2i)e_{i},\quad Fe_{i}=e_{i+1},Fe_{n}=0,\quad Ee_{i}=[i][n-i+1]e_{i-1},Ee_{0}=0.

(3)

For
n\in\{0,\ldots,N-2\}
, the module
P_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\overline{0}}
is the
2N
dimensional module with the basis
\{x_{i},y_{i},i=0,\ldots N-1\}
and module structure

\displaystyle Hx_{i}=(2N-2-n-2i)x_{i},\quad Hy_{i}=(n-2i)y_{i},\quad Fx_{i}=x_{i+1},\quad Fy_{i}=y_{i+1},\quad(\mbox{here }x_{N}=y_{N}=0);

\displaystyle Ex_{i}=-[i][i+1+n]x_{i-1},\quad Ey_{0}=x_{N-2-n},\quad Ey_{n+1}=x_{N-1},\quad Ey_{i}=[i][n+1+i]y_{i-1}\mbox{ for }i\neq 0,n+1.

(4)

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the module
\sigma^{n}
is the one-dimensional module
\mathbb{C}v
such that
Ev=Fv=0,Kv=v
and
Hv=n\frac{N}{k}v
.

The functor sending
V
to
V\otimes\sigma^{\otimes n}
induces an equivalence between
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha+n\frac{N}{k}}}
. Let
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
be the class of
\frac{N}{k}\mathbb{Z}
, so
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}\cong\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha+p}}
for
p\in\mathcal{P}
. We say that
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
-graded with periodicity group
\mathcal{P}
.
Note that
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{P}$}\right.}\cong\mathbb{C}/\frac{N}{k}\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}
where the second bijection is
\alpha\mapsto q^{\alpha}
.

Notations 3.3
.

Let
\ddot{\mathbb{C}}:=(\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z})\cup\frac{N}{2k}\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

(1)

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
,
V_{\alpha}
is simple if and only if
\alpha\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
. Also
V_{\alpha}
is projective if and only if
\alpha\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
.
Every
S_{n}
are simple. The
P_{n}
are indecomposable, projective but never simple.

(2)

The category
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
is semisimple if and only if
\alpha\notin\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}
in which case the simple objects of
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
are the modules isomorphic to a
V_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\overline{\alpha}
.

(3)

The indecomposable projective modules of
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{0}}
are the modules
V_{0}\otimes\sigma^{mk}
and
P_{n}\otimes\sigma^{mk}
.

(4)

One has
V_{\alpha+n\frac{N}{k}}\cong V_{\alpha}\otimes\sigma^{n}
and
S_{N-1}=V_{0}
.

(5)

For
n\in\{0,\ldots,N-2\}
, one has an exact sequence in
\mathcal{C}
:

0\to S_{n}\xrightarrow{\iota}V_{N-1-n}\xrightarrow{p}S_{N-n-2}\otimes\sigma^{k}\to 0,

where
\iota(e_{i}):=v_{i+N-1-n}
and
p(v_{m}):=e_{m}
for
m\leq N-2-n
and
p(v_{l})=0
for
l>N-2-n
.

(6)

For
n\in\{0,\ldots,N-2\}
, one has an exact sequence in
\mathcal{C}
:

0\to V_{N-1-n}\xrightarrow{\iota}P_{n}\xrightarrow{p}V_{-(N-1-n)}\to 0,

where
\iota(v_{i}):=x_{i}
and
p(x_{i}):=0
,
p(y_{i}):=v_{i}
. Moreover, this exact sequence does not split in
\mathcal{C}
.

(7)

Let
H_{N}:=\{N-1-2n,n=0,\ldots,N-1\}
. If
\alpha+\beta\notin\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}
, then

V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}\cong\oplus_{n\in H_{N}}V_{\alpha+\beta+n}.

(8)

If
\alpha\notin\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}
, one has

V_{\alpha}\otimes S_{n}\cong\oplus_{j\in\{0,\ldots,n\}}V_{\alpha+(n-2j)}.

(9)

For
\alpha\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
then

V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{-\alpha}\cong V_{0}\oplus\oplus_{n=0}^{(N-3)/2}P_{2n}.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward adaptation of the arguments in
[
CGPM15
]
(where
q
has order
2N
instead of
N
) which we include for the reader convenience.

(1)
First note that
Ev_{i}=0
if and only if
[\alpha-i]=0
, i.e. if and only if
\alpha\in i+\frac{N}{2k}\mathbb{Z}
. Such an
i\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
exists if and only if
\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}+\frac{N}{2k}\mathbb{Z}
, i.e. if and only if
\alpha\notin\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
.
So if
\alpha\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
then
Ev_{i}\neq 0
for all
1\leq i\leq N-1
and every vector
v_{n}
, for
0\leq n\leq N-1
, is cyclic. Moreover each axis
\mathbb{C}v_{n}
is an eigenspace of the operator associated to
H
so if
W\subset V_{\alpha}
is a non-null submodule, then
W
contains one vector
v_{n}
and thus all of them since they are cyclic, so
W=V_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}
is simple.
The fact that
S_{n}
is simple is proved similarly.

(2)
Let
\widetilde{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}\subset U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
be the sub-Hopf algebra generated by
E,F,K^{\pm 1/2}
(where
K^{1/2}=q^{H/2}
) and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
be the full subcategory of
\widetilde{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}-\operatorname{Mod}
of finite dimensional representations for which
E^{N}=F^{N}=0
and
K^{N/2}
acts semi-simply. Consider the graduation
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}=\sqcup_{\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{\alpha}}
where
K^{N/2}
acts as
e^{i\pi\alpha}id
on the modules in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{\alpha}}
.
There is an obvious forgetful functor
\mathcal{C}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
sending
V
to
\widetilde{V}
which consists in forgetting how
H
acts. A lift from
\widetilde{V}
to
V
is simply determined by how we lift the action of
K^{1/2}
to an action of
H
and we easily see that if
V,V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
are indecomposable and sent to the same
\widetilde{V}\in\widetilde{C}
then
V^{\prime}\cong V\otimes\sigma^{\otimes n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. So it suffices to prove that
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{\alpha}}
is semisimple when
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}
with simple objects isomorphic to the
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\overline{\alpha}
. It is standard facts, using PBW basis and the filtration associated, that
\widetilde{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
is a domain and that its center
Z
is generated by
K^{\pm N/2}
and the Casimir
C:=EF+\frac{qK^{-1}+q^{-1}K}{(q-q^{-1})^{2}}
with relation
T_{N}(-(q-q^{-1})^{2}C)=K^{N}+K^{-N}
. Moreover,
\widetilde{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
is free over
Z^{0}:=\mathbb{C}[K^{\pm N/2}]
with basis given by:

\{E^{a}K^{b/2}F^{c},0\leq a,b,c\leq N-1\}.

So
\widetilde{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
has rank
N^{3}
over
Z^{0}
and
Z
is an algebraic extension of
Z^{0}
of degree
N
(since
Z\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$Z^{0}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(T_{N}(X)-c)$}\right.}
for
c\in Z^{0}
).
Therefore
\widetilde{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
is almost Azumaya and its PI-degree is
N
(
=\sqrt{\frac{N^{3}}{N}}
). Since
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}
has dimension
N
and is simple for
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}
, its classical shadow belongs to the Azumaya locus of
\widetilde{U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
; this implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{\alpha}}
is semisimple with simple objects the
\widetilde{V}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\overline{\alpha}
which are thus projective (in a semisimple category, every object is projective).

Item
(3)
follows from the fact, proved in
[
KK
]
, that in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{0}}\cong\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}-\operatorname{Mod}
the indecomposable projective objects are the
\widetilde{V}_{0}
and
\widetilde{P}_{n}
. The proof given in
[
KK
]
is a straightforward adaptation of the similar statement proven in
[
Sut94
]
when
q
has order
2N
.

Items
(4)
,
(5)
and
(6)
are straightforward verifications. They imply that
P_{n}
is not simple and indecomposable and that when
\alpha\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
, then
V_{\alpha}
is not simple so its classical shadow does not belong to the Azumaya locus and
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
is not semisimple.

To prove items
(7)
,
(8)
and
(9)
,
consider the symbol morphism
\chi:K^{0}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{C}]
sending a weight module
V
with weight decomposition
V=\oplus_{\alpha\in\mathbb{C}}V(\alpha)
(here
V(\alpha)
is a weight submodule of weight
\alpha
) to its symbol
\chi([V])=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{C}}\dim(V(\alpha))X^{\alpha}
. Since
H
is primitive, the symbol map is a ring morphism. For instance
\chi(V_{\alpha})=\sum_{n\in H_{N}}X^{\alpha+n}
,
\chi(S_{n})=X^{n}+X^{n-2}+\ldots+X^{-n}
,
\chi(\sigma)=X^{\frac{N}{2k}}
and
\chi(P_{m})=\sum_{n\in H_{N}}X^{N-1-n+m}+X^{-(N-1-n+m)}
.
By
(2)
and
(3)
, the restriction of
\chi
to the subcategory of projective objects in
\mathcal{C}
is injective since the
\chi(V_{\alpha}\otimes\sigma^{m})
,
\alpha\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
and
\chi(P_{n}\otimes\sigma^{m})
are linearly independent. Items
(7)
,
(8)
and
(9)

thus follow from the equalities

\chi(V_{\alpha})\chi(V_{\beta})=\sum_{n\in H_{N}}\chi(V_{\alpha+\beta+n})\ \mbox{ and }\ \chi(V_{\alpha})\chi(S_{n})=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\chi(V_{\alpha+(n-2j)})

\chi(V_{\alpha})\chi(V_{-\alpha})=\chi(V_{0})+\sum_{n=0}^{N-3/2}\chi(P_{2n}).

∎

Remark 3.5
.

The categories
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
with
\alpha\in\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{Z}
are pairwise isomorphic (by tensoring with some
\sigma^{\otimes n}
). By definition,
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\overline{0}}
is the category of (weight) representations of the small quantum group
\mathfrak{u}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
whose indecomposables were classified in
[
Sut94
]
when
q
has even order and in
[
KK
]
at odd order. It
contains an infinite (countable) number of indecomposable representations up to isomorphism but the only indecomposable projective representations are isomorphic to the
\widetilde{P_{n}}
and
\widetilde{V_{0}}
(see
[
KK
, Appendix A]
for details). In this paper we will essentially use the semisimple categories
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
for
\alpha\notin\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}
.

3.2.
The ribbon structure and twisted skein algebra

Consider the completions
\widehat{U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}:=\int^{V\in\mathcal{C}}V^{*}\otimes V
and
\widehat{(U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})^{\otimes 2}}:=\int^{V,W\in\mathcal{C}}V^{*}\otimes V\otimes W^{*}\otimes W
. Let
q^{\frac{H\otimes H}{2}}\in\widehat{(U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})^{\otimes 2}}
be the operator defined on
V\otimes W
by

q^{\frac{H\otimes H}{2}}v\otimes w:=A^{wt(v)wt(w)}v\otimes w,

where
v\in V
and
w\in W
are weight vectors of weights
wt(v)
and
wt(w)
respectively (i.e.
Hv=wt(v)v
and
Hw=wt(w)w
). The
R
-matrix
\mathcal{R}\in\widehat{(U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})^{\otimes 2}}
is defined by

\mathcal{R}:=q^{\frac{H\otimes H}{2}}\exp_{q}^{<N}((q-q^{-1})E\otimes F),

where

\exp_{q}^{<N}(X):=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{q^{n(n-1)/2}}{[n]!}X^{n}.

The twist
\Theta_{0}\in\widehat{U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}
is the operator defined by

\Theta_{0}v:=K^{N-1}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{q^{n(n-1)/2}}{[n]!}(q-q^{-1})^{n}S(F^{n})q^{-H^{2}/2}E^{n},

where
q^{-H^{2}/2}
is defined by
q^{-H^{2}/2}v=A^{-wt(v)^{2}}v
for a weight vector
v
. The triple
(U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2},\mathcal{R},\Theta_{0})
forms a topological ribbon Hopf algebra which endows the category
\mathcal{C}
with a structure of ribbon category. The braiding will be denoted by
c_{V,W}:V\otimes W\to W\otimes V
and the twist will be denoted by
\theta_{V}:V\to V
.
The duality in
\mathcal{C}
is described as follows. For
V\in\mathcal{C}
, its dual is
V^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}(V,\mathds{1})
. The left duals are

\overrightarrow{ev}_{V}:V^{*}\otimes V\to\mathds{1},\overrightarrow{ev}_{V}(f\otimes v):=f(v),\quad\overrightarrow{coev}_{V}:\mathds{1}\to V\otimes V^{*},\overrightarrow{coev}_{V}(1):=\sum_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}^{*},

where
(e_{i})
is a basis of
V
and
e_{i}^{*}
its dual basis. The right duals are defined by

\overleftarrow{ev}_{V}:V\otimes V^{*}\to\mathds{1},\overleftarrow{ev}_{V}(v\otimes f):=f(K^{1-N}v),\quad\overleftarrow{coev}_{V}:\mathds{1}\to V^{*}\otimes V,\overleftarrow{coev}_{V}(1)=\sum_{i}e_{i}^{*}\otimes K^{N-1}e_{i}.

In particular
\operatorname{qdim}(S_{n})=[n+1]
,
\operatorname{qdim}(V_{\alpha})=0
and
\operatorname{qdim}(\sigma^{n})=1
.

Lemma 3.6
.

One has
c_{V_{\alpha},\sigma^{n}}\circ c_{\sigma^{n},V_{\alpha}}=e^{2i\pi\alpha n}id_{\sigma^{n}\otimes V_{\alpha}}
and
c_{\sigma^{nk},\sigma^{m}}\circ c_{\sigma^{m},\sigma^{nk}}=id_{\sigma^{nmk}}
.

Proof.

The first equality follows from the fact that
\exp_{q}^{<N}((q-q^{-1})E\otimes F)
acts by the identity operator on both
\sigma^{n}\otimes V_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}\otimes\sigma^{n}
and
q^{H\otimes H/2}
acts by
e^{i\pi n\alpha}
times the identity on both spaces. Similarly, in
\sigma^{m}\otimes\sigma^{nk}
, we have

c_{\sigma^{nk},\sigma^{m}}\circ c_{\sigma^{m},\sigma^{nk}}(v\otimes v)=q^{H\otimes H}v\otimes v=q^{(m\frac{N}{k})(nk(\frac{N}{k}))}v\otimes v=v\otimes v.

∎

Notations 3.7
.

We denote by
\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{C}}
the category of
\mathcal{C}
ribbon graphs and by
F^{RT}:\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{C}
the Reshetikhin-Turaev (ribbon, surjective) functor as defined in
[
Tur10
]
. So objects are tuples
((V_{1},\epsilon_{1}),\ldots,(V_{n},\epsilon_{n}))
with
V_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
and
\epsilon_{i}=\pm
, and morphisms are linear combinations of ribbon tangles read from bottom to top. More precisely, for each
n\geq 0
we consider the set
P_{n}\subset[0,1]^{2}
,
P_{n}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
where
p_{i}=(\frac{i}{n+1},\frac{i}{2})
. A ribbon tangle morphism
T:((V_{1},\epsilon_{1}),\ldots,(V_{n},\epsilon_{n}))\to((W_{1},\nu_{1}),\ldots,(W_{m},\nu_{m}))
is a
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon tangle
T\subset[0,1]^{3}
with
\partial T=(T\cap P_{n}\times\{0\})\cup(T\cap P_{m}\times\{1\})
with compatible colors and orientations
(see
[
Tur10
, Chapter
1
]
for details).

For
V\in\mathcal{C}
a simple object and
f\in\operatorname{End}(V)
, we denote by
\left<f\right>\in\mathbb{C}
the (unique) scalar such that
f=\left<f\right>id_{V}
. For
V,W\in\mathcal{C}
two simple objects, we denote by
S^{\prime}(V,W)\in\mathbb{C}
the scalar

S^{\prime}(V,W)=\left<F^{RT}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{lambda.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)\right>\in\mathbb{C}.

Lemma 3.8
.

One has

S^{\prime}(S_{1},V_{\alpha})=q^{\alpha}+q^{-\alpha},\quad S^{\prime}(S_{1},S_{i})=q^{i+1}+q^{-(i+1)}.

Proof.

The proof is an easy adaptation (left to the reader) of the similar results in
[
CGPM15
]
when
q
has order
2N
.
∎

3.3.
Renormalized trace and link invariants

Recall that a trace on a category
\mathcal{D}
is a ring morphism
\tau:\mathrm{HH}_{0}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{C}
, where
\mathrm{HH}_{0}(\mathcal{D})=\int^{V\in\mathcal{D}}\operatorname{End}(V)
. The set
\mathrm{HH}_{0}(\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}})
identifies canonically with the set
\mathcal{RG}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{S}^{3})
of
\mathcal{D}
-colored ribbon graphs in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, so a trace
\tau
on a ribbon category defines a link invariant

\left<\cdot\right>:\mathcal{RG}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{S}^{3})\cong\mathrm{HH}_{0}(\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}})\xrightarrow{F^{RT}}\mathrm{HH}_{0}(\mathcal{D})\xrightarrow{\tau}\mathbb{C}.

Any ribbon category
\mathcal{D}
has two canonical traces
\operatorname{qtr}^{L,R}
given on
f\in\operatorname{End}(V)
by
\operatorname{qtr}^{L}(f):=\overrightarrow{ev}_{V}\circ(id_{V^{*}}\otimes f)\circ\overleftarrow{coev}_{V}
and
\operatorname{qtr}^{R}(f):=\overleftarrow{ev}_{V}\circ(f\otimes id_{V^{*}})\circ\overrightarrow{coev}_{V}
. On
\mathcal{C}
both traces coincide. However, because
\operatorname{qdim}(V_{\alpha}):=\operatorname{qtr}(id_{V_{\alpha}})=0
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, the associated link invariant vanishes on any ribbon graph with an edge colored by a
V_{\alpha}
. To circumvent this problem, the authors of
[
GPMT09
]
introduced the so-called
modified trace
further studied in
[
GPMV13
]
that we now briefly describe.
Let

B:=\{V_{\alpha},\alpha\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}\}

and
\mathbf{d}:B\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
be the map defined by

\mathbf{d}(V_{\alpha})=\frac{\{\alpha+1-N\}}{\{N(\alpha+1-N)\}}.

Then
(B,\mathbf{d})
is an ambidextrous pair in the sense of
[
GPMV13
]
and defines a trace
\tau:\mathrm{HH}_{0}(\mathcal{P})\to\mathbb{C}
on the full subcategory
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{C}
of projective objects of
\mathcal{C}

such that when
f\in\operatorname{End}(V_{\alpha})
then
\tau(f)=\mathbf{d}(V_{\alpha})\left<f\right>
.
Let
\mathcal{RG}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{S}^{3})
be the set of (isotopy classes of)
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graphs in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
having at least one edge colored by a projective object. The renormalized trace
\tau
defines an invariant

\left<\cdot\right>:\mathcal{RG}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{S}^{3})\to\mathbb{C}

studied in great details in
[
GPM13
]
. When
\Gamma\in\mathcal{RG}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{S}^{3})
is a ribbon graph with one edge
e
colored by a module
V_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
, let
\Gamma(e)
be the
1-1
tangle in
\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{P}}((V_{\alpha},+),(V_{\alpha},+))
obtained by cutting
\Gamma
along
e
. The invariant then satisfies

\left<\Gamma\right>=\mathbf{d}(V_{\alpha})\left<F^{RT}(\Gamma(e))\right>.

The main result of
[
GPM13
]
is the fact that this invariant does not depend on the edge
e
.

3.4.
Multiplicity modules and trivalent graph calculus

For
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
in virtue of Items
(7)
,
(9)
in Lemma
3.4
the spaces
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}\otimes V_{\gamma},\mathds{1})
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathds{1},V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}\otimes V_{\gamma})
have dimension either
0
or
1
. The goal of this subsection is to fix, whenever this dimension is
1
, a non-zero element in each of these spaces. This will permit one to consider trivalent ribbon graphs colored by indecomposable modules without specifying the coupons inserted at each vertex and by only considering the cyclic ordering of the adjacent half-edges (see
[
Tur10
, Section
6.1
]
for an intensive discussion on these conventions).

Trivalent graph calculs

A triple
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
is

and

. In this case, we now define non-zero morphisms

Y_{\gamma}^{\alpha,\beta}:V_{\gamma}\to V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta},\quad Y_{\alpha,\beta}^{\gamma}:V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}\to V_{\gamma},\quad\cap_{\alpha}:V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{-\alpha}\to\mathds{1},\quad\cup_{\alpha}:\mathds{1}\to V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{-\alpha},\quad w_{\alpha}:V_{\alpha}\cong(V_{-\alpha})^{*}

such that, writing

Here and in all the paper, we use the convention that if a strand of a ribbon graph is colored by

.
The existence and explicit formulas for these operators will be derived from the work of Costantino-Murakami in

Notations 3.9
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
such that

. We write

\{n\}:=q^{n}-q^{-n};\quad\{n\}!:=\{n\}\{n-1\}\ldots\{1\};\{\alpha,\beta\}:=\{\alpha\}\{\alpha-1\}\ldots\{\beta+1\};\quad\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\alpha\\
\beta\end{array}\right]:=\frac{\{\alpha,\beta\}}{\{\alpha-\beta\}!}.

When

. Let

\kappa_{n}^{\alpha}:=q^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\left\{\alpha+N-1,\alpha+N-1-n\right\},\quad w_{\alpha}^{n}:=\kappa^{\alpha}_{n}\kappa^{-\alpha}_{N-1-n}q^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}(N-1)-n-\frac{1}{2}}.

Now suppose that
\alpha+\beta-\gamma\in H_{N}
and let

\gamma+N-1-n\end{array}\right]^{-1}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\gamma+N-1\\
\alpha+\beta+\gamma+\frac{N-1}{2}\end{array}\right]\\

We eventually set

D_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}:=\frac{\kappa_{n}^{\gamma}}{\kappa_{i}^{\alpha}\kappa_{j}^{\beta}}C_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\quad\mbox{ and }E_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}:=\frac{\kappa_{i}^{\alpha}\kappa_{j}^{\beta}}{\kappa_{n}^{\gamma}}C_{N-1-j,N-1-i,N-1-n}^{-\beta,-\alpha,-\gamma}.

Definition 3.10
.

For

•

w_{\alpha}(v_{i}):=w_{\alpha}^{i}(v_{N-1-i})^{*};

•

\cap_{\alpha}(v_{i}\otimes v_{j}):=\delta_{i+j,N-1}w_{\alpha}^{i};\quad\cup_{\alpha}(1):=\sum_{i}\left(w_{-\alpha}^{N-1-i}\right)^{-1}v_{i}\otimes v_{N-1-i};

•

Y_{\gamma}^{\alpha,\beta}(v_{n})=\sum_{i,j}D_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}v_{i}\otimes v_{j};\quad Y_{\alpha,\beta}^{\gamma}(v_{i}\otimes v_{j})=\sum_{n}E_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}v_{n}.

and
