We can now describe our conventions for trivalent graph calculus. Let
G\subset\mathbb{S}^{3}
be a ribbon trivalent graph. A
coloring
of
G
is a chain
c\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{C})
, where
G
is seen as a CW complex. Equivalently,
c
is described by a choice of orientation
\mathfrak{o}
to the edges
{E}
of
G
together with a map
c:E\to\mathbb{C}
which we identify with the chain
\sum_{e\in E}c(e)e
. A coloring is
strictly admissible
if
(i)

c(e)\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
and
(ii)
the map
\partial c:V(G)\to\mathbb{C}
satisfies
\partial c(v)\in H_{N}=\{-(N-1),\ldots,N-3,N-1\}
for all vertices
v\in V(G)
. For
(G,c)
a trivalent graph with strictly admissible coloring, we define the invariant

\left<G,c\right>\in\mathbb{C}

obtained by considering a standard planar projection of
G
with normal double crossings, coloring an oriented edge
e
by
V_{c(e)}
,
inserting the evaluations and coevaluations
\cup_{\alpha}
and
\cap_{\alpha}
and considering the renormalized invariant of the obtained
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graph. The strict admissibility condition ensures that for three adjacent edges oriented towards a common vertex and cyclically ordered by the ribbon graph, the associated highest weight modules
V_{\alpha}
,
V_{\beta}
,
V_{\gamma}
are such that
(\alpha,\beta,-\gamma)
is admissible.

The inclusion of the modules
\sigma^{n}

Definition 3.12
.

(Admissible coloring)
A coloring
c\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{C})
is said
admissible
if
(i)

c(e)\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
for all oriented edge
e
and
(ii)

\partial c(v)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all vertices
v\in V(G)
.

We now extend our trivalent graph calculus in order to include invariants
\left<G,c\right>
of (non-necessarily strictly) admissible colorings using the modules
\sigma^{n}
. We will use the convention that a dotted edge colored by

represents an edge colored by
\sigma^{n}
, i.e.

. Using the isomorphism
\sigma^{n_{1}}\otimes\sigma^{n_{2}}\cong\sigma^{n_{1}+n_{2}}

, the isomorphisms
V_{\alpha}\otimes\sigma^{n}\cong V_{\alpha+n\frac{N}{k}}

to

and the isomorphisms
\sigma^{n}\otimes V_{\alpha}\cong V_{\alpha+n\frac{N}{k}}
sending

to
v_{i}

\sigma^{n}
.

Suppose that
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
are such that
\alpha+\beta+\gamma\in\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{Z}
. Then there exists a unique

.
In this case, the triple

Define the multiplicity modules

and

by

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{HnDef.eps}}}}}}}$}.

The above equalities are ensured by the fact that the matrix coefficients
w_{\alpha}^{i}
,

and

only depend on the classes of

(G,c)

then there exists a unique
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\partial c(v)\in nN+H_{N}
and we want to introduce a module

that is, we want to replace

by

since
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
is

(1)

\sigma^{nk}
is located in the cyclic order of the half-edges adjacent to

by specifying a ciliated structure on
G
(in

, the edge colored by
\sigma^{nk}
is between the edge colored by

and the edge colored by

) and
(2)
how the edges colored by
\sigma^{n}
are connected. Recall that in a ribbon graph the half-edges adjacent to a vertex

are cyclically ordered and that a

is the data of a lift of this cyclic ordering to a total ordering (for instance by drawing a cilium between the last and the first half-edge).

Definition 3.13
.

(
\sigma
decoration) A
\sigma
-
decoration
on a trivalent ribbon graph
G\subset S^{3}
is the data of
(1)
a ciliated structure on
G
,
(2)
a point
v_{\infty}\in S^{3}\setminus G
and
(3)
for each vertex
v
, a smooth path
p_{v}:[0,1]\to S^{3}\setminus G\cup\{v\}
with
p_{v}(0)=v
and
p_{v}(1)=v_{\infty}
.

When
G
is equipped with a
\sigma
-decoration and
c
is an admissible coloring, we define a
\mathcal{C}
-colored graph
G(c)\subset S^{3}
by first orienting the edges of
G
arbitrary and coloring each edge
e
by
V_{c(e)}
. Then for each vertex
v
, with
\partial c(v)\in H_{N}+Nn_{v}
where
n_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}
, color the path
p_{v}
with
\sigma^{n_{v}k}
(with arbitrary framing), replace
v
by a coupon colored by
H^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}_{n_{v}}
(if the three adjacent edges are colored by
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
in counter-clockwise order) and eventually replace
v_{\infty}
by a coupon colored by the morphism
\bigotimes_{v\in V(G)}\sigma^{kn_{v}}\to\mathds{1}
sending
\bigotimes v
to
1
(well-defined since
\sum_{v}n_{v}=0
).

We eventually define

\left<G,c\right>\in\mathbb{C}

as the invariant of the graph
G(c)
.

For instance for the theta graph with
\sigma
decoration

and colored by
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
with
\alpha+\beta+\gamma\in nN+H_{N}
, we set

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{ExConvention.eps}}}}}}}$}.

When
c
is an admissible coloring of
G
, since
\partial c\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(G;\mathbb{Z})
, then
c
induces a class
\overline{c}\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(G;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
to which we associate a class
\omega_{\overline{c}}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(S^{3}\setminus G;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
as follows. The inclusion
G\subset S^{3}
induces a long exact sequence in homology:

0=\mathrm{H}_{2}(S^{3})\to\mathrm{H}_{2}(S^{3},G)\xrightarrow[\cong]{\partial}\mathrm{H}_{1}(G)\to\mathrm{H}_{1}(S^{3})=0

where homology is taken with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
. By excision, we have
\mathrm{H}_{2}(S^{3},G)\cong\mathrm{H}_{2}(S^{3}\setminus G)
so we obtain an isomorphism
\delta_{1}:\mathrm{H}_{1}(G)\xrightarrow{\partial^{-1}}\mathrm{H}_{2}(S^{3},G)\cong\mathrm{H}_{2}(S^{3}\setminus G)
. Let
\delta_{2}:\mathrm{H}_{2}(S^{3}\setminus G)\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(S^{3}\setminus G)
the Poincaré duality morphism sending a class
[S]
to the cocycle
[S]\cap\bullet
.

Definition 3.14
.

(Shadow of a coloring) The
shadow

\omega_{\overline{c}}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(S^{3}\setminus G;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
of an admissible coloring
c
of
G\subset S^{3}
is the image of
\overline{c}
by the composition
\mathrm{H}_{1}(G;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\delta_{1}}\mathrm{H}_{2}(S^{3}\setminus G;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\delta_{2}}\mathrm{H}^{1}(S^{3}\setminus G;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}).

Unfolding the definition,
\omega_{\overline{c}}
has the following down-to-earth description. For
\gamma\subset S^{3}\setminus G
an oriented simple closed curve, let
D_{\gamma}\subset S^{3}
be an oriented immersed disc positively bounded by
\gamma
which intersects
G
transversally. Orient the edges of
G
arbitrarily. Then

\omega_{\overline{c}}([\gamma])=\sum_{v\in D_{\gamma}\cap G}\epsilon(v)\overline{c}(v),

where
\epsilon(v)\in\{-1,+1\}
is the intersection sign and
\overline{c}(v)\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
is the image by
\overline{c}
of the oriented edge of
G
which intersects
D_{\gamma}
at
v
.

Suppose that
G
is
\sigma
-decorated and let
\widehat{G}\subset S^{3}
be the ribbon graph obtained from
G
by adding the vertex
v_{\infty}
and the edges
p_{v}
with arbitrary framing. Define an embedding
\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Z}_{1}(\widehat{G};\mathbb{Z})=\mathrm{H}_{1}(\widehat{G};\mathbb{Z})
by sending
c_{0}\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})
such that
\partial c_{0}=\sum_{v\in V(G)}n_{v}v
to
\widehat{c_{0}}\in\mathrm{Z}_{1}(\widehat{G};\mathbb{Z})
defined by
\widehat{c}_{0}:=c_{0}+\sum_{v}n_{v}p_{v}
(here
p_{v}
is oriented from
v
to
v_{\infty}
). Using the framing of
G
, we can push an edge of
\widehat{G}
inside
S^{3}\setminus G
thus defining a morphism
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\widehat{G};\mathbb{Z})\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(S^{3}\setminus G;\mathbb{Z})
by which we define
\omega_{\overline{c}}(\widehat{c_{0}})\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 3.15
.

If
c
is an admissible coloring and
c_{0}\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})
, then

\left<G,c+\frac{N}{k}c_{0}\right>=\left<G,c\right>e^{2i\pi\omega_{\overline{c}}(\widehat{c_{0}})}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.6
, we have the skein relation
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{CrossSigma1.eps}}}}}}}$}=e^{2i\pi\alpha n}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{CrossSigma2.eps}}}}}}}$}
which, together with the fact that
\operatorname{qdim}(\sigma)=1
, implies the relation:

(7)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{skein_relation_sigma.eps}}}}}}}$}.

An
inner curve
in the ribbon graph
\widehat{G}
is a simple closed curve defined by a cycle
v_{0}\xrightarrow{e_{0}}v_{1}\xrightarrow{e_{1}}v_{2}\xrightarrow{e_{2}}\ldots\xrightarrow{e_{n-1}}v_{n}\xrightarrow{e_{n}}v_{0}
(
v_{i}
are vertices,
e_{i}
are oriented edges from
v_{i}
to
v_{i+1}
) such that the half-edges of
e_{i}
and
e_{i-1}
adjacent to
v_{i}
are consecutive in the cyclic ordering at
v_{i}
(indexes are modulo
n+1
).
Note that if we thickened the ribbon graph
\widehat{G}
to a surface
S
then the inner curves are isotopic to the boundary components of
S
so the homology classes
[\gamma]

of inner curves
\gamma
generate
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\widehat{G};\mathbb{Z})
. We can suppose that
\widehat{c_{0}}=[\gamma]
for such an inner curve
\gamma
.

As illustrated in Figure
1
,
(G,c+\frac{N}{k}c_{0})
is skein equivalent to the union of
(G,c)
with the curve
\gamma
(pushed inside
S^{3}\setminus G
) colored by
\sigma
.
Using the skein relation

from Lemma
3.6
we see that
(\gamma,\sigma)
is transparent with respect to the edges
p_{v}
of
\widehat{G}
.
We conclude using the previous skein relation (
7
).

Figure 1.
On the top: a ribbon graph
G\subset S^{3}
and a
\sigma
decoration. On the bottom: two illustrations of the equality
\left<G,c+\frac{N}{k}c_{0}\right>=\left<G,c\right>e^{2i\pi\omega_{\overline{c}}(\widehat{c_{0}})}
when
c_{0}=ne_{1}+ne_{2}
is a cycle so
\widehat{c_{0}}=c_{0}
and when
c_{0}=ne_{3}
(in this case
\widehat{c_{0}}
is contractible).

∎

The inclusion of the standard module
S_{1}

We now extend our trivalent graph calculus to include trivalent graphs with edges colored by the standard representations
S_{1}
. We will color these edges in blue.
Recall from Item
(8)
in Lemma
3.4
that we have morphisms
\iota:S_{1}\hookrightarrow V_{N-2}
and
p:V_{N-2}\to S_{1}
defined by
\iota(e_{n}):=v_{N-2+n}
,
p(v_{N-2+n})=e_{n}
for
n=0,1
and
p(v_{i})=0
for
i=0,\ldots,N-3
(so
p\circ i=id
). We extend our trivalent graph calculus using the conventions:

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{YDef3.eps}}}}}}}$}.

3.5.
6j symbols

Definition 3.16
.

For
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)

0
-admissible and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{-1,+1\}
, the
6j
-symbol
6S(\alpha,\beta,\gamma;\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})\in\mathbb{C}
is defined by the skein relation

The following proposition is the key to prove Theorem
1.3
. We postpone its (technical) proof to Appendix
B
.

Proposition 3.17
.

There exists a non-zero rational fraction
R(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})\in\mathbb{Q}(A)(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})
(which depends on
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}
) such that

6S(\alpha,\beta,\gamma;\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=R(q^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},q^{\frac{\beta}{2}},q^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}).

4.
Skein representations coming from non-semisimple TQFTs

4.1.
The spaces
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma,\omega)

The link invariants described in Section
3.3
were extended in
[
CGP14
]
to invariants of triples
(M,\Gamma,\omega)
where
M
is a
3
-manifold equipped with an embedded
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graph
\Gamma\subset M
with at least one edge colored by a projective module and a cohomology class
\omega\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(M\setminus\Gamma;\mathbb{C}/\frac{N}{2k}\mathbb{Z})
compatible with
\Gamma
. These invariants were proved in
[
BCGP16
]
to be part of a more elaborate construction named
extended non-semisimple TQFT
where the authors considered roots of unity of even order. The construction of non-semisimple TQFTs was latter extended to arbitrary modular relative categories in
[
DR22
]
and it follows from
[
DRGPM20
, Theorem
1.3
]
that

\mathcal{C}
is a relative
\mathbb{C}
-modular category in the sense of
[
DR22
]
so it defines an extended TQFT
\mathbb{V}
. As a byproduct of this elaborated construction, to each surface
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g}
with
g\geq 2
endowed with a cohomology class
\omega\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})\setminus\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma;\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z})
, we associate a
N^{3g-3}
dimensional representation

r_{\omega}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)\to\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma,\omega)).

We propose below an alternative construction of this space and of its skein module structure, similar to the construction of the Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev representations described in Section
2.5
.
This will make the paper (almost) self-contained since we will almost never rely on the results in
[
BCGP16
]
with the exception of Lemma
4.9
.
The main difference with the WRT case is the fact that we use a different skein module for the handlebody. The Kauffman-bracket skein modules are particular cases of the following more general class of skein modules.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a ribbon category and
M
a compact oriented
3
-manifold. Let
F^{RT}:\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}\to\mathcal{D}
be the Reshetikhin-Turaev functor where the morphisms in
\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
are
\mathcal{D}
-colored standard tangles in the cube
C=[0,1]^{3}
with incoming and outgoing endpoints equal to some prescribed points in the sets
\partial_{in}C=[0,1]^{2}\times\{0\}
and
\partial_{out}C=[0,1]^{2}\times\{1\}
.
Let
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{RG}_{\mathcal{D}}(M)]
be the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space freely spanned by
\mathcal{D}
-colored ribbon graphs in
M
. Consider an oriented embedding
\phi:C\hookrightarrow M
. A ribbon graph
\Gamma\subset M
is
\phi
-adapted if it intersects
\phi(\partial C)
transversally along
\phi(\partial_{in}C)\cup\phi(\partial_{out}C)
so that the restriction
\mathchoice{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\phi(C)}}{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,\phi(C)}}{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.20056pt}}_{\,\phi(C)}}{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.78612pt}}_{\,\phi(C)}}
defines a morphism
\mathchoice{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\phi}}{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\phi}}{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=1.72832pt}}_{\,\phi}}{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.31387pt}}_{\,\phi}}:V_{in}(\Gamma)\to V_{out}(\Gamma)
in
\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
(so, using Notations
3.7
,
\phi^{-1}(\Gamma)\cap\partial_{in}=P_{n}\times\{0\}
and
\phi^{-1}(\Gamma)\cap\partial_{out}=P_{m}\times\{1\}
)
. A linear combination

X=\sum_{i}x_{i}\Gamma_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{RG}_{\mathcal{D}}(M)]
is
a skein relation with support in

\phi
if

(1)

every
\Gamma_{i}
are
\phi
adapted and such that
V_{in}(\Gamma_{i})
and
V_{out}(\Gamma_{i})
do not depend on
i
;

(2)

the restriction of the
\Gamma_{i}
to
M\setminus\phi(C)
are pairwise equal;

(3)

one has
\sum_{i}x_{i}F^{RT}\left(\mathchoice{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\phi}}{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\phi}}{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=1.72832pt}}_{\,\phi}}{{\Gamma\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.31387pt}}_{\,\phi}}\right)=0
.

Definition 4.1
.

(Skein module) The
skein module

\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(M)
is the quotient of the space
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{RG}_{\mathcal{D}}(M)]
by the subspace spanned by skein relations.

For instance, the Kauffman-bracket skein module is the skein module associated to the Temperley-Lieb category
TL
. As we shall prove in the next subsection, it is closely related to the skein module associated to the full subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{small}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\overline{0}}
generated by the representations
(S_{1}^{\pm 1})^{\otimes n}
where
S_{1}^{+1}=S_{1}
and
S_{1}^{-1}=(S_{1})^{*}
. By construction,
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(M)
is functorial in both the variables
\mathcal{D}
and
M
in the sense that:

(1)

an oriented embedding
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
induces a linear map
f_{*}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(M_{1})\to\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(M_{2})
defined by
f_{*}([\Gamma])=[f(\Gamma)]
, so
f_{*}
only depends on the isotopy class of
f
;

(2)

a ribbon functor
F:\mathcal{D}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}_{2}
induces a linear map
F_{*}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}_{1}}(M)\to\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}_{2}}(M)
by acting by
F
on the colors and coupons of ribbon graphs.

More precisely, if
\mathcal{M}
is the category with objects the compact oriented
3
-manifolds and morphisms the oriented embeddings, then
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{M}\to\operatorname{Vect}
is a functor and
F_{*}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}_{1}}\to\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
is a natural morphism.
In particular
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma):=\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma\times[0,1])
has an algebra structure and if
\partial M=\Sigma
then
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(M)
is a
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma)
-module (since
\Sigma\times[0,1]
is an algebra object in
\mathcal{M}
and
M
a module object over it).

Moreover, the inclusion functor
\mathcal{C}^{small}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\overline{0}}
induces an algebra morphism
\iota:\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}^{small}}(\Sigma)\to\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}_{\overline{0}}}(\Sigma)
and if
\partial M=\Sigma
then
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(M)
is a
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}^{small}}(\Sigma)
-module. For now on, we will denote by
\mathcal{S}_{A}^{QG}
the functor
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}^{small}}
for simplicity.

Let us now suppose that the ribbon category
\mathcal{D}
is graded by an abelian group
\mathcal{A}
, i.e. that
\mathcal{D}=\cup_{g\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{D}_{g}
with
\mathcal{D}_{g_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{g_{2}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{g_{1}+g_{2}}
. The category
\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
is then
\mathcal{A}
-graded as well where the object
((V_{1},\epsilon),\ldots,(V_{n},\epsilon_{n}))\in\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
belong to the graded part associated to
g=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}g_{i}
where
V_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{g_{i}}
and
\epsilon_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
. By construction, the Reshetikhin-Turaev functor
F^{RT}:\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}\to\mathcal{D}
preserves the grading (has degree
0
).

Let
G\subset\mathbb{S}^{3}
be an oriented ribbon trivalent graph embedded in the sphere, let
V
and
E
be its sets of vertices and edges respectively and let
H=N(G)
be a tubular neighborhood, so
H
is a handlebody with boundary
\Sigma:=\partial H
. For each edge
e\in E
, one associates a properly embedded oriented disc
D_{e}\subset H
with
\gamma_{e}:=\partial D_{e}\subset\Sigma
chosen such that
D_{e}
intersects
G
in a single point
p_{e}
in the center of
D_{e}
. The framing of
G
at
p_{e}
defines an oriented axis of
D_{e}
passing by
p_{e}
. An embedded ribbon graph
\Gamma\subset H
is in good position if it intersects each
D_{e}
transversally along the oriented axis with framing given by the oriented axis. So the intersection
\Gamma\cap D_{e}
defines an element
X_{\Gamma,e}\in\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
and we denote by
g_{e}(\Gamma)\in A
its grading. Any ribbon graph can be isotoped such that it is in good position and two isotopic ribbon graphs in good position define the same graded elements
g_{e}(\Gamma)
(note that the fact that
A
is abelian is important here). Note also that for
v\in V
corresponding to a pair of pants
P_{v}
with adjacent edges
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
(not necessarily pairwise distinct, so it corresponds rather to half-edges), writing
\epsilon_{i}=+1
if
e_{i}
is incoming to
v
and
\epsilon_{i}=-1
else, then
\epsilon_{1}g_{e_{1}}(\Gamma)+\epsilon_{2}g_{e_{2}}(\Gamma)+\epsilon_{3}g_{e_{3}}(\Gamma)=0
. Therefore the
g_{e}(\Gamma)
define a cycle
g(\gamma)\in\mathrm{Z}^{1}(G;\mathcal{A})\cong\mathrm{H}_{1}(H;\mathcal{A})
.
Therefore, the skein module
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(H)
is
\mathrm{H}^{1}(H;\mathcal{A})
-graded. For
\omega_{H}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(H;\mathcal{A})
, we denote by
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(H,\omega_{H})
the
\omega_{H}
graded part.

Now consider the two trivalent ribbon graphs
G_{g},G^{\prime}_{g}\subset\mathbb{S}^{3}
of Figure
2
and choose the tubular neighborhoods
H_{g}=N(G_{g})
and
H_{g}^{\prime}=N(G_{g}^{\prime})
such that
H_{g}\cap H_{g}^{\prime}=\partial H_{g}=\partial H_{g}^{\prime}=:\Sigma_{g}
has genus
g
.
Note that the graphs have been chosen such that for any edge
e
of either
G_{g}
or
G^{\prime}_{g}
, then the curve
\gamma_{e}\subset\Sigma_{g}
is non-separating.
Let
\omega\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g};\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})\setminus\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g};\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z})
. By pulling back
\omega
along the inclusion maps
\Sigma_{g}\subset H_{g}
and
\Sigma_{g}\subset H^{\prime}_{g}
, we obtain two classes
\omega_{H_{g}}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(H_{g};\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
and
\omega_{H^{\prime}_{g}}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(H^{\prime}_{g};\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
and the pair
(\omega_{H_{g}},\omega_{H_{g}^{\prime}})
determines
\omega
. Consider the Hopf pairing

\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)^{H}_{\omega}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H^{\prime}_{g},\omega_{H^{\prime}_{g}})\otimes\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H_{g},\omega_{H_{g}})\to\mathbb{C},\quad(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)^{H}_{\omega}:=\left<\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\Gamma\right>,

where
\left<\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\Gamma\right>
is the link invariant of Section
3.3
defined using the renormalized trace of the ribbon graph
\Gamma\cup\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{RG}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{S}^{3})
(the condition
\omega\notin\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g};\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z})
implies that at least one edge of
\Gamma\cup\Gamma^{\prime}
is colored by a projective module).

Figure 2.
On the left: a trivalent oriented graph
G
, an enumeration
e_{1},\ldots,e_{3g-3}
of its edges and some curves
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{g}
. On the right: two copies
G
and
G^{\prime}
of the graph embedded in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
which define a Heegaard splitting of the sphere.

Definition 4.2
.

The space
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g};\omega)
is defined as the quotient

\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g};\omega):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H_{g},\omega_{H_{g}})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{Rker}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{\omega}^{H}$}\right.}.

We also define the space

\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{g};\omega):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H^{\prime}_{g},\omega_{H^{\prime}_{g}})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{Lker}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{\omega}^{H}$}\right.}

so the Hopf pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)^{H}:\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)\otimes\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)\to\mathbb{C}
is non-degenerate.

The skein module
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma_{g})
naturally acts on the left on
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H_{g})

and on the right on
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H^{\prime}_{g})

and the Hopf pairing is invariant for this action (i.e.
(x,z\cdot y)^{H}=(x\cdot z,y)^{H}
). The action of the submodule
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}_{\overline{0}}}(\Sigma_{g})\subset\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma_{g})
preserves the graded subspaces
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H_{g},\omega_{H_{g}})
and
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H^{\prime}_{g},\omega_{H_{g}^{\prime}})
, so we obtain a left action of
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}_{\overline{0}}}(\Sigma_{g})
on
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
by quotient.
(and a right action on
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
).

Definition 4.3
.

The representation
\overline{r}_{\omega}:\mathcal{S}_{A}^{QG}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega))
is defined as the composition

\overline{r}_{\omega}:\mathcal{S}_{A}^{QG}(\Sigma_{g})\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}_{\overline{0}}}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)),

where
\mathcal{S}_{A}^{QG}
denotes the functor
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}^{small}}
.

4.2.
Relating Kauffman-bracket and quantum groups skein modules

We now define an algebra morphism
f:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{S}_{A}^{QG}(\Sigma)
. In this subsection, we do not assume
\Sigma
to be closed, nor do we assume that
A
is a root of unity (the results work over the ring
\mathbb{Z}[A^{\pm 1}]
for instance). This task is not trivial due to the fact that the Temperley-Lieb category
TL
defining the Kauffman-bracket skein algebra is not ribbon equivalent to
\mathcal{C}^{small}
. As detailed in
[
Tin17
]
, there is an equivalence of categories
\mathrm{Cauchy}(TL)\to\mathcal{C}^{small}
between the Cauchy closure of
TL
and
\mathcal{C}^{small}
which is a braided functor preserving the pivotal structure but which does not preserve the twist. Said differently,
\mathrm{Cauchy}(TL)
is equivalent to
\mathcal{C}^{small}
with a ”modified twist” responsible for the fact that the class of an unknot is sent to
-(q+q^{-1})
in
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
whereas the q-dimension of
S_{1}
is
\operatorname{qdim}(S_{1})=+(q+q^{-1})
.

First note that since
S_{1}
is isomorphic to its dual
(S_{1})^{*}
, the class
[L(S_{1})]\in\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}^{small}}(\Sigma)
of an oriented framed link
L\subset\Sigma\times[0,1]
with all its components colored by
S_{1}
, does not depend on the orientations of the components of
L
, so
[L(S_{1})]
is well-defined for unoriented framed links
L
. We use a similar notation
\gamma(S_{1})
for
\gamma
a multicurve.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
S
be a spin structure on
\Sigma
with Johnson quadratic form
w_{S}
.
There is an algebra morphism
f:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{S}_{A}^{QG}(\Sigma)
sending the class
[\gamma]
of a multicurve to
(-1)^{|\gamma|+w_{S}([\gamma])}[\gamma(S_{1})]
, where
|\gamma|
is the number of components of
\gamma
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
M
be a
3
-manifold. We have a linear morphism
h:\mathcal{S}_{-A}(M)\to\mathcal{S}_{A}^{QG}(M)
defined by
h([L])=(-1)^{|L|}[L(S_{1})]
for any framed link
L\subset M
. Moreover
h
is an algebra morphism when
M
is a thickened surface.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathcal{S}_{-A}(M)
is defined as the quotient of the module
\mathbb{C}[L\subset M]
freely generated by isotopy classes of framed links by a submodule of skein relations.
Let
h:\mathbb{C}[L\subset M]\to\mathcal{S}^{QG}_{A}(M)
be the linear morphism defined by
h([L]):=(-1)^{|L|}[L(S_{1})]
and let us prove that the images
h([L])
satisfy the Kauffman bracket skein relations of
\mathcal{S}_{-A}(M)
. For
L\subset M
and
U\subset M
an unknot bounding a disc not intersecting
L
, since
\operatorname{qdim}(S_{1})=q+q^{-1}
, we find

h([L\cup U])=(-1)^{|L|+1}[L(S_{1})]\operatorname{qdim}(S_{1})=(-q-q^{-1})(-1)^{|L|}[L(S_{1})]=-(q+q^{-1})h([L]).

For the other skein relation, let
f:S_{1}\xrightarrow{\cong}(S_{1})^{*}
be the equivariant isomorphism defined by
f(e_{0}):=e_{1}^{*}
and
f(e_{1}):=-qe_{0}^{*}
. We claim that in
\mathcal{S}^{QG}(\Sigma)
the following skein relation is satisfied:

(8)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{CrossingDown.eps}}}}}}}$}=A\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{arrowDown.eps}}}}}}}$}+A^{-1}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{Cupcap.eps}}}}}}}$}

The claim is well-known (see e.g.
[
Oht02
, Appendix
H.1
]
) and follows from the following computation. First we compute:

\overrightarrow{ev}_{S_{1}}(f\otimes id):e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{, if }i=j,\\
1&\mbox{, if }(i,j)=(0,1),\\
-q&\mbox{, if }(i,j)=(1,0).\end{array}\right.\quad(id\otimes f^{-1})\overrightarrow{coev}_{S_{1}}:1\mapsto-q^{-1}e_{0}\otimes e_{1}+e_{1}\otimes e_{0}.

From which we deduce that in the ordered basis
B=(e_{0}\otimes e_{0},e_{0}\otimes e_{1},e_{1}\otimes e_{0},e_{1}\otimes e_{1})
, we have the matrices

\mathcal{U}:=F^{RT}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{Cupcap.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0\\
0&-q^{-1}&1&0\\
0&1&-q&0\\
0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathscr{R}:=F^{RT}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{crossingDown.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}q^{1/2}&0&0&0\\
0&0&q^{-1/2}&0\\
0&q^{-1/2}&q^{1/2}-q^{-1/2}&0\\
0&0&0&q^{1/2}\end{pmatrix}

The skein relation (
8
) now follows from the equality
\mathscr{R}=q^{1/2}\mathds{1}_{4}+q^{-1/2}\mathcal{U}.

Let
L_{0},L_{1},L_{2}\subset M
be three framed links which are equal outside a ball
B\subset M
inside which they look like
,

and

respectively. Let us prove that
h(L_{0}+AL_{1}+A^{-1}L_{2})=0
.

Case 1:
Suppose that the two brands in the crossing
belong to two distinct components, i.e. that
L_{0}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{8pp.eps}}}}}}}$}
. In this case
|L_{1}|=|L_{2}|=|L_{0}|+1
so

h([L_{0}])=(-1)^{|L_{0}|}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{7_oriented.eps}}}}}}}$}=(-A)(-1)^{|L_{1}|}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{72.eps}}}}}}}$}-A^{-1}(-1)^{|L_{2}|}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{74.eps}}}}}}}$}\\
=(-A)(-1)^{|L_{1}|}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{72.eps}}}}}}}$}-A^{-1}(-1)^{|L_{2}|}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{73.eps}}}}}}}$}=-Ah([L_{1}])-A^{-1}h([L_{2}]),

where we pushed the box colored by
f^{-1}
along the strand and canceled it with the box colored by
f
using a skein relation while passing from the first to the second line.

Case 2:
If the two brands in the crossing
belong to the same component, i.e. if
L_{0}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{8.eps}}}}}}}$}
or
L_{0}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{8p.eps}}}}}}}$}
we get

-Ah\left(\hbox to17.47pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.7359pt\lower-8.7359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

Where we used the fact that
\theta_{S_{1}}=+A^{3}id_{S_{1}}
in
\mathcal{C}
in the last equality. So we get again the equality
h([L_{0}]+A[L_{1}]+A^{-1}[L_{2}])=0
. This concludes the proof.

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

The theorem follows from Propositions
2.4
and
4.5
.
∎

Definition 4.6
.

The representation
r_{\omega}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega))
is the composition
r_{\omega}=\overline{r}_{\omega}\circ f
where
\overline{r}_{\omega}
is defined in Definition
4.3
.

4.3.
Bases and classical shadows

Given
c\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{C})
and
c^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G^{\prime};\mathbb{C})
admissible, we want to consider
\mathcal{C}
-colored graphs
(G,c)\subset H_{g}
and
(G^{\prime},c^{\prime})\subset H^{\prime}_{g}
so that the vector
[G,c]\in\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
is well-defined and so is the pairing
\left<[G,c],[G^{\prime},c^{\prime}]\right>=\left<(G\cup G^{\prime},c\cup c^{\prime})\right>
(recall that
\left<(G\cup G^{\prime},c\cup c^{\prime})\right>
is the CGP invariants of the colored graph
(G\cup G^{\prime},c\cup c^{\prime})
) . Recall from Section
3.3
that to define such
\mathcal{C}
-colored graphs, we need to specify a
\sigma
-decoration. We thus fix a
\sigma
-decoration of
G\cup G^{\prime}
such that
(1)

v_{\infty}\in\Sigma_{g}\subset S^{3}\setminus(G\cup G^{\prime})
and
(2)
for
v\in V(G)
(resp.
v^{\prime}\in V(G^{\prime})
) the path
p_{v}:v\to v_{\infty}
(resp.
p_{v^{\prime}}
) lies inside
H_{g}
(resp. inside
H_{g}^{\prime}
). This condition permits one to define the
\mathcal{C}
-colored graphs
(G,c)\subset H_{g}
,
(G^{\prime},c^{\prime})\subset H_{g}^{\prime}
and
(G\cup G^{\prime},c\cup c^{\prime})\subset S^{3}
.

We now suppose that
\omega
belongs to the open dense subset

\mathcal{U}=\{\omega\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g};\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})\mbox{ such that }\omega(\gamma_{e})\notin\left(\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}\right)/\mathbb{Z},\forall e\in E(G)\cup E(G^{\prime})\}.

Recall that the embedded graphs
G\cup G^{\prime}\subset S^{3}
have been chosen in Figure
2
such that for any edge
e\in E(G)\cup E(G^{\prime})
then
\gamma_{e}\subset\Sigma_{g}
is non-separating. This ensures that indeed
\mathcal{U}
is non empty.

For
c\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{C})
such that
\overline{c(e)}=\omega(\gamma_{e})
for all edge
e\in E(G)
, we say that
c
is
\omega
-compatible
and denote by
[G,c]\in\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
the corresponding vector. Similarly, for
c^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{C})
such that
\overline{c^{\prime}(e)}=\omega(\gamma_{e}^{\prime})
for all
e\in E(G^{\prime})
,
c^{\prime}
is said
\omega
-compatible
and we define
[G^{\prime},c^{\prime}]\in\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
.

Lemma 4.7
.

The space
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
is spanned by the vectors
[G,c]
for
c

\omega
-compatible.
Similarly,
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
is spanned by vectors
[G^{\prime},c^{\prime}]
with
c^{\prime}

\omega
-compatible.

Proof.

We prove the assertion concerning
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
, the second assertion is proved similarly.
Let
[\Gamma]\in\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
be the class of a
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon graph
\Gamma\subset H_{g}
which induces the class
\omega
. We suppose that
\Gamma
is isotoped such that it intersects each disc
D_{e}
transversally along the axis given by the framing. As before, the intersection
\Gamma\cap D_{e}
defines an element
X_{\Gamma,e}\in\operatorname{Rib}_{\mathcal{C}}
sent to an element of
\mathcal{C}_{\omega(e)}
by the Reshetikhin-Turaev functor. By assumption,
\omega(e)\notin\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z}
so
\mathcal{C}_{\omega(e)}
is semisimple with simple objects the
V_{\alpha}
with
\overline{\alpha}=\omega(e)
. So

F^{RT}(X_{\Gamma,e})\cong\oplus_{\alpha\in\omega(e)}n_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}

for some integers
n_{\alpha}
.
Using a skein relation in the neighborhood of each
\mathbb{D}_{e}
which replaces the identity strand
id_{X_{\Gamma,e}}
by
\sum_{\alpha}n_{\alpha}id_{(V_{\alpha},+)}
, we see that
[\Gamma]
is a linear combination of classes
[\Gamma_{i}]
such that each
\Gamma_{i}
intersects
D_{e}
once exactly along an edge colored by a module
V_{\alpha_{e}}
with
\overline{\alpha_{e}}={\omega(e)}
.

While cutting
H_{g}
along the discs
\mathbb{D}_{e}
, we get a collection of balls
B_{v}
indexed by
v\in V(G)
. If
e,f,g
are the three (non-necessarily pairwise distinct) adjacent edges to
v
, the boundary of
B_{v}
is the union of the pair of pants
P_{v}
and the discs
\mathbb{D}_{e},\mathbb{D}_{f},\mathbb{D}_{g}
. Let
n_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}
be the unique integer such that
\alpha_{e}+\alpha_{f}+\alpha_{g}\in\frac{N}{k}n_{v}+H_{N}
.
Since the multiplicity module
\operatorname{Hom}(V_{\alpha_{e}}\otimes V_{\alpha_{f}}\otimes V_{\alpha_{g}},\sigma^{n})
is one-dimensional generated by
H_{n_{v}}^{\alpha_{e},\alpha_{f},\alpha_{g}}
, using a skein relation inside each ball
B_{v}
, we see that
[\Gamma_{i}]
is proportional to a vector
[G,c]
for
c

\omega
-compatible.

∎

Lemma 4.8
.

For
c_{0}\in C_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})
, we have
[G,c+Nc_{0}]=q^{N\omega(\widehat{c_{0}})}[G,c]
in
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
.

Proof.

Let
c^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
be such that
\overline{c^{\prime}(e)}=\omega(\gamma_{e})
for all edges
e\in E(G^{\prime})
.
First note that the inclusion
\Sigma_{g}\subset S^{3}\setminus(G\cup G^{\prime})
is a retract by deformation and that the induced isomorphism
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g};\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}(S^{3}\setminus(G\cup G^{\prime});\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
sends
\omega
to the shadow
\omega_{\overline{c+c^{\prime}}}
of Definition
3.14
.
By Lemma
3.15
, the CGP invariant satisfies

\left<(G,c+Nc_{0})\cup(G^{\prime},c^{\prime})\right>=q^{N\omega(\widehat{c_{0}})}\left<(G,c)\cup(G^{\prime},c^{\prime})\right>.

Since this is true for every
c^{\prime}
and since, by Lemma
4.7
, the vectors
[G^{\prime},c^{\prime}]
span
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
, then
(G,c+Nc_{0})-e^{4i\pi N\omega(\widehat{c_{0}})}(G,c)
belongs to the right kernel of the Hopf pairing and
[G,c+Nc_{0}]=q^{N\omega(\widehat{c_{0}})}[G,c]
.
∎

Set

\operatorname{col}(G):=\{c:E(G)\to\mathbb{C},\mbox{ such that }\overline{c(e)}=\omega(\gamma_{e})\mbox{ and }0\leq\Re(c(e))<N\mbox{ for all }e\in E(G)\}

and

\mathcal{B}:=\{[G,c],c\in\operatorname{col}(G)\}\subset\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega).

While gluing the handlebody
H_{g}
with itself along the boundary using the identity map
id:\Sigma_{g}\to\Sigma_{g}
we obtain the manifold
X_{g}:=H_{g}\cup_{id}H_{g}\cong(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{2})^{\#g}
made of
g
connected sums of
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Let
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}:H_{g}\hookrightarrow X_{g}
the two inclusion maps.
The class
\omega
induces a class
\overline{\omega}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
given by the class of
\omega_{H_{g}}
in both copies of
H_{g}
inside
X_{g}
. Given two ribbon graphs
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H_{g},\omega_{H_{g}})
, we get a ribbon graph
\iota_{1}(\Gamma)\cup\iota_{2}(\Gamma^{\prime})
in
X_{g}
compatible with
\overline{\omega}
so we can consider the CGP invariant
\left<X_{g},\iota_{1}(\Gamma)\cup\iota_{2}(\Gamma^{\prime}),\overline{\omega}\right>\in\mathbb{C}
defined in
[
CGP14
]
. The pairing

\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{\omega}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H_{g})\otimes\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H_{g})\to\mathbb{C},\quad\left<\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\right>_{\omega}:=\left<X_{g},\iota_{1}(\Gamma)\cup\iota_{2}(\Gamma^{\prime}),\overline{\omega}\right>,

has the same right kernel than the Hopf pairing and
induces a non-degenerate pairing
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{\omega}:\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbb{C}
, named the
invariant pairing
which is indeed invariant in the sense that
\left<r_{\omega}(z)\cdot x,y\right>=\left<x,r_{\omega}(z)\cdot y\right>
. The following lemma is the only place where we actually need the whole TQFTs properties of
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma,\omega)
proved in
[
BCGP16
,
DR22
,
DRGPM20
]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

For
c,c^{\prime}\in\operatorname{col}(G)
, then
\left<[G,c],[G,c^{\prime}]\right>=0
if
c\neq c^{\prime}
and
\left<[G,c],[G,c]\right>\neq 0
.

Proof.

If
c(e)\neq c^{\prime}(e)
, then one has an open ball
\mathbb{B}\subset X_{g}
transversed to the graph
\iota_{1}(\Gamma)\cup\iota_{2}(\Gamma^{\prime})
such that
\partial\mathbb{B}
intersects
\iota_{1}(\Gamma)\cup\iota_{2}(\Gamma^{\prime})
along two points: one colored by
V_{c(e)}
and the other colored by
V_{c^{\prime}(e)}
. Since
c(e)\neq c^{\prime}(e)
have real part in
[0,N)
they are not congruent modulo
\mathbb{Z}
so one has
\operatorname{Hom}(V_{c(e)},V_{c^{\prime}(e)})=0
.
It follows from the TQFTs properties of the CGP invariant
\left<M,\Gamma,\omega\right>
that whenever we have an embedded sphere
S^{2}\subset M
which intersects
\Gamma
along two points colored by
V,W
with
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,W)=0
then
\left<M,\Gamma,\omega\right>=0
so this implies that
\left<[G,c],[G,c^{\prime}]\right>=0
.

When
c^{\prime}=c
, we need again to use the TQFTs property of the CGP invariant to prove that
\left<[G,c],[G,c]\right>
can be written as a product
of non-vanishing
3j
-symbols with the invariant of the sphere
S^{3}
(see the proof of
[
BCGP16
, Proposition
6.7
]
for details). This implies that
\left<[G,c],[G,c]\right>\neq 0
and concludes the proof.
∎

An analogue of the following was proved in
[
BCGP16
, Proposition
6.7
]
and
[
BCGP15
]
in the case of even roots.

Theorem 4.10
.

\mathcal{B}
is a basis of
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
.

In particular
\dim(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega))=N^{|E(G)|}=N^{3g-3}=PI-deg(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g}))
.

Proof.

Let us first prove that
\mathcal{B}
spans
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
. By Lemma
4.7
,
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
is spanned by the vectors
[G,c]
with
c

\omega
-compatible. For such a coloring
c
, there exists
c_{0}\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})
and
c_{1}\in\operatorname{col}(G)
such that
c=c_{1}+Nc_{0}
. By Lemma
4.8
, we have
[G,c]=q^{N\omega(c_{0})}[G,c_{1}]
so
\mathcal{B}
spans
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
.

Let us prove that
\mathcal{B}
is free. First, by Lemma
4.9
, the vectors
[G,c]
are non-null.
Consider the commutative subalgebra
T\subset\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
generated by the classes
[\gamma_{e}]
of the curves in the pants decomposition. By Lemma
3.8
, writing
\lambda_{e}:=q^{\omega([\gamma_{e}])}+q^{-\omega([\gamma_{e}])}
, one has

r_{\omega}([\gamma_{e}])\cdot[G,c]=\lambda_{c(e)}[G,c],\quad\mbox{ for all }e\in{E}(G).

Note that
\lambda_{c(e)}=\lambda_{c^{\prime}(e)}
implies
c(e)=c^{\prime}(e)
for every
c,c^{\prime}\in\operatorname{col}(G)
, so each axis
[G,c]\mathbb{C}
is an intersection of eigenspaces of the operators in
r_{\omega}(T)
. This implies that
\mathcal{B}
is free and concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

The representation
r_{\omega}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})\to\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega))
is central and its classical shadow is the class of the diagonal representation

\rho_{\omega}:\gamma\mapsto\varepsilon_{\gamma}\begin{pmatrix}q^{N\omega([\gamma])}&0\\
0&q^{-N\omega([\gamma])}\end{pmatrix},

where
\varepsilon_{\gamma}=+1
if
A^{N}=1
and
\varepsilon_{\gamma}=(-1)^{w_{S}([\gamma])}
if
A^{N}=-1
.

Remark 4.12
.

Here we implicitly used the same spin structure
S
in the identifications of Theorems
2.17
and
4.4
.

Proof.

We need to prove that for all
\gamma
, then
T_{N}(r_{\omega}([\gamma]))=\varepsilon_{\gamma}(q^{N\omega([\gamma])}+q^{-N\omega([\gamma])})id
. Since the skein algebra at
A=\pm 1
is generated by the classes of non-separating curves, we can suppose that
\gamma
is non-separating.
When
e\in E(G)
, then by Lemma
3.8
one has

r_{\omega}([\gamma_{e}])\cdot[G,c]=\varepsilon_{\gamma}(q^{c(e)}+q^{-c(e)})[G,c].

Since
T_{N}(x+x^{-1})=x^{N}+x^{-N}
, one finds that
T_{N}(r_{\omega}([\gamma_{e}]))\cdot[G,c]=\varepsilon_{\gamma}(q^{N\omega([\gamma_{e}])}+q^{-N\omega([\gamma_{e}])})[G,c]
as expected. Note that the basis
\mathcal{B}
depends on the choice of a pants decomposition of
\Sigma
and we have proved that for any pants decomposition
\mathcal{P}=\{\gamma_{e}\}_{e}
such that all
\gamma_{e}
are non-separating, all curves
\gamma_{e}
satisfy
T_{N}(r_{\omega}([\gamma_{e}]))\cdot[G,c]=\varepsilon_{\gamma}(q^{N\omega([\gamma_{e}])}+q^{-N\omega([\gamma_{e}])})[G,c]
. We conclude using the fact that every non-separating curve
\gamma\subset\Sigma
belongs to such a pants decomposition of
\Sigma
.
∎

4.4.
Irreducibility

The following classical lemma is the key to prove the irreducibility of
r_{\omega}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra and
r:\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(V)
a finite dimensional representation such that
V
is equipped with an invariant form
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>:V\otimes V\to\mathbb{C}
. Let
T\subset\mathcal{A}
a commutative subalgebra and consider the weight decomposition

V=\oplus_{\chi:T\to\mathbb{C}}V^{(\chi)},\quad V^{(\chi)}:=\{v\in V|r(t)v=\chi(t)v,\forall t\in T\}.

Suppose that:

(1)

the spaces
V^{(\chi)}
have dimension
1
, say
V^{(\chi)}=\mathbb{C}v_{\chi}
;

(2)

for each
\chi,\chi^{\prime}
, there exists
a\in\mathcal{A}
such that
\left<v_{\chi^{\prime}},r(a)\cdot v_{\chi}\right>\neq 0.

Then
r
is irreducible.

Proof.

Let
0\neq W\subset V
be an
\mathcal{A}
-invariant subspace and let
\pi_{W}:V\to W
be the orthogonal projector with image
W
. Since
W
is
\mathcal{A}
-invariant, then
\pi_{W}
commutes with
r(\mathcal{A})
. In particular it commutes with
r(T)
so for each character
\chi
, either
V^{(\chi)}\subset W
or
V^{(\chi)}\cap W=\{0\}
. Since
W\neq 0
, there exists
\chi_{0}
such that
v_{\chi_{0}}\in W
so the cyclic space
r(\mathcal{A})\cdot v_{\chi_{0}}
is included in
W
. For every
\chi
, one has
\left<v_{\chi},r(\mathcal{A})v_{\chi_{0}}\right>\neq 0
so
V^{(\chi)}\subset W
therefore
W=V
so
V
has no non-zero proper submodule.
∎

We want to apply Lemma
4.13
to the representation
r_{\omega}
of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
with torus
T=\{[\gamma_{e}],e\in E(G)\}
. In this case, the decomposition writes

\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)=\oplus_{c\in\operatorname{col}(G)}\mathbb{C}[G,c]

where
\mathbb{C}[G,c]
is the one-dimensional weight space corresponding to the character
\chi_{c}
sending
[\gamma_{e}]
to
(q^{\omega(c(e))}+q^{-\omega(c(e))})
. So Lemma
4.13
shows that
r_{\omega}
is irreducible if and only if for each
c,c^{\prime}\in\operatorname{col}(G)
there exists an element
\beta\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
such that
\left<[G,c^{\prime}],r_{\omega}(\beta)\cdot[G,c]\right>\neq 0
.

Let
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{g}\subset H_{g}
be the framed curves drawn in Figure
2
. Each
\beta_{i}
can be represented uniquely by a cycle
c_{\beta_{i}}\in\mathrm{Z}_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z})
such that
c_{\beta_{i}}(e)\in\{-1,0,+1\}
. Let
cl(\beta_{i})
be the set of cycles
\eta:E(G)\to\{-1,0,+1\}
such that
\eta(e)=0
if and only if
c_{\beta_{i}}(e)=0
.

Notations 4.14
.

Let
Y(\omega)\subset\mathbb{C}
be the finite set of
6j
-symbols of the form

6S(\eta_{1}c(e_{a})+\varepsilon_{1},\eta_{2}c(e_{b})+\varepsilon_{2},\eta_{3}c(e_{c})+\varepsilon_{3};\eta_{4},\eta_{5})

where
(e_{a},e_{b},e_{c})
are three adjacent edges of
G
oriented towards a common vertex,
c\in\operatorname{col}(G)
,
\eta_{u}\in\{-1,+1\}
and
\varepsilon_{v}\in\{-1,0,+1\}
are such that
\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3}
is even.

Lemma 4.15
.

One has

r_{\omega}([\beta_{i}])\cdot[G,c]=\sum_{\eta\in cl(\beta_{i})}x_{\eta}[G,c+\eta]

where
x_{\eta}
is a product of elements of
Y(\omega)
and of non-vanishing quantum binomials of the form
\left[\begin{array}[]{l}a+N-1\\
a\end{array}\right]^{-1}
with
a\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

The proof is a simple computation using the following two skein relations:

\includegraphics[width]{relationskein.eps}.

For instance, for
\beta_{1}
, writing
x_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{l}c_{1}+\eta_{1}+N-1\\
c_{1}+\eta_{1}\end{array}\right]^{-1}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}c_{2}+\eta_{2}+N-1\\
c_{2}+\eta_{2}\end{array}\right]^{-1}
,
one finds (here we put
c_{4}:=c_{3}
if
g=2
):

r_{\omega}(\beta_{1})\cdot[G,c]=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{calcul1.eps}}}}}}}$}=\sum_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}=\pm 1}x_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{calcul2.eps}}}}}}}$}\\
=\sum_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}=\pm 1}x_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}6S(-c_{2}-\eta_{2},-{c_{1}}-\eta_{1},c_{3};\eta_{2},\eta_{1})6S(c_{1}+\eta_{1},c_{2}+\eta_{2},c_{4};-\eta_{1},-\eta_{2})\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{calcul3.eps}}}}}}}$}.

The cases of
\beta_{i}
for
i>1
are similar and left to the reader.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

If
\omega\in\mathcal{U}
is such that
0\notin Y(\omega)
, then
r_{\omega}
is irreducible.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{A}:=r_{\omega}(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g}))\subset\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega))
.
Note that
\mathbb{C}v_{c}
is an intersection of eigenspaces of the operators
r_{\omega}(\gamma_{e})
. If
\pi\in\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega))
is a projector which commutes with
\mathscr{A}
, it must preserve these eigenspaces so
\pi(v_{c})\subset\mathbb{C}v_{c}
. This means that if
W\subset\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
is preserved by
\mathscr{A}
then there exists
J\subset\operatorname{col}(G)
such that
W=\operatorname{Span}([G,c],c\in J)
. In particular, for
c,c^{\prime}\in\operatorname{col}(G)
, either

\mathscr{A}v_{c}=\mathscr{A}v_{c^{\prime}}
, in which case we say that
c,c^{\prime}
are
related
, or
\mathscr{A}v_{c}\cap\mathscr{A}v_{c^{\prime}}=0
. Since the pairing
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>
is
\mathscr{A}
invariant, then
c,c^{\prime}
are related if and only if

\left<[G,c],r_{\omega}(x)[G,c^{\prime}]\right>\neq 0
for some
x\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
. Being related is clearly an equivalence relation.
By Lemma
4.13
, to prove that
r_{\omega}
is irreducible, it suffices to prove that every
c,c^{\prime}\in\operatorname{col}(G)
are related. Lemma
4.15
shows that, when
0\notin Y(\omega)
, for every
1\leq i\leq g
and for every
\eta\in cl(\beta_{i})
then
c
and
c+\eta
are related.
\operatorname{col}(G)
has a natural structure of torsor over the abelian group
\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})^{E(G)}
where the free transitive action is given by sending
c_{0}\in\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})
and
c_{1}\in\operatorname{col}(G)
to the unique element of
\operatorname{col}(G)
which induces the class of
c_{0}+c_{1}
in
\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{C}/N\mathbb{Z})
. The set
\{\eta;\eta\in k(\beta_{i}),1\leq i\leq g\}\subset\operatorname{col}(G)
generates the group
\mathrm{C}_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})
so every
c,c^{\prime}\in\operatorname{col}(G)
are related and
r_{\omega}
is irreducible.
∎

Lemma 4.17
.

There exists
\omega\in\mathcal{U}
such that
0\notin Y(\omega)
.

Proof.

Let
Z(\omega)\in\mathbb{C}
be the product of every
6j
-symbols in
Y(\omega)
. By Proposition
3.17
, there exists
R\in\mathbb{Q}(A)(X_{1},\ldots,X_{3g-3})
such that
R\neq 0
and

Z(\omega)=R(q^{\frac{\omega(\gamma(e_{1}))}{2}},\ldots,q^{\frac{\omega(\gamma(e_{3g-3}))}{2}}).

Let
\varphi:\mathcal{U}\to(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{3g-3}
be the analytic map sending
\omega
to
(q^{\frac{\omega(\gamma(e_{1}))}{2}},\ldots,q^{\frac{\omega(\gamma(e_{3g-3}))}{2}})
; so
Z
decomposes as:

Z:\mathcal{U}\xrightarrow{\varphi}(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{3g-3}\xrightarrow{R}\mathbb{C}.

Since the image
\varphi(\mathcal{U})
is a non-empty open (analytic) subset, if
Z(\mathcal{U})=\{0\}
, then we would have
R=0
which would contradict Proposition
3.17
. Therefore there exists
\omega\in\mathcal{U}
such that
Z(\omega)\neq 0
, i.e. such that
0\notin Y(\omega)
.
∎

Proof of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
.

By Lemma
4.17
, there exists
\omega\in\mathcal{U}
such that
0\notin Y(\omega)
so Lemma
4.16
implies that
r_{\omega}
is irreducible. By Proposition
4.11
,
r_{\omega}
is a central representation with classical shadow
[\rho_{\omega}]\in\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
and its dimension is equal to the PI-degree of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
(i.e.
N^{3g-3}
) by Theorem
4.10
. So Corollary
2.11
implies that
[\rho_{\omega}]
belongs to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
and Corollary
2.26
implies that the whole strata
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
is included in the Azumaya locus.

∎

5.
Open surfaces

We now extend the previous results to the open surfaces
\Sigma_{g,n}
with
n\geq 1
. Since the skein algebras are commutative (and thus Azumaya) when
(g,n)
is
(0,0)
,
(0,1)
,
(0,2)
or
(0,3)
, we now assume that we are not in one of these cases.

5.1.
Center and sliced character varieties

Let
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}
be the peripheral curves encircling once the boundary components of
\Sigma_{g,n}
.

Theorem 5.1
.

(Frohman-Kania Bartoszynska, Lê
[
FKL19
,
FKL21
]
) The center
Z
of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
is the algebra
Ch_{A}(\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\Sigma_{g,n}))[\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}]
with relation
T_{N}(\gamma_{i})=Ch_{A}(\gamma_{i})
. Moreover
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
has PI-degree
N^{3g-3+n}
.

Therefore the variety
\operatorname{MaxSpec}(Z)
is isomorphic to the variety

\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})=\{([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\mid[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n}),z_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\mbox{ s.t. }T_{N}(z_{i})=-\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))\}

and the Chebyshev-Frobenius morphism
Ch_{A}:\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma_{g,n})\hookrightarrow Z

defines a branched covering
\pi:\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
sending
([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
to
[\rho]
.
When
\varepsilon=+1
, we have chosen the Spin structure
S
such that its quadratic form
w_{S}
satisfies
w_{S}([\gamma_{i}])=0
.

Definition 5.2
.

(Fully Azumaya locus) The
fully Azumaya locus
is the subset

\mathcal{FAL}:=\{([\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})\mbox{ such that }\pi^{-1}([\rho])\subset\mathcal{AL}\}

i.e. is the set of classes of representations
[\rho]
such that
([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is in the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
for every compatible
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
.

Extend the Poisson structure of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
to
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
by setting that the elements
\gamma_{i}
are Casimir (i.e. that
\{\gamma_{i},f\}=0
for all
f
). Recall from Section
2.4
that we have a Poisson order
(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n}),\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n}),Ch_{A},D)
where
D
is defined as in the closed case with the additional condition
D_{\gamma_{i}}=0
. A classification of the symplectic leaves is not known in general (see however
[
FR99
, Proposition
2
]
and
[
GHJW97
, Theorem
9.1
]
for related results).
Consider the morphisms

{\nu}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\mathbb{C}^{n},\quad{\nu}([\rho]):=(\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{1})),\ldots,\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{n})))

and

\widehat{\nu}:\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\mathbb{C}^{n},\quad\widehat{\nu}([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}):=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}).

For
c\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that
T_{N}(z_{i})=c_{i}
, we set
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},c):=\nu^{-1}(c)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},z):=\widehat{\nu}^{-1}(z)
.

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},c)
is called the
relative
or
sliced
character variety. Since the
\gamma_{i}
are Casimir elements, then the symplectic leaves are contained in the lifts of the sliced character varieties.
It is proved independently in
[
Wha20
,
FKL23
]
that
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},c)
is irreducible, in
[
Wha20
]
that it is normal and in
[
FR99
, Proposition
2
]
and
[
GHJW97
, Theorem
9.1
]
that the smooth loci of sliced character varieties are symplectic. However none of these loci is dense in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
so we cannot conclude easily like in the case of closed surfaces. The trick is to consider the
sliced skein algebras
:

\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n},z):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left(\gamma_{i}-z_{i},i=1,\ldots,n\right)$}\right.}.

Let
c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
and
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
with
c_{i}=-T_{N}(z_{i})
.
It is proved in
[
FKL23
]
that
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n},z)
is an almost Azumaya algebra with the same PI-degree as
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
(i.e.
N^{3g-3+n}
), therefore the inclusion
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},c)\subset\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
sends the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n},z)
into the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
.

The Chebyshev morphism induces an isomorphism
Ch_{A}:\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},c)]\cong\mathcal{Z}\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n},z)\right)

and we obtain a Poisson order
(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n},z),\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},z),Ch_{A},D)
. Since the smooth locus of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},z)
is symplectic, we deduce the

Theorem 5.3
.

(Frohman-Kania Bartoszynska-Lê
[
FKL23
, Theorem
11.1
]
)
If
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},c)
belongs to the smooth loci of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},c)
, then
[\rho]
is in the fully Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
.

An explicit description of these smooth loci is not known in general but a lot can be deduced:

Corollary 5.4
.

(1)

(
[
FKL23
, Proposition
4.3
]
) If
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{0,n})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is irreducible and
c_{i}=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))\neq\pm 2
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, then
[\rho]\in\mathcal{FAL}(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{0,n}))
.

(2)

(
[
FKL23
, Proposition
4.4
]
) Let
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
such that:
(1)

c_{i}=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))\neq\pm 2
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
(2)

if
c_{i}=t_{i}+t_{i}^{-1}
then
t_{1}t_{2}\ldots t_{n}\neq 1
. Then
[\rho]\in\mathcal{FAL}(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n}))
.

Let us first make some simple remarks. First Lemma
2.28
has an obvious analogue for open surfaces.
The group
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
acts by automorphism on
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
by the formula
\chi\cdot[\gamma]:=(-1)^{\chi(\gamma)}[\gamma]
. This action induces an action on the center
Z
which defines an action on
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})\cong\operatorname{MaxSpec}(Z)
described by

\chi\cdot([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}):=([\chi\cdot\rho],(-1)^{\chi(\gamma_{1})}z_{1},\ldots,(-1)^{\chi(\gamma_{n})}z_{n}),\mbox{ where }\chi\cdot\rho(\alpha):=(-1)^{\chi(\alpha)}\rho(\alpha).

Lemma 5.5
.

The Azumaya locus is preserved by this
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
action.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that the action of
\chi\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
on
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
induces an isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})_{x}\cong\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})_{\chi\cdot x}
.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that
(g,n)\neq(1,1)
. If
x=([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is such that
z_{i}=-(q+q^{-1})
for some
i
, then
x
does not belong to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
. In particular, writing
c_{i}:=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))=-T_{N}(z_{i})
, if there exists
i
such that
c_{i}=2
then
[\rho]\notin\mathcal{FAL}
. So
n\geq 2
and
c_{i}=-2
for some
i
also implies that
[\rho]\notin\mathcal{FAL}
.

Proof.

The condition
(g,n)\neq(1,1)
ensures that
PI-deg(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n-1}))<PI-deg(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n}))
. The inclusion
\Sigma_{g,n}\hookrightarrow\Sigma_{g,n-1}
(
\Sigma_{g,n}
is obtained from
\Sigma_{g,n-1}
by removing an open disc) induces a surjective morphism
p:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n-1})
and an embedding
i:\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n-1})\to\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
. If
x=([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is such that
z_{1}=-(q+q^{-1})
(
z_{1}
corresponds to the boundary of the disc we removed) then
x
is in the image of
i
so
x=i(x_{0})
. Let
r_{0}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n-1})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
be an irreducible representation with classical shadow
x_{0}
. Then
\dim(V)\leq PI-deg(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n-1}))
, so
r:=r_{0}\circ p:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\operatorname{End}(V)
is a non-trivial representation of dimension
\dim(V)<PI-deg(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n}))
thus its classical shadow
x
does not belong to the Azumaya locus. So
[\rho]\notin\mathcal{FAL}
whenever
c_{i}=2
for some
i
. If
n\geq 2
, we can always find
\chi\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
such that
\chi(\gamma_{i})\equiv 1\pmod{2}
. So Lemma
5.5
implies that
[\rho]\notin\mathcal{FAL}
whenever
c_{i}=-2
for some
i
in this case.
∎

We will be interested in the loci of diagonal representations.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}_{c}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n},c)
be the locus of classes of diagonal non-central representations. Then
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}_{c}
is a connected smooth irreducible symplectic subvariety. In particular, either
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}_{c}
is included in the Azumaya locus or it does not intersect it.

Proof.

We proceed in the same manner than in the proof of Lemma
2.16
. Let
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma_{g,n})=\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{C}^{*})=\operatorname{MaxSpec}(\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{Z})])
equipped with the Poisson structure defined by
\{X_{[\alpha]},X_{[\beta]}\}=([\alpha],[\beta])X_{[\alpha+\beta]}
. Since the kernel of the intersection form
(\cdot,\cdot)
is generated by the classes
[\gamma_{i}]
, the symplectic leaves of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
are the fibers of the map
f:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
sending
\chi
to
(\chi([\gamma_{1}]),\ldots,\chi([\gamma_{n}]))
. Denote by
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{z}}:=f^{-1}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
such a fiber: it is a symplectic torus of dimension
2g
.
Define
\Phi:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\mathcal{X}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
by
\Phi(\chi):=[\varphi\circ\chi]
. Like in the proof of Lemma
2.16
, using Goldman’s formula (which also holds for open surfaces as proved in
[
Law09
,
Kor19b
]
), we see that
\Phi
is a Poisson double covering branched along the locus
\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
of central representations. The intersection
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{z}}^{(2)}:=\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{X}^{(2)})\cap\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{z}}
is finite so
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{z}}\setminus\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{z}}^{(2)}
is connected.
Thus
\Phi
induces a double regular Poisson covering
\Phi:\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{z}}\setminus\mathbb{T}_{\mathbf{z}}^{(2)}\to\mathcal{X}^{(1)}_{c}
so
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}_{c}
is symplectic and connected.
This concludes the proof.
∎

5.2.
Relative skein modules

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a ribbon category and
M
a compact oriented
3
-manifold. A
marking
is a tuple
\mathcal{P}=\left((p_{1},\epsilon_{1},V_{1}),\ldots,(p_{n},\epsilon_{n},V_{n})\right)
where
p_{i}
is a marked point in
\partial M
, (i.e. a point in
\partial M
equipped with a non-zero tangent vector in
T_{p}\partial M
),
\epsilon_{i}=\pm 1
and
V_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
. The
relative skein module

\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(M,\mathcal{P})
is the quotient of the linear span of
\mathcal{D}
-colored ribbon graphs
\Gamma\subset M
such that
\partial\Gamma=\mathcal{P}
by skein relations (as defined in Definition
4.1
). Suppose that
\mathcal{D}
is
\mathcal{A}
-graded for
\mathcal{A}
an abelian group. Like in Section
4.1
, for
H
a handlebody and
\mathcal{P}
a marking in
\partial H
, the relative skein module
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(H,\mathcal{P})
is
\mathrm{H}^{1}(H;\mathcal{A})
-graded and we denote by
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(H,\mathcal{P},\omega_{H})
its
\omega_{H}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(H;\mathcal{A})
-graded part. Let
G_{g,n},G^{\prime}_{g,n}\subset\mathbb{S}^{3}
be the uni-trivalent ribbon graphs of Figure
3
and choose tubular neighborhoods
H_{g,n}=N(G_{g,n})
and
H^{\prime}_{g,n}=N(G^{\prime}_{g,n})
such that
G_{g,n}\cap G^{\prime}_{g,n}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
is the set of vertices of valence
1
of both
G_{g,n}
and
G^{\prime}_{g,n}
and such that
H_{g,n}\cap H_{g,n}=\partial H_{g,n}=\partial H^{\prime}_{g,n}
. Note again that for any edge
e
of
G_{g,n}\cup G^{\prime}_{g,n}
then
\gamma_{e}
is non-separating.

Figure 3.
Two uni-trivalent graphs of genus
g
with
n
external edges.

Let
\Sigma_{g,n}
be obtained from
\partial H_{g,n}=\partial H^{\prime}_{g,n}
by removing a small open disc around each
p_{i}
.
Suppose that in
\mathcal{P}=\left((p_{i},\epsilon_{i},V_{i})\right)
, each
V_{i}
is homogeneous of degree
a_{i}\in A
.
Let
\omega\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathcal{A})
be a class such that
\omega([\gamma_{i}])=a_{i}
. By restriction, the class
\omega
induces two classes
\omega_{H}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(H_{g,n};\mathcal{A})
and
\omega_{H^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(H^{\prime}_{g,n};\mathcal{A})
such that
\omega
is completely determined by the tuple
(\omega_{H},\omega_{H^{\prime}},\{a_{i}\}_{i})
.

Note that
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(H_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega_{H})
and
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(H^{\prime}_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega_{H^{\prime}})
admit natural structures of left and right
\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
-modules respectively and we have a Hopf pairing:

(\cdot,\cdot)_{\omega}^{H}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(H^{\prime}_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega_{H^{\prime}})\otimes\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(H_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega_{H})\to\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{S}^{3}),\quad(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)_{\omega}^{H}:=\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\Gamma.

5.3.
WRT representations for open surfaces

Consider the case where
\mathcal{D}=TL
is the Cauchy closure of the Temperley-Lieb category with trivial grading: so as a braided category,
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to the category
\mathcal{C}^{small}
equipped with a modified twist. Consider
\mathcal{P}=\left((p_{1},+,S_{i_{1}}),\ldots,(p_{n},+,S_{i_{n}})\right)
where the punctures are colored by the (non-projective) simple modules
S_{i_{k}}
with
i_{k}\in\{0,\ldots,\frac{N-3}{2}\}
with non-vanishing q-dimension (as objects in
\mathcal{D}
they correspond to the Jones-Wenzl idempotents). Using the natural identification
\mathscr{S}^{TL}(\mathbb{S}^{3})\cong\mathbb{C}
(sending the empty link to
1
) we consider the Hopf pairing with values in
\mathbb{C}
and define

V(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P}):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathscr{S}^{TL}(H_{g,n},\mathcal{P})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{Rker}(\cdot,\cdot)^{H}$}\right.}.

The left action of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})=\mathscr{S}^{TL}(\Sigma_{g,n})
on
\mathscr{S}^{TL}(H_{g,n},\mathcal{P})
induces a representation

r^{WRT}_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\operatorname{End}(V(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P})).

Let
\operatorname{col}(G_{g,n})
denote the set of maps
c:E(G_{g,n})\to\{0,\ldots,\frac{N-3}{2}\}
such that
(1)

c(e_{\partial_{k}})=i_{k}
where
e_{\partial_{k}}
is the edge adjacent to
p_{k}
and
(2)
when
e_{i},e_{j},e_{k}
are three adjacent edges then
c(e_{i})+c(e_{j})+c(e_{k})
is even and
\leq N-3
and
c(e_{i})+c(e_{j})\leq c(e_{k})
. For
c\in\operatorname{col}(G_{g,n})
, let
[G,c]\in V(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P})
be the class of the ribbon graph obtained by coloring each edge
e
of
G_{g,n}
by
S_{c(e)}
and each trivalent vertex
v
with adjacent edges
e_{i},e_{j},e_{k}
by an arbitrary non-zero element of
\operatorname{Hom}(S_{c(e_{i})}\otimes S_{c(e_{j})}\otimes S_{c(e_{k})},\mathds{1})
.

Theorem 5.8
.

(
[
Tur10
,
BHMV95
]
) The set
\mathcal{B}:=\{[G,c],c\in\operatorname{col}(G_{g,n})\}
is a basis of
V(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P})
.

Lemma 5.9
.

(1)

\dim(V(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P}))<PI-deg(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n}))
.

(2)

r^{WRT}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a central representation with classical shadow
([\rho^{0}],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
where
\rho^{0}
is central and
z_{k}:=(-1)^{w_{S}(\gamma_{k})}(q^{i_{k}+1}+q^{-i_{k}-1})
.

Proof.

The first item follows from the inequality
|\operatorname{col}(G_{g,n})|<N^{3g-3+n}
together with Theorems
5.3
and
5.8
. The proof of the second item is identical to the proof of Proposition
4.11
using the identity
S^{\prime}(S_{i},S_{1})=q^{i+1}+q^{-(i+1)}
of Lemma
3.8
.
∎

We can now prove the first item of Theorem
1.5
.

Theorem 5.10
.

If
x=([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is such that
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
is central and
z_{i}\neq\pm 2
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, then
x
does not belong to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
.

Proof.

Let
i_{k}\in\{0,\ldots,\frac{N-3}{2}\}
be the unique integer such that
z_{k}=\pm(q^{i_{k}+1}+q^{-(i_{k}+1)})
and consider
\mathcal{P}=\left((p_{1},+,S_{i_{1}}),\ldots,(p_{n},+,S_{i_{n}})\right)
. On the one hand, there exists
\chi\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
(which depends on the choice of spin structure when
\varepsilon=+1
) such that
\chi\cdot x
is the classical shadow of
r_{\mathcal{P}}^{WRT}
. On the other hand, by Lemma
5.9
,
r_{\mathcal{P}}^{WRT}
is a central representation with dimension strictly smaller than the PI-degree of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
, therefore its classical shadow does not belong to the Azumaya locus. So
x
does not either.
∎

5.4.
BCGP representations for open surfaces

Now consider the case where
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}
is the category of representations of the unrolled quantum group. Let
\omega\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
and
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\overline{\alpha_{i}}=\omega([\gamma_{i}])
and
\omega(e)\notin(\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}
for all
e\in\mathring{E}(G)\cup\mathring{E}(G^{\prime})
(here
\mathring{E}(G):=E(G)\setminus\{e_{\partial_{1}},\ldots,e_{\partial_{n}}\}
). Consider
\mathcal{P}:=\left((p_{1},+,V_{\alpha_{1}}),\ldots,(p_{n},+,V_{\alpha_{n}})\right)
and consider the Hopf pairing

(\cdot,\cdot)_{\omega}^{H}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H^{\prime}_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega_{H^{\prime}})\otimes\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega_{H})\to\mathbb{C},\quad(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)_{\omega}^{H}:=\left<\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\Gamma\right>,

where
\left<\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\Gamma\right>
denotes the CGP invariants of the ribbon graph
\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\Gamma
. Since
\alpha_{i}\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\Gamma
has one edge colored by a projective module so the CGP invariant is well-defined. Set

\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}}(H_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega_{H})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{Rker}(\cdot,\cdot)_{\omega}^{H}$}\right.}

and consider the representation

r_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{S}^{\mathcal{C}^{small}}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega)).

Here the spin structure chosen to define
f
is the same as the one used to identify
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\Sigma_{g,n})
with
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})]
. Recall that we made the convention that
w_{S}([\gamma_{i}])=0
.
Set
z_{i}:=-\left(q^{\alpha_{i}}+q^{-\alpha_{i}}\right)
and define
\rho_{\omega}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
by the same formula than in Proposition
4.11
.
Set

\operatorname{col}(G):=\{c:E(G)\to\mathbb{C}\mbox{ such that }c(e_{\partial_{i}})=\alpha_{i}\mbox{ and }\overline{c(e)}=\omega(\gamma_{e}),0\leq\Re(c(e))<N\mbox{ for all }e\in\mathring{E}(G)\}

and consider
\mathcal{B}:=\{[G,c],c\in\operatorname{col}(G)\}
, where
[G,c]
is defined by fixing a
\sigma
-decoration of
G\cup G^{\prime}\subset S^{3}
as before.

Proposition 5.11
.

(1)

\mathcal{B}
is a basis of
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega)
.

(2)

r_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}
is a central representation with classical shadow
([\rho_{\omega}],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
and dimension
N^{3g-3+n}=PI-deg(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n}))
.

(3)

if
g\geq 1
,
r_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}
is irreducible.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward adaptation of the proofs of Theorem
4.10
, Proposition
4.11
and Theorem
1.3
. We only detail the proof of the last item. Suppose
g\geq 1
, fix
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
and
set

\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{z}}:=\{\omega\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}),\mbox{ such that }\omega(\gamma_{i})=T_{N}(z_{i}),\omega(\gamma_{e})\not\in(\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ for all }1\leq i\leq n,e\in\mathring{E}(G)\cup\mathring{E}(G^{\prime})\}.

For every
[\omega]\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{z}}
, then
([\rho_{\omega}],\mathbf{z})
belongs to the symplectic core
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}\cap\widehat{\nu}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})
, therefore either every representation
r_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}
with
\omega\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{z}}
is irreducible or none of them is. Since we made the assumptions
n\geq 1
and
(g,n)\neq(0,i)
for
i\leq 3
, the analytic set
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{z}}
has dimension
\geq 1
. If
Z(\omega)\in\mathbb{C}
is the product of all
6j
-symbols in Notations
4.14
, then
Z:\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{z}}\to\mathbb{C}
is a non-vanishing analytic function by Proposition
3.17
, so there exists
\omega\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{z}}
for which none of these
6j
-symbol vanishes. The proof of Lemma
4.16
extends word-by-word to the open case and proves that
r_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}
is irreducible (note that this proof would not work if
g=0
in which case the curves
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{g}
used in the proof are not defined).
Since it is true for one
\omega\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{z}}
, it is true for all of them. This concludes the proof.
∎

We can now prove the fourth part of Theorem
1.5
.

Theorem 5.12
.

If
g\geq 1
and
x=([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is such that
(1)

[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}^{(1)}
and
(2)
for every
1\leq i\leq n
, either
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))\neq\pm 2
or
z_{i}=\pm 2
, then
x
belongs to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
.

Proof.

Let
x=([\rho],z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
satisfying the conditions
(1)
and
(2)
of the theorem. By
(2)
, for every
1\leq i\leq n
, there exists
\alpha_{i}\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
such that
z_{i}=-\left(q^{\alpha_{i}}+q^{-\alpha_{i}}\right)
. Let
\mathcal{P}
denote the associated marked points. By
(1)
,
x
belongs to the connected symplectic variety
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}_{\mathbf{z}}
.
By Proposition
5.11
, there exists
x^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}_{\mathbf{z}}
which is the classical shadow of a representation
r_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}
which is irreducible and has dimension
N^{3g-3+n}
by Proposition
5.11
. Therefore
x^{\prime}
belongs to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
and so does
x
by Theorem
2.22
and Lemma
5.7
.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

The definition we gave of the representations
r_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}
still makes sense under the weaker hypothesis that either at least one puncture of
\mathcal{P}
is colored by a projective module or that
\omega\notin\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
(this condition ensures that the CGP invariants used in the Hopf pairing are well-defined). Therefore the BCGP representations could also be used in order to determine the intersection of the Azumaya locus with the whole set
\widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
. The reason we added hypotheses
(1)
and
(2)
in Theorem
5.12
is that without those hypotheses, the space
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega)
is no longer spanned by trivalent graphs colored by typical modules
V_{\alpha},\alpha\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
since we need to considerate graphs colored by modules
S_{n}
and
P_{n}
as well. However, in
[
BCGP15
, Theorem
5.1
]
, the authors still found basis (in the non-closed case) for these spaces and it seems that our techniques might be generalized to this more general setting as well.

5.5.
Particular cases

5.5.1.
One holed torus

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\subset\Sigma_{1,1}
be the three curves of Figures
4
and
\gamma_{p}
the peripheral curve around the puncture. Fricke showed in
[
Fri96
]
(see also
[
Gol09
]
) that the map sending
[\rho]
to
\varphi([\rho]):=(\operatorname{tr}(\alpha_{1}),\operatorname{tr}(\alpha_{2}),\operatorname{tr}(\alpha_{3}))
defines an isomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Moreover, if
\varphi([\rho])=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
then
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p}))=t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}+t_{3}^{3}-t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}-2
, so the sliced character variety writes

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1},c)\cong\{(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})|t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}+t_{3}^{2}-t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}-2=c\}.

Figure 4.
Three curves
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
in the holed torus
\Sigma_{1,1}
and a triangulation
\Delta
.

The
quaternionic representation

\rho_{0}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1,1})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is defined by

\rho_{0}(\alpha_{1}):=\mathbf{i}=\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\
0&-i\end{pmatrix},\quad\rho_{0}(\alpha_{2}):=\mathbf{j}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\rho_{0}(\alpha_{3}):=\mathbf{k}=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}.

Note that
[\rho_{0}]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1},-2)
has coordinate
\varphi([\rho_{0}])=(0,0,0)
. For
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}=\pm 1
, let
\rho_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
be the central representation with coordinates
(\varepsilon 2,\varepsilon^{\prime}2,\varepsilon\varepsilon^{\prime}2)
.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
([\rho],z)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1})
be such that
([\rho],z)\neq([\rho_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}],-2)
for all
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}=\pm 1
.
Then
([\rho],z)
belongs to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})

if and only if either
[\rho]
is neither central, nor quaternionic, or
[\rho]=[\rho_{0}]
and
z=+2
. In particular, the fully Azumaya locus is the locus of classes of representations which are neither central nor quaternionic.

The techniques of the present paper are insufficient to determine whether the four points
([\rho_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}],-2)
belong to the Azumaya locus or not (though either all of them or neither of them belong to this locus).
Soon after the prepublication of the present paper, Yu proved in
[
Yu23
]
that they do belong to the Azumaya locus.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1})
and set
c:=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p}))
. Then
[\rho]
is a smooth point of
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1},c)
if and only if
[\rho]
is not central and
[\rho]\neq[\rho_{0}]
.

Proof.

Let
f:\mathbb{C}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}
be defined by
f(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})=t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}+t_{3}^{2}-t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}-2
. Then
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1},c)\cong f^{-1}(c)
so
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1},c)
with
\varphi([\rho])=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
is smooth if and only if
D_{(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})}f\neq(0,0,0)
. We compute:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t_{1}}f(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})=0\\
\partial_{t_{2}}f(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})=0\\
\partial_{t_{3}}f(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})=0\end{array}\right.\Leftrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}2t_{1}=t_{2}t_{3}\\
2t_{2}=t_{1}t_{3}\\
2t_{3}=t_{1}t_{2}\end{array}\right.\Leftrightarrow(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})=(0,0,0)\mbox{ or }(\varepsilon_{1}2,\varepsilon_{2}2,\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}2)\mbox{ where }\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1.

This concludes the proof.
∎

When
\rho
is a central representation and
z=-q^{n}-q^{-n}
for
n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
, then
([\rho],z)
is not in the Azumaya locus by Theorem
5.10
. Therefore the only remaining cases are the cases
(1)
of elements
([\rho_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}],-2)
and
(2)
the quaternionic cases
([\rho_{0}],+(q^{n}+q^{-n}))
with
n=0,\ldots,N-1
.

In
[
BP00
]
, Bullock and Przytycki proved that
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})
is finitely presented by the generators
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
and the following relations:

(9)

A\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}-A^{-1}\alpha_{i+1}\alpha_{i}=(q-q^{-1})\alpha_{i+2}\quad\mbox{, for }i\in\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}.

Let
U^{\prime}_{q}\mathfrak{so}_{3}
be the algebra with generators
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
and relations
AI_{i}I_{i+1}-A^{-1}I_{i+1}I_{i}=I_{i+2}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
introduced in
[
HP01
]
.
Obviously, we have an isomorphism
\Psi:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})\cong U_{q}^{\prime}\mathfrak{so}_{3}
sending
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
to
((q-q^{-1})I_{1},(q-q^{-1})I_{2},(q-q^{-1})I_{3})
.
Large classes of irreducible representations of
U_{q}^{\prime}\mathfrak{so}_{3}
have been defined and studied by Havlíček-Pošta
in
[
HP01
]
from which Takenov deduced in
[
Tak
, Theorem
15
]
that if
[\rho]
is neither central, nor quaternionic and satisfies the additional hypothesis that

(\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\alpha_{i})),\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\alpha_{i+1})),\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\alpha_{i+2})))\neq(\varepsilon_{1}2,\varepsilon_{2}\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}},\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}),\mbox{ for }i\in\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ and }\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}=\pm 1,

then
[\rho]
is in the fully Azumaya locus. So Takenov’s result is a weaker form (of the particular case
(g,n)=(1,1)
) of Theorem
5.3
. The following representations were introduced in
[
HP01
]
.

Definition 5.16
.

(Havlíček-Pošta representations)
For
1\leq r\leq N
, the representation
S_{r}
has basis
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{r-1}\}
and module structure:

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}v_{j}=-q^{-1/2}\frac{q-q^{-1}}{q^{-j+\frac{r-1}{2}}+q^{j-\frac{r-1}{2}}}(q[j][j-r]v_{j-1}+v_{j+1});

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}v_{j}=iq^{1/2}\frac{q-q^{-1}}{q^{-j+\frac{r-1}{2}}+q^{j-\frac{r-1}{2}}}(q^{\frac{r}{2}-j}[j][j-r]v_{j-1}+q^{-\frac{r}{2}+j}v_{j+1});

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}v_{j}=-i(q^{-j+\frac{r-1}{2}}-q^{j-\frac{r-1}{2}})v_{j};

where we set
v_{r}:=v_{-1}=0
.

The following lemma will be used in order to compute the classical shadow of
S_{r}
.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
N\geq 1
be an odd integer and
M=(M_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
an
N\times N
matrix. Suppose that

(1)

there exists
t\in\mathbb{C}
such that
T_{N}(M)=tid
and

(2)

we have
M_{ij}=0
whenever
|i-j|\neq 1
.

Then
t=0
.

Proof.
