Let us first prove that the second hypothesis implies that
M
is not invertible by induction on
N
. If
N=1
, the second hypothesis implies that
M=(0)
. Now suppose that the results is true for
N-2
and let
M^{\prime}
be the
(N-2)\times(N-2)
matrix obtained from
M
by removing the two first lines and two first columns. Clearly
M^{\prime}
satisfies the second hypothesis so is not invertible. By developing the determinant
\det(M)
first along the first line then along the first column, we obtain the identity
\det(M)=-M_{2,1}M_{1,2}\det(M^{\prime})=0
so
M
is not invertible. By induction, this is true for all
N
odd.

Now using the first hypothesis and writing
t=x^{N}+x^{-N}
for some
x\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, the identity
T_{N}(M)=t
implies that the spectrum of
M
is included in the set
\{q^{n}x+q^{-n}x^{-1},n=0,\ldots,N-1\}
. Since
M
is not invertible,
0
belongs to this spectrum so there exists
n\geq 0
such that

q^{n}x+q^{-n}x^{-1}=0\Leftrightarrow x=\pm iq^{-n}\Rightarrow t=\pm(i-i)=0.

So
t=0
.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

S_{r}
is a well-defined irreducible representation with classical shadow
([\rho_{0}],q^{r}+q^{-r})

Proof.

The fact that
S_{r}
is a well-defined irreducible representations follows from the fact that the induced
U^{\prime}_{q}\mathfrak{so}_{3}
-modules obtained using
\Psi:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})\cong U_{q}^{\prime}\mathfrak{so}_{3}
were proved to be well-defined and irreducible in
[
HP01
, Note
4(a)
]
(compare with Equations
(22)
,
(23)
,
(24)
in
[
HP01
]
where
v_{j}
is denoted
x_{-j}
). Let
C\in U_{q}^{\prime}\mathfrak{so}_{3}
be the central element defined by
C:=q^{2}I_{1}^{2}+I_{2}^{2}+q^{2}I_{3}^{2}-(q^{5/2}-q^{1/2})I_{1}I_{2}I_{3}
. Then it is proved in
[
HP01
]
that
C
acts by the scalar
c=-q[\frac{r-1}{2}][\frac{r+1}{2}]
. Since
\gamma_{p}=-q^{-1}(q-q^{-1})^{2}\Psi^{-1}(C)+(q+q^{-1})
we deduce that
\gamma_{p}
acts by the scalar
z:=q^{r}+q^{-r}
.

Suppose that
T_{N}(\alpha_{i})
acts by
t_{i}id
on
S_{r}
. One has:

T_{N}(\alpha_{3})v_{j}=T_{N}\left((-iq^{-j+\frac{r-1}{2}})+(-iq^{-j+\frac{r-1}{2}})^{-1}\right)v_{j}=\left((-iq^{-j+\frac{r-1}{2}})^{N}+(-iq^{-j+\frac{r-1}{2}})^{-N}\right)v_{j}=0.

So
t_{3}=0
. Applying Lemma
5.17
to the matrices
\rho(\alpha_{1})
and
\rho(\alpha_{2})
, we obtain that
t_{1}=t_{2}=0
.
So
S_{r}
has classical shadow
([\rho_{0}],q^{r}+q^{-r})
.

∎

Proof of Theorem
5.14
.

If
[\rho]
is not central nor quaternionic, then
[\rho]\in\mathcal{FAL}
by Theorem
5.3
and Lemma
5.15
.
When
[\rho]
is central and
z\neq-2
, then
([\rho],z)
does not belong to the Azumaya locus by Theorem
5.10
.
When
[\rho]=[\rho_{0}]
is quaternionic and
r\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
then by Lemma
5.18
,
([\rho_{0}],+(q^{r}+q^{-r}))
is the classical shadow of the irreducible representation
S_{r}
of dimension
r
so it belongs to the Azumaya locus if and only if
r=N
. This concludes the proof.

∎

5.6.
Indecomposable semi-weight representations

Recall that
Z_{\Sigma}
is the center of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
, that
Z_{\Sigma}^{0}\subset Z_{\Sigma}
is the image of the Chebyshev morphism. We defined a weight module as a module which is semisimple over
Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
. For open surfaces, we rather consider the following larger class.

Definition 5.19
.

(Semi-weight modules) A module over
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
is a
semi-weight module
if it is semisimple as a module over
Z_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{0}
.

So in semi-weight modules, the peripheral curves do not necessarily acts semi-simply. For instance the representations coming from non-semisimple TQFTs (
[
BCGP16
]
) where we chose a surface with punctures colored by the projectives
P_{n}
are semi-weight modules which are not weight (because the Casimir does not act semi-simply on
P_{n}
).
Recall that we identify
\operatorname{MaxSpec}(Z_{\Sigma}^{0})
with the character variety
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
so an indecomposable semi-weight module has a classical shadow which lies in
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
. For
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
, we denote by
\mathfrak{m}_{[\rho]}\subset Z_{\Sigma}^{0}
the corresponding maximal ideal and write:

Z([\rho]):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$Z_{\Sigma}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathfrak{m}_{[\rho]}Z_{\Sigma}$}\right.},\quad\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)_{[\rho]}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathfrak{m}_{[\rho]}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)$}\right.}.

Theorem 5.20
.

(Brown-Gordon
[
BG01
, Corollary
2.7
]
) If
[\rho]
is in the fully Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
, then we have an algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)_{[\rho]}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(Z([\rho]))
.

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
. For
1\leq i\leq n
write
c_{i}:=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{i}))
and let

\mathcal{A}_{i}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{C}^{\oplus N}&\mbox{if }c_{i}\neq\pm 2;\\
\mathbb{C}\oplus\left({\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$(X)^{2}$}\right.}\right)^{\oplus(N-1)/2}&\mbox{if }c_{i}=\pm 2.\end{array}\right.

Then

Z([\rho])\cong\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{n}.

Proof.

Let us first make a simple remark. Let
P(X)\in\mathbb{C}[X]
be a polynomial with decomposition
P(X)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}(X-\lambda_{i})^{n_{i}}
where
n_{i}\geq 1
and the
\lambda_{i}
are pairwise distinct.

Claim:
We have an isomorphism of algebras:

{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(P(X))$}\right.}\cong\oplus_{i=1}^{d}{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(X^{n_{i}})$}\right.}.

Indeed, since the ideals
(X-\lambda_{i})^{n_{i}}
are pairwise coprime, by the chinese reminder theorem we have an isomorphism
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(P(X))$}\right.}\cong\oplus_{i=1}^{d}{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left((X-\lambda_{i})^{n_{i}}\right)$}\right.}
and setting
Y:=X-\lambda_{i}
one has
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(X-\lambda_{i})^{n_{i}}$}\right.}\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[Y]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(Y)^{n_{i}}$}\right.}
.

Recall from Theorem
5.3
that
{Z}_{\Sigma}\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{${Z}_{\Sigma}^{0}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(T_{N}(X_{i})-\gamma_{i})$}\right.}
from which we deduce that

Z([\rho])\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(T_{N}(X_{i})+c_{i})$}\right.}\cong\otimes_{i=1}^{n}{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(T_{N}(X)+c_{i})$}\right.}.

If
c_{i}\neq\pm 2
, then the polynomial
T_{N}(X)+c_{i}
has its zeroes of multiplicity one, so
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(T_{N}(X)+c_{i})$}\right.}\cong\mathbb{C}^{\oplus N}
. If
c_{i}=\pm 2
, then
T_{N}(X)\pm 2=(X\pm 1)\prod_{i=1}^{(N-1)/2}(X\pm(q^{i}+q^{-i}))^{2}
so
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(T_{N}(X)+c_{i})$}\right.}\cong\mathbb{C}\oplus\left({\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(X^{2})$}\right.}\right)^{\oplus(N-1)/2}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.22
.

If
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
is in the fully Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
then we have

\dim\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})_{[\rho]}\right)=N^{6g-6+3n}.

Proof.

Let
D=PI-deg(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n}))
.
By Theorem
5.20
, we have

\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})_{[\rho]}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(Z([\rho]))\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(\mathbb{C})\otimes Z([\rho]).

By Lemma
5.21
,
\dim(Z([\rho]))=N^{n}
so

\dim\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})_{[\rho]}\right)=N^{n}D^{2}=N^{n}(N^{3g-3+n})^{2}=N^{6g-6+3n}.

∎

Theorem
5.20
can be used to produce new indecomposable semi-weight
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,1})
-modules which are not weight modules whenever we can find
[\rho]
in the fully Azumaya locus such that
c:=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p}))=-2
(when
n>1
, such a
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
cannot exist in virtue of Lemmas
5.5
and
5.6
). For instance consider the case
(g,n)=(1,1)
and let
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{1,1})
be such that
c=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma_{p}))=-2
and
[\rho]\neq[\rho_{0}]
. By Theorem
5.14
,
[\rho]
is in the fully Azumaya locus so using Theorem
5.20
and Lemma
5.21
, for every
n\in\{1,\ldots,(N-1)/2\}
one has:

{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})_{[\rho]}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\gamma_{p}-q^{n}-q^{-n})^{2}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})_{[\rho]}$}\right.}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{D}\left({\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(X)^{2}$}\right.}\right).

Let
W
be the two dimensional representation of
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(X^{2})$}\right.}
with basis
\{x,y\}
such that
X\cdot x=0
and
X\cdot y=x
(so
W
is free of rank
1
). Let
V
be the
N
-dimensional simple module of
\operatorname{Mat}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
and consider the representation

\operatorname{Mat}_{N}\left({\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(X^{2})$}\right.}\right)\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{N}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\left({\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(X^{2})$}\right.}\right)\to\operatorname{End}(V)\otimes\operatorname{End}(W)\cong\operatorname{End}(V\otimes W).

We thus obtain an indecomposable representation of dimension
2N
:

r_{n}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})\to{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})_{[\rho]}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\gamma_{p}-q^{n}-q^{-n})^{2}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,1})_{[\rho]}$}\right.}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{D}\left({\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(X^{2})$}\right.}\right)\to\operatorname{End}(V\otimes W).

This representation is not a weight representation since
r_{n}(\gamma_{p})
is not semisimple and contains the Azumaya irreducible representation with classical shadow
([\rho],q^{n}+q^{-n})
as a sub-representation.

6.
Applications

6.1.
Representations of the Torelli group

Suppose
g\geq 1
.
Let
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,n})
be the mapping class group of
\Sigma_{g,n}
and let
\mathcal{I}(\Sigma_{g,n})\subset\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,n})
denote its Torelli subgroup. By definition, a mapping class belongs to
\mathcal{I}(\Sigma_{g,n})
if it acts trivially in homology. Define a left action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,n})
on
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
by
\phi\cdot[\gamma]:=[\phi(\gamma)]
for
\phi
a mapping class and
\gamma\subset\Sigma_{g}
a multicurve. The mapping class group also acts on
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g,n})
on the right by the formula
\rho\cdot\phi(\gamma)=\rho(\phi(\gamma))
for
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
\phi\in\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,n})
. Note that the composition

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\Sigma_{g,n})})\cong\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma_{g,n})\xrightarrow{Ch_{A}}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})

is equivariant for the associated left
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,n})
action.
Let
\omega\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})\setminus\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{g,n};(\frac{1}{2k}\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathbf{z}
compatible as before, and consider the associated representation
\rho_{\omega}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
as before. Since every element of
\mathcal{I}(\Sigma_{g,n})
fixes
[\rho_{\omega}]
(by definition of the Torelli group), the action of
\mathcal{I}(\Sigma_{g,n})
on
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
passes to the quotient to an action on
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})_{([\rho_{\omega}],\mathbf{z})}
. By Theorems
1.2
and
1.5
,
([\rho_{\omega}],\mathbf{z})
belongs to the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})
so
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})_{([\rho_{\omega}],\mathbf{z})}\xrightarrow[\cong]{r_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}}\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega))
is a matrix algebra and since every automorphism of a matrix algebra is inner, for every
\phi\in\mathcal{I}(\Sigma_{g,n})
, there exists an operator
\pi_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}(\phi)\in\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega))
, unique up to multiplication by a non-zero scalar, such that

\phi\cdot x=\pi_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}(\phi)^{-1}x\pi_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}(\phi),\quad\mbox{ for all }x\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,n})_{([\rho_{\omega}],\mathbf{z})}.

The assignation
\phi\mapsto\pi_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}(\phi)
defines a projective representation

\pi_{\omega,\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{I}(\Sigma_{g,n})\to\operatorname{PGL}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{P},\omega))

of the Torelli group. This representation (or rather its variant where
q
has order
2N
) first appeared in
[
BCGP16
]
where the whole structure of (non-semisimple) TQFT were used to construct it. The alternative definition we gave here, based on Theorems
1.2
and
1.5
, is much simpler.

6.2.
Reduced skein modules of
3
-manifolds

Let
M
be a compact connected
3
-manifold. As proved by Lê in
[
Le15
]
, the Chebyshev-Frobenius morphism
Ch_{A}:\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M)\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)
is still well-defined for
3
-manifolds (though not necessarily injective): it sends a framed link
L=\gamma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\gamma_{n}\subset M
to
Ch_{A}(L):=T_{N}(\gamma_{1})\cup\ldots\cup T_{N}(\gamma_{p})
. A framed link colored by
T_{N}
is
transparent
in the sense that if
L_{1},L_{2}\subset M
are two framed links, then the class
[Ch_{A}(L_{1})\cup L_{2}]\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)
does not depend on how
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are entangled. So

Ch_{A}
endows
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)
with a structure of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M)
-module via
[L_{1}]\rhd[L_{2}]:=[Ch_{A}(L_{1})\cup L_{2}]
. Fixing an identification
\Psi:\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)]\cong\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M)
as before, we thus obtain that
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)
defines a coherent sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{M}
over
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
. The section of the fiber
\mathchoice{{\mathscr{L}^{M}\,\smash{\vrule height=7.12982pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,[\rho]}}{{\mathscr{L}^{M}\,\smash{\vrule height=6.72983pt,depth=3.02223pt}}_{\,[\rho]}}{{\mathscr{L}^{M}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.1476pt,depth=2.20056pt}}_{\,[\rho]}}{{\mathscr{L}^{M}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.8889pt,depth=2.78612pt}}_{\,[\rho]}}
over a point
[\rho]
will be called the
reduced skein module
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
be a closed point which corresponds to a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{[\rho]}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M)
through
\Psi
. The
reduced skein module
is the quotient

\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$Ch_{A}(\mathfrak{m}_{[\rho]})\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)$}\right.}.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
be the class of a non-central representation and
M
a closed
3
-manifold. Then the reduced skein module
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}
is one-dimensional.

Said differently, the restriction of
\mathscr{L}^{M}
to the locus of non-central representations is a line bundle.
Theorem
6.2
was proved in
[
FKL23
, Theorem
12.2
]
under the weaker hypothesis that
[\rho]
is irreducible and mostly relied on the fact that for closed surfaces the Azumaya loci of skein algebras contain irreducible representations. Our proof is very similar to the proof of
[
FKL23
, Theorem
12.2
]
where we use Theorem
1.2
. Since the proof is short and enlightening, we repeat it for self-completeness.

The following proposition is interesting on its own and is an extension of
[
FKL23
, Theorem
12.1
]
. The inclusion
\partial M\subset M
induces a morphism
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)\to\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\partial M)
sending
[\rho]
to, say,
[\rho_{\partial}]
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
M
be a
3
-manifold homeomorphic to a thickened surface
\Sigma\times[0,1]
. Let
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
such that the restriction of
\rho_{\partial}
to every connected component of
\partial M
is non-central. Then
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}
is simple as a module over
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\partial M)_{[\rho_{\partial}]}
.

We will use the following extension of
[
FKL23
, Proposition
12.3
]
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
H_{g}
be a genus
g
handlebody and
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{g}
some free generators of
\pi_{1}(H_{g})
. Let
\rho:\pi_{1}(H_{g})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
a representation whose class
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(H_{g})
is non-central. Then there exists a homeomorphism
\phi\in\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})
which satisfies one of the following conditions:

(1)

either
\rho
is irreducible or
g=1
and
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\phi(\gamma_{i})))\neq\pm 2
for all
1\leq i\leq g
and
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\phi(\gamma_{1}\ldots\gamma_{g})))\neq\pm 2
;

(2)

or
\rho
is diagonal,
g\geq 2
and
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\phi(\gamma_{i})))\neq\pm 2
for all
1\leq i\leq g
;

(3)

or
\rho
is diagonal,
g\geq 2
and
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\phi(\gamma_{i})))\neq\pm 2
for all
2\leq i\leq g
and
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\phi(\gamma_{1}\ldots\gamma_{g})))\neq\pm 2
.

Proof.

If
g=1
, the lemma is tautological.

(1)
If
\rho:\pi_{1}(H_{g})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is irreducible, then the lemma is proved in
[
FKL23
, Proposition
12.3
]
so Assertion
(1)
holds.

(2)
Assume that
\rho
is diagonal non-central, so
\rho(\gamma_{i})=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{i}&0\\
0&\lambda_{i}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
for some
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Let
I=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,g\}\mbox{ such that }\lambda_{i}=\pm 1\}
. Since
\rho
is not central, there exists an index
i
such that
i\notin I
.
Up to permuting the indices, we can thus suppose that
1\notin I
. Recall that the map
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})\to\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{F}_{g})
sending a mapping class to the corresponding automorphism of the free group
\mathbb{F}_{g}=\pi_{1}(H_{g})=\left<\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{g}\right>
is surjective.
Let
\phi\in\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})
be a mapping class corresponding to the automorphism of
\mathbb{F}_{g}
sending
\gamma_{i}
to
\gamma_{i}\gamma_{1}
if
i\in I
and
\gamma_{j}
to
\gamma_{j}
if
j\notin I
. Then
\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\phi(\gamma_{i})))\neq\pm 2
for all
1\leq i\leq g
and we have proved Assertion
(2)
.

(3)
Eventually assume that
\rho
is diagonal non-central, so by
(2)
up to automorphism, we can suppose that
\rho(\gamma_{i})\neq\pm\mathds{1}
for
1\leq i\leq g
. If
\rho(\gamma_{1}\ldots\gamma_{g})=\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
, i.e. if
\lambda_{1}\ldots\lambda_{n}=\pm 1
, then we precompose
\rho
by the mapping class corresponding to the automorphism
\phi
of
\mathbb{F}_{g}
sending
\gamma_{1}
to
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{i}
to
\gamma_{i}
for
i\neq 1
.
Then
\psi\circ\phi
satisfies the conclusion of Assertion
(3)
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
H_{g}
be a genus
g
handlebody and
\rho:\pi_{1}(H_{g})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
a non-central representation. Then
\dim\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g})_{[\rho]}\right)=N^{3g-3}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
5.22
, it suffices to find an open surface
\Sigma_{g_{0},n}
and an homeomorphism
\phi:H_{g}\cong\Sigma_{g_{0},n}\times[0,1]
such that the class of the induced representation
\rho_{\Sigma}:=\rho\circ\phi_{*}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g_{0},n})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
lies in the fully Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g_{0},n})
. Indeed, an homeomorphism
H_{g}\cong\Sigma_{g_{0},n}\times[0,1]
exists if and only if
2g_{0}+n-1=g
in which case the conclusion of Corollary
5.22
writes:

\dim(\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g})_{[\rho]})=\dim(\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g_{0},n})_{[\rho_{\Sigma}]})=N^{6g_{0}+3n-6}=N^{3g-3}.

Note that
\rho_{\Sigma}
is non-central since
\rho
is non-central. If
g=1
, we chose
\Sigma_{g_{0},n}=\Sigma_{1}
and since, by Corollary
2.30
, any non-central representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{1})
is fully Azumaya, we have the desired equality. If
g\geq 2
and
\rho
is irreducible, we choose
\Sigma_{g_{0},n}=\Sigma_{0,g-1}
. By Lemma
6.4

(1)
, we can choose the homeomorphism
H_{g}\cong\Sigma_{0,g-1}\times[0,1]
such that
\operatorname{tr}(\rho_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{p}))\neq\pm 2
for all inner puncture
p
so
\rho_{\Sigma}
is fully Azumaya by Corollary
5.4
. If
g\geq 2
and
\rho
is diagonal non-central, we choose
\Sigma_{g_{0},n}=\Sigma_{1,g-1}
. Again, by Lemma
6.4

(3)
, we can choose the homeomorphism
H_{g}\cong\Sigma_{1,g-1}\times[0,1]
such that
\operatorname{tr}(\rho_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{p}))\neq\pm 2
for all inner puncture
p
so
\rho_{\Sigma}
is fully Azumaya by Theorem
5.12
. We conclude using the fact that in the GIT quotient
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(H_{g})
, any non-central representation
\rho
has the same class that a representation which is either diagonal or irreducible.

∎

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Without loss of generality, we can suppose that
\Sigma
is connected. Let
D_{\Sigma}
and
D_{\partial M}
the PI-degrees of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\partial M)
. By hypothesis,
[\rho_{\partial}]
is in the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\partial M)
so it suffices to prove that
\dim\left(\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}\right)=D_{\partial M}
.
The identification
M\cong\Sigma\times[0,1]
induces an isomorphism
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)\cong\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
. If
\Sigma
is closed, then
[\rho]
is in the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
so
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(\mathbb{C})
has dimension
D_{\Sigma}^{2}
. Since
\partial M=-\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma
in that case, we have
D_{\partial M}=D_{\Sigma}^{2}
as well.

Now if
\Sigma=\Sigma_{g_{0},n}
for
n\geq 1
, set
g:=2g_{0}+n-1
. Then
M\cong\Sigma_{g_{0},n}\times[0,1]\cong H_{g}
and
\partial M\cong\Sigma_{g}
so
D_{\partial M}=N^{3g-3}
.
Thus by Lemma
6.5
we have
\dim(\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]})=D_{\partial M}
.
This concludes the proof.

∎

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

Let
M
be a closed oriented
3
manifold and consider a genus
g
Heegaard splitting
M=H_{g}^{(1)}\cup_{\Sigma_{g}}H_{g}^{(2)}
where
H_{g}^{(i)}
are genus
g
handlebodies glued along some oriented homeomorphisms
\phi_{1}:\Sigma_{g}\cong-\partial H_{g}^{(1)}
and
\phi_{2}:\Sigma_{g}\cong\partial H_{g}^{(2)}
. The attaching maps endow
\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g}^{(1)})
and
\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g}^{(2)})
with some structures of right and left
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
-modules respectively and a classical argument of Hoste and Przyticki (detailed in
[
Prz99
]
) shows that the inclusion morphisms
\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g}^{(i)})\to M
induce an isomorphism

(10)

\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)\cong\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g}^{(1)})\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{A}({\Sigma}_{g})}\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g}^{(2)}).

Similarly, at the classical level, the Van Kampen theorem asserts that we have a push-out square of groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},v)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(H_{g}^{(1)},v)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(H_{g}^{(2)},v)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(M,v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{2})_{*}}

which induces an isomorphism

\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)]\cong\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(H_{g}^{(1)})]\otimes_{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})]}\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(H_{g}^{(2)})]

as proved, for instance, in
[
Mar16
, Proposition
2.10
]
. Through this isomorphism, the isomorphism of (
10
) becomes an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)]
-modules.
Let
[\rho_{i}]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(H_{g}^{(i)})
and
[\rho_{\Sigma}]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma_{g})
the classes of the representations obtained by precomposing
\rho
with the group morphisms
\pi_{1}(H_{g}^{(i)},v)\to\pi_{1}(M,v)
and
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},v)\to\pi_{1}(M,v)
. Since
[\rho]
is non-central, so are
[\rho_{\Sigma}]
and
[\rho_{i}]
. By Theorem
1.2
,
[\rho]
is in the Azumaya locus of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})
. Therefore
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})_{[\rho_{\Sigma}]}\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g}^{(i)})_{[\rho_{i}]}
is isomorphic to the standard simple (left or right) module of
\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(\mathbb{C})
by Proposition
6.3
. Therefore
one obtains isomorphisms:

\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}\cong\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g}^{(1)})_{[\rho_{1}]}\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g})_{[\rho_{\Sigma}]}}\mathcal{S}_{A}(H_{g}^{(2)})_{[\rho_{2}]}\cong\mathbb{C}^{D}\otimes_{\operatorname{Mat}_{D}(\mathbb{C})}\mathbb{C}^{D}\cong\mathbb{C}.

This concludes the proof.

∎

6.3.
Skein modules reduced at central representations

Let
\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)
be the skein module over the ring
K:=\mathbb{Q}[v^{\pm 1}]
, so here
v
is a generic formal parameter and
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)=\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}_{A}
(here
\mathbb{C}_{A}
is
\mathbb{C}
with the
K
-module structure obtained by replacing
v
by
A
). Following
[
DKS23
]
, we say that the skein module
\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)
is
A
-
tame
if is a direct sum of a cyclic
K
-modules which does not contain any summand of the form
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$K$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\phi_{N}(v))$}\right.}
if
\varepsilon=+1
or of the form
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$K$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\phi_{2N}(v))$}\right.}
if
\varepsilon=-1
. Here
\phi_{N}(X)
is the cyclotomic polynomial and the summands we exclude are the ones which do not vanish once tensored with
\mathbb{C}_{A}
. Adapting the arguments in
[
DKS23
]
to our purpose, we prove the following

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
M
be a closed
3
-manifold such that
(1)
the character variety
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
consists in a finite number of points and is reduced and
(2)
the skein module
\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)
is
A
-tame. Then for every central representation
[\rho_{0}]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
then
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}
is one-dimensional. So
\mathscr{L}^{M}\to\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
is a line bundle.

The following is a straightforward adaptation of
[
DKS23
, Lemma
2.6
]
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
M
be a closed
3
-manifold such that the character variety
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
has a finite number of points. Then the linear morphism

\Phi:\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)\to\oplus_{[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)}\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]},

obtained by taking the direct sum of the quotient maps, is surjective.

Proof.

For each
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
, let
f_{[\rho]}\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M)
be skein element corresponding under
\Psi
to the regular function
g_{[\rho]}=\Psi(f_{[\rho]})
defined by
g_{[\rho]}([\rho^{\prime}]):=\delta_{[\rho],[\rho^{\prime}]}
.
Let
(w_{1,[\rho]},\ldots,w_{d_{[\rho]},[\rho]})
be a basis of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}
, so
(w_{i,[\rho]})_{i,[\rho]}
is a basis of
\oplus_{[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)}\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}
. Since the quotient map
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]}
is surjective, we can find
v_{i,[\rho]}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)
which is sent to
w_{i,[\rho]}
by the quotient map. Then the set
\{Ch_{A}(f_{[\rho]})v_{i,[\rho]}\}\subset\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)
is sent to the basis
(w_{i,[\rho]})_{i,[\rho]}
by
\Psi
, so
\Psi
is surjective.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.6
.

By Theorem
6.2
,
\dim(\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]})=1
whenever
[\rho]
is not central. By Lemma
5.5
, when
[\rho]
is central then
d:=\dim(\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho]})
is the same for all central representations. It follows from the non-trivialness of the Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev invariants, proved in
[
DKS23
, Proposition
2.4
]
, that
d\geq 1
.
By Lemma
6.7
we have
\dim(\mathcal{S}_{A}(M))\geq|\mathcal{X}^{(0)}\cup\mathcal{X}^{(1)}|+d|\mathcal{X}^{(2)}|
so it suffices to prove that
\dim(\mathcal{S}_{A}(M))\leq|\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)|
to conclude that
d=1
. We argue like in the proof of
[
DKS23
, Theorem
3.1
]
. By the
A
-tameness assumption, we have

\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)\cong F\oplus\oplus_{i=1}^{n}{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{Q}[v^{\pm 1}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(P_{i}(v))$}\right.}

where
F
is a free
K
-module and the cyclotomic polynomial
\phi_{N}(v)
(resp.
\phi_{2N}(v)
) does not divide
P_{i}(v)
if
\varepsilon=+1
(resp. if
\varepsilon=-1
). So, while tensoring with
\mathbb{C}_{A}
all torsion modules vanishes and
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)\cong\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}_{A}\cong F\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}
has dimension
\dim_{K}(F)
. On the other hand, we have

\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)]\cong\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M)\cong\mathcal{S}_{v}(M)\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon}\cong(F\otimes_{K}\mathbb{C})\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n}

where
n
is the number of elements with torsion
(v-\varepsilon 1)^{n}
. Since we assumed that
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
is reduced (i.e. that its nilradical is trivial) then
\dim(\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)])=|\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)|
, so

|\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)|=\dim(\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(M))\geq\dim_{K}(F)=\dim(\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)).

This implies that
\dim(\mathcal{S}_{A}(M))=|\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)|
so
d=1
. This concludes the proof.
∎

6.4.
Concluding remarks

Let
M
be a closed oriented
3
manifold. The coherent sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{M}\to\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
is expected to be a line bundle over the whole character variety.
The Costantino-Geer-Patureau Mirand invariants of
[
CGP14
]
form a section
\sigma^{CGP}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{X}^{(1)},\mathcal{L}^{*})
of the restriction of the dual of
\mathcal{L}
to the locus of diagonal non-central representations (which depends on choice of signature for a bounding
4
-manifold
W
with
\partial W=M
).
The fiber of
\sigma^{CGP}
over
[\rho_{\omega}]
is the linear map
\mathcal{S}_{A}(M)_{[\rho_{\omega}]}\to\mathbb{C}
sending
[\gamma]
to the CGP invariant
\left<M,[\gamma],\omega\right>
. Similarly, the Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev invariants form a section of the dual of
\mathscr{L}^{M}
over the locus of central representations.
An important problem is to extend these sections to the whole character variety
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(M)
, that is to define invariants
\left<M,[\gamma],[\rho]\right>
for
[\rho]\in\mathcal{X}^{(2)}
. It is conjectured that such invariants fit into a
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
-HQFT which would refine the work of Baseilhac-Benedetti
[
BB07
]
and generalize the non-semisimple TQFTs of
[
BCGP16
]
. The link invariants of this conjectural HQFT were defined independently in
[
BGPR18
]
and
[
Kor19a
]
. The construction of these conjectural HQFTs is the main motivations for the authors to consider Problem
1.1
and for the present paper.

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
3.11

First, the facts that
w_{\alpha}
is an equivariant isomorphism and the identities of Equation (
4
) follow from easy computations left to the reader. To prove the other identities, we will adapt the work of Costantino and Murakami in
[
CM13
]
to our context. The non-triviality of this adaptation comes from the facts that

(1)

we work with
q
a root of unity of order
N
, whereas it has order
2N
in
[
CM13
]
,

(2)

we work with the unrolled quantum group with the additional generator
H
which is not considered in
[
CM13
]
,

(3)

we followed the conventions of
[
CGP14
,
CGPM15
,
BCGP16
,
BCGP15
]
which are completely different from the ones in
[
CM13
]
.

To remedy the third problem, let us first establish a dictionary between our notations and the ones in
[
CM13
]
. Let
\mathbf{U}
be the quantum group considered in
[
CM13
]
and denote by
\mathbf{E}
,
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{K}^{\pm 1}
its generators. By definition, they satisfy the relations

\mathbf{K}\mathbf{E}=q\mathbf{E}\mathbf{K},\quad\mathbf{K}\mathbf{F}=q^{-1}\mathbf{F}\mathbf{K},\quad[\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F}]=\frac{\mathbf{K}^{2}-\mathbf{K}^{-2}}{q-q^{-1}}

with the coproduct

\Delta(\mathbf{K})=\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{K},\quad\Delta(\mathbf{E})=\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{K}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{E},\quad\Delta(\mathbf{F})=\mathbf{F}\otimes\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{K}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{F}.

Therefore, we establish a morphism between the quantum group
\mathbf{U}
of
[
CM13
]
and our quantum group
U^{H}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
by sending
\mathbf{K}
to
K^{1/2}
,
\mathbf{E}
to
K^{-1/2}E
and
\mathbf{F}
to
FK^{1/2}
, where
K^{1/2}
is the element acting as
q^{H/2}
. The authors consider highest weight
\mathbf{U}
-modules
V^{a}
with the basis
(e_{i}^{a})_{i=0}^{N-1}
defined by

\mathbf{E}e_{i}^{a}=[i]e_{i-1}^{a},\quad\mathbf{F}e_{i}^{a}=[2a-i]e_{i+1}^{a},\quad\mathbf{K}e_{i}^{a}=q^{a-i}e_{i}^{a}\quad(e_{-1}^{a}=e_{N}^{a}=0).

Setting
2a=\alpha+N-1
, the
\mathbf{U}
-module
V^{a}
is isomorphic to the
U_{q}^{H}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-module
V_{\alpha}
via the isomorphism identifying
v_{i}
with
\kappa_{i}^{\alpha}e_{i}^{a}
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
and consider
a,b,c
such that
2a=\alpha+N-1
,
2b=\beta+N-1
and
2c=\gamma+N-1
. The identity
\alpha+\beta-\gamma\in H_{N}
corresponds to the identity
a+b-c\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
and the authors consider
Y_{c}^{a,b}:V^{c}\to V^{a}\otimes V^{b}
defined by
Y_{c}^{a,b}(e_{n}^{c})=\sum_{i,j}\sqrt{-1}^{-c+a+b}C_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}e_{i}^{a}\otimes e_{j}^{b}
which, under the above isomorphism corresponds to our operator
Y_{\gamma}^{\alpha,\beta}
by definition up to the multiplication by the factor
\sqrt{-1}^{-c+a+b}
. Its absence in our formulas will be explained below.
Similarly, under the same correspondence, our operators
Y_{\alpha,\beta}^{\gamma}
,
\cap_{\alpha}
and
\cup_{\alpha}
correspond to the operators
Y_{a,b}^{c}
,
\cap_{a,N-1-a}
and
\cup_{a,N-1-a}
in
[
CM13
]
(up to a factor
\sqrt{-1}^{-c+a+b}
in
Y_{a,b}^{c}
). To prove Proposition
3.11
, we need to prove that

(i)

Y_{\gamma}^{\alpha,\beta}
is equivariant by verifying that the computations in Appendix
A.1
of
[
CM13
]
still hold when
q
has order
N
;

(ii)

Equation (
5
) holds by verifying that the computations in Appendix
A.2
of
[
CM13
]
still hold when
q
has order
N
;

(iii)

Equation (
6
) holds by verifying that the computations in Appendix
A.3
of
[
CM13
]
still hold when
q
has order
N
: this is where the fact that we removed the factor
\sqrt{-1}^{-i-j+n}
from the formula of
C_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
is important.

(i)
First, it is a trivial verification (left to the reader) that the computations done in
[
CM13
, Appendix
A.1
]
hold word-by-word in the case where
q
has order
N
instead of
2N
.

(ii)
The fact that the equality of
[
CM13
, Lemma
1.8
]
still holds when
q
has order
N
is a non-trivial fact; let us detail the computation. Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
and
a,b,c
as before and write
m:=a+b-c
. The operators
V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}\to V_{\gamma}
of the middle and right hand side of Equation (
5
) both send
v_{0}\otimes v_{m}
to a vector proportional to
v_{0}
. Equating the two proportionality factors reduces to prove that

C_{N-1,0,m}^{-\alpha,\gamma,\beta}q^{-a(N-1)}=C_{0,N-1-m,0}^{\gamma,-\beta,\alpha}q^{b(N-1)+m}.

Denote by LHS (resp. RHS) the left-hand-side (resp. right-hand-side) of this equality. Developing the formulas in Notations
3.9
, one finds that

LHS=\left(q^{(N-1)a}q^{\frac{m}{2}(2b-m+(N-1))}(-1)^{m}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}N-1-2a+m\\
N-1-2a\end{array}\right]\right)q^{-a(N-1)}=(-1)^{m}q^{bm-\frac{m(m+1)}{2}}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}N-1-2a+m\\
N-1-2a\end{array}\right].

Similarly

RHS=\left(q^{\frac{(N-1-m)(-2b-(N-1)-(N-1-m))}{2}}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}2a\\
2a-m\end{array}\right]\right)q^{b(N-1)+m}=q^{bm-\frac{m(m+1)}{2}}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}2a\\
2a-m\end{array}\right].

And we derived the equality
LHS=RHS
from the equality

\left[\begin{array}[]{l}N-1-2a+m\\
N-1-2a\end{array}\right]=(-1)^{m}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}2a\\
2a-m\end{array}\right].

(iii)
Again, that the computations of
[
CM13
, Appendix
A.3
]
still hold for
q
of order
N
is non-trivial and must be checked by a careful analysis. The only difference with the case where
q
has order
2N
are that:

(1)

while passing from the last line of page
26
of
[
CM13
, Appendix
A.3
]
to the first line of page
27
, the authors used the identity
\left[\begin{array}[]{l}N-1-y\\
N-1-x\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{l}x\\
y\end{array}\right]
three times. When
q
has order
N
, this identity is replaced by
\left[\begin{array}[]{l}N-1-y\\
N-1-x\end{array}\right]=(-1)^{x-y}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}x\\
y\end{array}\right]
. It results that the identity is multiplied by three new factors
(-1)^{v}
,
(-1)^{m}
and
(-1)^{m+t}
where
m=a+b-c
as before and
v+t=N-1-m
, so the formula in the first line of page
27
gets multiplied by a factor
(-1)^{m}
.

(2)

In the last equality of the proof of Lemma
1.10
in
[
CM13
, Appendix
A.3
]
, the authors used a formula of the form
\left[\begin{array}[]{l}x\\
y\end{array}\right]=(-1)^{x-y}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}x-N\\
y-N\end{array}\right]
which becomes
\left[\begin{array}[]{l}x\\
y\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{l}x-N\\
y-N\end{array}\right]
when
q
has order
N
. It results that the identity gets multiplied by an additional factor
(-1)^{m}
which compensates the previous one.

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
3.17

Recall that
6S(\alpha,\beta,\gamma;\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})
is the scalar defined by the skein relation

Without loss of generality, we can suppose that
\alpha+\beta-\gamma\in H_{N}
.
Set
m:=\frac{\alpha+\beta-\gamma}{2}+\frac{N-1}{2}\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
. By evaluating each operator of the above equality in the vector
v_{0}
, we obtain a vector proportional to
v_{0}\otimes v_{m}
. Equating both proportionality scalars we obtain the equality

D_{0,m,0}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}6S(\alpha,\beta,\gamma;\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=\sum_{a,b=0,\ldots,N-1}\sum_{n=0,1}D_{a,b,0}^{\alpha+\varepsilon_{1},\beta+\varepsilon_{2},\gamma}D_{0,N-2+n,a}^{\alpha,k(N-2),\alpha+\varepsilon_{1}k}E_{N-2+n,b,m}^{(N-2),\beta+\varepsilon_{2},\beta}.

Set
\theta_{1}:=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}-1}{2}
and
\theta_{2}:=m+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}+1}{2}
.
The summand in the right hand side of the equality vanishes if
(a,b)\neq(\theta_{1}+n,\theta_{2}-n)
.
Indeed, by definition, both
C_{i,j,k}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
and
E_{i,j,k}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
vanish if
i+j-k\neq\frac{\alpha+\beta-\gamma+(N-1)}{2}
. We deduce the implications
D_{a,b,0}^{\alpha+\varepsilon_{1},\beta+\varepsilon_{2},\gamma}\neq 0\Rightarrow a+b=m+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}{2}
and
E_{N-2+n,b,m}^{N-2,\beta+\varepsilon_{2},\beta}\neq 0\Rightarrow b=\theta_{2}-n
; so both inequalities can happen only if
(a,b)=(\theta_{1}+n,\theta_{2}-n)
.

Therefore one has

(11)

\left(D_{0,m,0}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\right)6S(\alpha,\beta,\gamma;\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=D_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},0}^{\alpha+\varepsilon_{1},\beta+\varepsilon_{2},\gamma}D_{0,N-2,\theta_{1}}^{\alpha,N-2,\alpha+\varepsilon_{1}}E_{N-2,\theta_{2},m}^{N-2,\beta+\varepsilon_{2},\beta}\\
+D_{\theta_{1}+1,\theta_{2}-1,0}^{\alpha+\varepsilon_{1},\beta+\varepsilon_{2},\gamma}D_{0,N-1,\theta_{1}+1}^{\alpha,N-2,\alpha+\varepsilon_{1}}E_{N-1,\theta_{2}-1,m}^{N-2,\beta+\varepsilon_{2},\beta}.

Write
K:=\mathbb{Q}(A)(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})
and let us define a rational function
\mathbb{D}_{i,j,n}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})\in K
such that
D_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}=\mathbb{D}_{i,j,n}(q^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},q^{\frac{\beta}{2}},q^{\frac{\gamma}{2}})
. Set

{\mathds{k}}_{n}(X):=X^{-1}q^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}(X^{2}q^{-1}-X^{-2}q)(X^{2}q^{-2}-X^{-2}q^{2})\ldots(X^{2}q^{-n}-X^{-2}q^{n})\in\mathbb{Q}(A)(X)

so that
\kappa^{\alpha}_{n}=\mathds{k}_{n}(q^{\frac{\alpha}{2}})
. Also set

f_{a,b}(X):=\frac{1}{\{b\}!}(X^{2}q^{a-1}-X^{-2}q^{1-a})(X^{2}q^{a-2}-X^{-2}q^{2-a})\ldots(X^{2}q^{a-b}-X^{-2}q^{b-a})\in\mathbb{Q}(A)(X)

so that
\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\alpha+N-1+a\\
\alpha+N-1+a-b\end{array}\right]=f_{a,b}(q^{\frac{\alpha}{2}})
. Writing
m:=\frac{\alpha+\beta-\gamma}{2}+\frac{N-1}{2}
as before, we set

\mathbb{C}_{i,j,n}:=(-1)^{j-n}q^{\frac{i-j}{2}}X_{1}^{-i}X_{2}^{j}(f_{0,n}(X_{3}))^{-1}f_{0,m}(X_{3})\\
\sum_{z+w=n}(-1)^{z}q^{-\frac{n}{2}(2z-n)}X_{3}^{2z-n}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}i+j-n\\
i-z\end{array}\right]f_{u+z,z}(X_{1})f_{w-j,w}(X_{2}).

so that
C_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}=\mathbb{C}_{i,j,n}(q^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},q^{\frac{\beta}{2}},q^{\frac{\gamma}{2}})
. Set

\mathbb{D}_{i,j,n}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}):=\frac{\mathds{k}_{n}(X_{3})}{\mathds{k}_{i}(X_{1})\mathds{k}_{j}(X_{2})}\mathbb{C}_{i,j,n}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})

and

\mathbb{E}_{i,j,n}:=\frac{\mathds{k}_{i}(X_{1})\mathds{k}_{j}(X_{2})}{\mathds{k}_{n}(X_{3})}\mathbb{C}_{N-1-j,N-1-i,N-1-n}(X_{2}^{-1},X_{1}^{-1},X_{3}^{-1})

so that
D_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}=\mathbb{D}_{i,j,n}(q^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},q^{\frac{\beta}{2}},q^{\frac{\gamma}{2}})
and
E_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}=\mathbb{E}_{i,j,n}(q^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},q^{\frac{\beta}{2}},q^{\frac{\gamma}{2}})
. Eventually set

\mathbb{F}_{1}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}):=\mathbb{D}_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},0}(X_{1}A^{\varepsilon_{1}},X_{2}A^{\varepsilon_{2}},X_{3})\mathbb{D}_{0,N-2,\theta_{1}}(X_{1},q^{-1},X_{1}A^{\varepsilon_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{N-2,\theta_{2},m}(q^{-1},X_{2}A^{\varepsilon_{2}},X_{2})

and

\mathbb{F}_{2}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}):=\mathbb{D}_{\theta_{1}+1,\theta_{2}-1,0}(X_{1}A^{\varepsilon_{1}},X_{2}A^{\varepsilon_{2}},X_{3})\mathbb{D}_{0,N-1,\theta_{1}+1}(X_{1},q^{-1},X_{1}A^{\varepsilon_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{N-1,\theta_{2}-1,m}(q^{-1},X_{2}A^{\varepsilon_{2}},X_{2}).

Definition B.1
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
such that
\alpha+\beta-\gamma\in H_{N}
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}=\pm 1
. The rational function
R\in K
is

R(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}):=\left(\mathbb{D}_{0,m,0}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbb{F}_{1}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})+\mathbb{F}_{2}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})\right).

It follows from Equation (
11
) that

6S(\alpha,\beta,\gamma;\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})=R(q^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},q^{\frac{\beta}{2}},q^{\frac{\gamma}{2}})
, so it remains to prove that
R\neq 0
. Note that, since
R_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\neq 0
and
E_{i,j,n}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\neq 0
, then
\mathbb{D}_{i,j,n}\neq 0
and
\mathbb{E}_{i,j,n}\neq 0
so
\mathbb{F}_{1}\neq 0
and
\mathbb{F}_{2}\neq 0
. We need to show that
\mathbb{F}_{1}\neq-\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

For
L
a field, define a discrete valuation
v:L(X)\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
by setting
v(0)=\infty
and for
f(X)=X^{n}\frac{P(X)}{Q(X)}
with
P(0)Q(0)\neq 0
, set
v(f):=n
. For instance, when
L=\mathbb{Q}(A)
, one has:

v(\mathds{k}_{i}(X))=-1-2i\quad\mbox{ and }\quad v(f_{a,b}(X))=-2b.

Setting
L=\mathbb{Q}(A)(X_{2},X_{3})
, and by precomposing with the natural embedding
K\hookrightarrow L(X_{1})
, we get a discrete valuation
v
on
K
(for instance
v(X_{1}^{a}X_{2}^{b}X_{3}^{c})=a
). Using the facts that
v(fg)=v(f)+v(g)
and that
v(f+g)\geq\min(v(f),v(g))
with equality when
v(f)\neq v(g)
, we easily compute the following

v\left(\mathbb{D}_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},0}(X_{1}A^{\varepsilon_{1}},X_{2}A^{\varepsilon_{2}},X_{3})\right)=2\theta_{1},\quad v\left(\mathbb{D}_{0,N-2,\theta_{1}}(X_{1},q^{-1},X_{1}A^{\varepsilon_{1}})\right)=5\theta_{1}-2N+5,

so

v(\mathbb{F}_{1})=7\theta_{1}-2N+5.

Similarly

v\left(\mathbb{D}_{\theta_{1}+1,\theta_{2}-1,0}(X_{1}A^{\varepsilon_{1}},X_{2}A^{\varepsilon_{2}},X_{3})\right)=2\theta_{1}+2,\quad v\left(\mathbb{D}_{0,N-1,\theta_{1}+1}(X_{1},q^{-1},X_{1}A^{\varepsilon_{1}})\right)=5\theta_{1}-2N+8,

so

v(\mathbb{F}_{2})=7\theta_{1}-2N+10.

Therefore
v(\mathbb{F}_{1})\neq v(\mathbb{F}_{2})
so
\mathbb{F}_{1}+\mathbb{F}_{2}\neq 0
. This implies that
R\neq 0
and concludes the proof.
